# Decisions

Architecture and implementation decisions for System 3 (search agent, API, UI). Append-only table, most recent at the bottom.

This repo was created from `agentic-search-data-engineering` (System 1+2) as a structural template. System 1+2 decision history lives in that repo.

## Index by theme

An index rather than a table of contents, because the body below is a single append-only table with no headings to link to. Each theme names the dates a reader should scan for.

- Data access and the knowledge graph: the read-only AGE connection, how Cypher is generated and bounded, the schema slice handed to the model, and the query-planner fix that cut a 42 second lookup to 108ms. 2026-05-05, 2026-05-07, 2026-07-21, 2026-07-25, 2026-07-29, 2026-08-07, 2026-08-09.
- The seven tools and the external APIs: the adapter pattern, the roster growing from five tools to seven, per-API rate limits and timeouts, and the live-probing that repeatedly found the documented behaviour and the real behaviour disagreeing. 2026-05-05, 2026-05-07, 2026-07-22, 2026-07-25, 2026-08-05, 2026-08-07, 2026-08-08, 2026-08-10.
- The agent loop and the model harness: the five-step loop, the three capability tiers, cost caps and per-step budgets, reasoning effort, and prompt-cache discipline. 2026-05-05, 2026-05-07, 2026-07-21, 2026-07-25, 2026-07-28, 2026-07-29, 2026-07-31, 2026-08-02 through 2026-08-05, 2026-08-09.
- Trust, citations and refusal: cite-or-refuse, the provenance fields, the two-tier risk gate, freshness and conflict resolution, and the repeated question of what silence from a check should mean. The single most consequential theme in the file. 2026-05-07, 2026-07-21, 2026-07-22, 2026-07-25, 2026-08-03, 2026-08-04, 2026-08-09, 2026-08-10, 2026-08-14.
- The delivery surfaces: REST plus SSE, the MCP server, GraphQL, the CLI, KGX export, and the web UI's own wiring and auth. 2026-05-05, 2026-05-07, 2026-07-25, 2026-07-28, 2026-08-10, 2026-08-11, 2026-08-15.
- Design and the design system: the prototype-first design loop, MUI adoption, the provenance spine, and the contrast and focus-nesting defects found in the design itself. 2026-07-21, 2026-08-11 through 2026-08-14.
- Evaluation, the rubric and the moat test: the 8-point rubric and its threshold, the hard-fail conditions, how competency questions are selected, and the online feedback loop. 2026-07-22.
- The v1 scope boundary: what is deliberately excluded, most often the compute tools (BLAST, sequence similarity, VCF) and the seven must-pass questions that stand in for them. 2026-07-22.
- Process, cadence and tooling: the largest theme, and the one that grows fastest. Branch and commit conventions, the premise-gate-first stage, the judge and adversary rounds, the one-owner-per-fact rule and the drift check that enforces it, security-scan deferrals, and the build order's exceptions. Spread throughout, 2026-05-05 to 2026-08-15.

## Table

Each row's reasoning sits behind a "why" dropdown, so the table scans as one line per decision and you open only the row you care about. The date, the decision and the alternatives stay visible.

One tradeoff, stated rather than hidden: a collapsed block is invisible to the browser's find-in-page. Searching for a word that appears only in a "why" will not find it. Use the index above to narrow by theme, or expand the rows in a section before searching them.


| Date | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why |
|------|----------|------------------------|-----|
| 2026-05-05 | PostgreSQL + Apache AGE knowledge graph accessed read-only via psycopg2; no graph write operations in this repo | Embed graph queries directly in the agent; use a GraphQL layer over AGE; use Neo4j client | <details><summary>why</summary>AGE graph already exists on Hetzner CPX42 (115M nodes, 693M edges). psycopg2 is the simplest client with no additional infrastructure. Read-only enforced at connection level (kg_reader role).</details> |
| 2026-05-05 | Multi-model harness with 3 tiers (guard/plan/synth) via LiteLLM | Single model for everything; two tiers (cheap/expensive); direct Anthropic/OpenAI SDK calls | <details><summary>why</summary>Guard tier keeps input validation cheap (~$0.001/query). Plan tier balances cost/capability for tool selection. Synth tier uses the strongest model only for final answer generation. LiteLLM abstracts provider differences. Hard cost caps at every tier prevent runaway spend.</details> |
| 2026-05-05 | LangGraph for agent orchestration (not raw LangChain or custom state machine) | LangChain AgentExecutor; custom async state machine; CrewAI; AutoGen | <details><summary>why</summary>LangGraph gives typed state, conditional edges, and built-in persistence without the abstraction overhead of AgentExecutor. Supports streaming natively. The 5-step agent loop maps directly to a LangGraph graph with conditional routing.</details> |
| 2026-05-05 | FastAPI + SSE streaming (not WebSocket) for chat responses | WebSocket; HTTP polling; gRPC streaming | <details><summary>why</summary>SSE is simpler than WebSocket for unidirectional server-to-client streaming. Works through CDNs and proxies without upgrade negotiation. sse-starlette integrates with FastAPI's async generators. Client reconnection is built into the EventSource spec.</details> |
| 2026-05-05 | Data source adapter pattern: each source implements only applicable adapters (Query, Facet, Citation, Relationship, Streaming) | Single monolithic interface with NotImplementedError; separate class per operation; duck typing | <details><summary>why</summary>Adapters are capability declarations. The agent checks adapter availability before attempting operations. No "not implemented" exceptions, no silent no-ops. Each source is honest about what it can do.</details> |
| 2026-05-05 | Production-level SDLC standards from day 1 via 6-lens dev-standards skill (adapted from NWS enterprise standards) | Add standards later when closer to production; use a lighter checklist; no formal review process | <details><summary>why</summary>Technical debt compounds exponentially. Patterns established in week 1 become the baseline forever. The 6-lens approach (security, testing, quality, PR readiness, deployment, hardening) catches issues before they become architectural problems. Cost of applying standards early is near zero; cost of retrofitting is high.</details> |
| 2026-05-05 | Cypher queries must always specify edge labels explicitly (never untyped `[r]`) | Allow untyped edges and rely on planner pruning; force all queries through a builder abstraction | <details><summary>why</summary>AGE compiles untyped edges to UNION ALL over all 14 edge label tables (693M rows). Specifying the edge label lets the planner touch only the relevant table. This convention alone takes queries from minutes to milliseconds. Learned from Gate 3 indexing work in System 1+2.</details> |
| 2026-05-07 | Innovation proposal is the starting ground for V1: all scope elements and requirements apply | Treat the proposal as aspirational and cherry-pick for V1 | <details><summary>why</summary>The innovation proposal defines the minimum viable product. Even though V1 is personally funded, it must demonstrate the full system to serve as evidence for the NCBI track.</details> |
| 2026-05-07 | All 5 delivery formats in scope for V1: web UI, GraphQL API, MCP server, KGX export (System 1/2 graph only), CLI agent | Web UI only for V1, add others later | <details><summary>why</summary>The proposal specifies 5 formats. KGX export is scoped to the existing Hetzner graph (not a full Neo4j export). All formats are part of demonstrating the system works end to end.</details> |
| 2026-05-07 | FastAPI over Django for V1 backend | Django (NCBI standard stack) | <details><summary>why</summary>Agent loop requires native async for concurrent API calls, LLM streaming, and LangGraph integration. Django's async support is bolted on, and its ORM adds no value (we use raw Cypher, not SQL models). Core agent code is framework-agnostic, so porting to Django for NCBI Track 2 is bounded work (HTTP layer only, not the agent logic).</details> |
| 2026-05-07 | Hybrid API surface: REST + SSE for chat streaming, GraphQL (via Strawberry) for structured programmatic access | GraphQL only; REST only | <details><summary>why</summary>Chat is a conversation (stateful, streaming) and fits REST+SSE naturally. Programmatic access to nested biomedical data (genes, variants, diseases, papers) fits GraphQL. Both served from the same FastAPI app, sharing auth middleware and underlying tools.</details> |
| 2026-05-07 | NCBI strategic directives (FY26 guiding principles, Gold Standard Science, AI Action Plan) are real requirements for V1, not aspirational alignment | Treat as nice-to-have positioning | <details><summary>why</summary>These define how the system must behave: simplify discovery (FY26), reproducibility and transparency (Gold Standard), AI-ready datasets (AI Action Plan). V1 is personally funded but must meet these standards to serve as evidence for the NCBI track.</details> |
| 2026-05-07 | Open-source LLMs preferred for the running system; commercial models reserved for development debugging only | Commercial models (Anthropic, OpenAI) for all tiers; single model for everything | <details><summary>why</summary>Harness engineering philosophy: a strong harness (guardrails, structured tool calls, citation assembly) reduces the capability required from each model. Guard tier is classification (tiny model). Plan tier is structured output (mid-range). Synth tier assembles pre-fetched cited data, not open-ended reasoning. Open-source keeps the running system cost at single digits per month.</details> |
| 2026-05-07 | OpenRouter as the inference provider, accessed via LiteLLM in application code | Groq free tier; direct provider API keys (Groq + Together + Fireworks); OpenRouter without LiteLLM; LiteLLM with direct provider keys | <details><summary>why</summary>OpenRouter provides a unified API gateway to 100+ models (one API key, one billing). LiteLLM stays as the in-code SDK abstraction (typed calls, cost tracking, retry logic, LangGraph integration). Each layer does what it's good at: LiteLLM controls which model gets called per tier, OpenRouter handles provider routing and fallbacks. Avoids managing multiple provider accounts. Pricing for open-source models is comparable to direct access.</details> |
| 2026-05-07 | V1 budget target: ~$100 total for 2-month build (not counting Claude Code Max subscription) | Original $300-500 estimate | <details><summary>why</summary>Hetzner CPX42 ~$60 (already running). Railway free (1-year plan). LangSmith free tier. LLM inference via OpenRouter on open-source models: single digits per month. Claude Code Max ($100/mo) is a personal development tool, not a project cost.</details> |
| 2026-05-07 | Single orchestrator agent with 3-tier model routing for V1; sub-query decomposition as the upgrade path for complex queries | Multiple independent agents; orchestrator + domain worker agents; single model for everything | <details><summary>why</summary>V1 competency questions need 3-5 tool calls per query. One agent handles this trivially. Multi-agent adds inter-agent communication, handoff protocol, and error handling across agent boundaries for no benefit at this scale. The harness provides multi-model routing (different model per step) without multi-agent complexity. Sub-query decomposition (LangGraph subgraph, not a separate agent) is the planned upgrade for deep research queries needing 10+ sequential tool calls. Trigger: >20% failure rate on deep research class queries in the golden dataset.</details> |
| 2026-05-07 | Model selection deferred to build phase (Phase 6); harness pattern locked now, specific models selected via golden dataset ablation | Lock specific models now | <details><summary>why</summary>The harness pattern (3 tiers, LiteLLM + OpenRouter) is the decision. Specific models (DeepSeek R1, Kimi K2, Gemma 4, Llama 4, Qwen3) are candidates to be benchmarked against the golden dataset during build. Open-source Chinese models (DeepSeek, Kimi K2, Qwen) are competitive on structured tasks and preferred for cost. Locking models before measuring performance wastes the harness's main advantage: swapping models is a config change.</details> |
| 2026-05-07 | Streaming with typed SSE events (status, tool_result, token, citation, done) and hard latency budgets per query class | Non-streaming request/response; WebSocket streaming | <details><summary>why</summary>Users must see agent progress from T+0.1s, never a blank screen. SSE event types give the UI structured data to render progress (status updates, citation chips, streaming tokens). Hard latency budgets enforced by the harness: lookup 5s, single-hop 10s, multi-hop 30s, deep research 2min. If exceeded, synthesize with partial results and explain what timed out. Already decided SSE over WebSocket in Step 1.1; this decision adds the event type schema and latency enforcement.</details> |
| 2026-05-07 | Three-layer data architecture confirmed: Layer 1 (AGE graph, <10ms), Layer 2 (NCBI APIs, 100-500ms), Layer 3 (enrichment APIs, 200ms-2s) | Ingest all databases into graph; API-only with no graph; two layers | <details><summary>why</summary>The architecture doc and three-layer doc agree. 5 databases in Layer 1 (Gene, PubMed, ClinVar, MedGen, Taxonomy) provide the graph skeleton for fast traversals. Layer 2 (dbSNP, Protein, PMC, OMIM, GTR, GEO, dbVar, Assembly) are leaf nodes reached via live API at query time. Layer 3 (PubTator3, LitVar2, LitSense, ClinicalTrials.gov) augments answers with enrichment. This is already built and running on Hetzner.</details> |
| 2026-05-07 | Parallel tool execution via asyncio.gather for independent API calls | Sequential tool calls | <details><summary>why</summary>Three API calls at 300ms each: 300ms total (parallel) vs 900ms (sequential). Independence between calls is determined by the planner output. The LangGraph agent loop dispatches independent tool calls in parallel by default.</details> |
| 2026-05-07 | Result compression in the harness before re-injecting tool results into agent context | Pass full tool results into context | <details><summary>why</summary>A Cypher query returning 500 rows would consume the context window and cause hallucination of remaining data. The harness summarizes results (e.g. "14 genes found: HNF1A, HNF4A, GCK... Top pathogenic variants: 47 across 8 genes") before the agent sees them. Keeps context small regardless of result volume. Already specified in system-design-patterns rule (output truncation for large results).</details> |
| 2026-05-07 | NCBI API rate limit upgraded from 10 to 100 req/sec (admin access); architecture unchanged but headroom significantly relaxed | Keep at 10 rps and design around it | <details><summary>why</summary>Admin access on the API key enables 100 rps. At 10 rps, caching was survival-critical and concurrent users competed for budget. At 100 rps, 10 concurrent users still get 10 calls/sec each. Fan-out across 5 databases in parallel is comfortable. Planner budget enforcement still matters for cost/latency, but throttling is no longer the binding constraint. Variation Services API remains at 1 rps (IP-based, separate limit).</details> |
| 2026-05-07 | Two-API strategy for Layer 2: E-utilities (ClinVar, PubMed, OMIM) + Datasets API v2 (Gene, Genome, Orthologs, Taxonomy) + Variation Services (dbSNP) | E-utilities only; Datasets API only; single unified client | <details><summary>why</summary>E-utilities and Datasets API cover complementary domains with no overlap. Gene records are richer via Datasets API (GO terms, transcripts). ClinVar, PubMed, OMIM are only available via E-utilities. dbSNP individual lookups use the Variation Services API (1 rps, separate from E-utilities). Three APIs, three rate limit pools, three tools.</details> |
| 2026-05-07 | PubTator3 REST API replaces all local NER/normalization (GNorm2, tmVar3, AIONER) | Build local NER stack (65GB+ memory, mixed Java/Python) | <details><summary>why</summary>PubTator3 wraps GNorm2, tmVar3, AIONER, and BioREx behind one REST endpoint. Accepts raw text, returns normalized entities with database IDs. Free, no auth. Avoids 60GB JVM heap for GNorm2 alone. Already confirmed in NCBI repos deep dive.</details> |
| 2026-05-07 | NCBI KG reference repo used as infrastructure template (UI, observability, guardrails, testing), not as architecture blueprint | Copy the NL-to-Cypher pipeline architecture; build everything from scratch | <details><summary>why</summary>The repo's 7-stage NL-to-Cypher pipeline is a monolithic 2-day prototype, not suitable as architecture. But the surrounding infrastructure (React chat shell with AbortController, LangSmith tracing wired to feedback buttons, guardrail disclaimers, 234-test organization by domain, MCP server patterns) provides solid boilerplate. Avoids reinventing commodity components while keeping our 5-step agent loop architecture clean.</details> |
| 2026-05-07 | Simplified query classification (query class + target entities + tool list) from Plan step instead of contractor's full typed query-plan IR | Full typed IR with cqClass, focusEntities, joins, constraints, modules, resultShape, provenanceRequired; no classification at all | <details><summary>why</summary>The contractor's typed IR is a comprehensive JSON contract between language understanding and execution. For V1 with 3-5 tool calls per query, a full IR adds schema maintenance cost for no benefit. Query class (lookup/single-hop/multi-hop/aggregate/exploratory) drives model tier routing and latency budgets. Target entities and tool list give the Act step what it needs. Upgrade to full IR if query complexity grows beyond what the simplified output can express.</details> |
| 2026-05-07 | Schema slicing for Plan step: send only relevant graph schema portions to the LLM, not the full schema | Send full schema every time; hardcode schema in prompts | <details><summary>why</summary>AGE graph has 14 edge types and dozens of node properties. Sending the full schema wastes prompt tokens and dilutes attention. A lightweight function selects the relevant schema slice based on query class and target entities. Hand-authored for V1, formalized into a builder when query complexity demands it. Concept adopted from contractor's context-pack builder but implemented as a simple function, not the full builder.</details> |
| 2026-05-07 | Contractor's 8-layer architecture not adopted; 5-step agent loop provides equivalent coverage | Adopt full 8-layer architecture (Entry, Guarded Planning, Context Retrieval, Public Surface, Backend Compilation, Knowledge Modules, Provenance Response, Governance) | <details><summary>why</summary>The 8-layer architecture was designed for an enterprise team with federation, multiple knowledge modules, and a GraphQL-only public surface. Our 5-step loop (Guard, Think, Plan, Act, Write) covers the same concerns with less ceremony. Guard = Entry + governance. Think + Plan = Guarded Planning + Context Retrieval. Act = Backend Compilation + Knowledge Modules. Write = Provenance Response. Fewer layers means fewer abstraction boundaries to maintain in a solo build.</details> |
| 2026-05-07 | NL-to-Cypher separation principle adopted: Plan step produces structured output, tools compile to Cypher; LLM never generates Cypher directly | LLM generates raw Cypher from natural language (reference repo pattern); full typed IR intermediary (contractor pattern) | <details><summary>why</summary>Key learning from contractor architecture: natural language should never directly produce database queries. Intermediate representation prevents prompt injection into queries, enables validation before execution, and lets tools apply domain-specific rewrites (GO-term expansion, edge label enforcement). In our architecture: Plan step reasons about what to query (structured output), cypher_query tool handles how to query it (Cypher compilation with guardrails).</details> |
| 2026-05-07 | V1 shoring-up recommendations (6 items) are not System 3 blockers; all are data pipeline fixes for the next graph reload | Treat items 1-3 as blockers that must be fixed before System 3 development | <details><summary>why</summary>The graph is already loaded and running (115M nodes, 693M edges). All 6 recommendations affect the data pipeline (merger, loader, health check), not the query path. System 3 connects read-only and queries the graph as-is. The ~81K NamedThing stubs (0.07% of nodes) rarely appear in typed queries. MedGen name corruption is a display issue with a Layer 2 API fallback. Tag all 6 as "fix before next graph reload."</details> |
| 2026-05-07 | Layer 2 serves as authoritative fallback when Layer 1 graph data is suspect; agent validates graph results before synthesis | Trust Layer 1 data blindly; fail and show error on bad data | <details><summary>why</summary>Layer 1 is a periodic snapshot that can have stale data, corrupted fields (MedGen names showing "SNOMEDCT_US"), wrong categories (NamedThing stubs), or missing nodes. Layer 2 (live NCBI APIs) is always current and authoritative. The agent's Write step validates graph results and falls back to Layer 2 for correction when data looks suspect. Same pattern handles known issues (MedGen names, NamedThing stubs) and future unknown data quality issues. Principle: Layer 1 for speed, Layer 2 for correction, Layer 3 for enrichment.</details> |
| 2026-05-07 | Graceful degradation: the user must never see nothing; always synthesize from whatever layers responded | Show error message on failure; show nothing and let user retry | <details><summary>why</summary>If Layer 1 has bad data, fall back to Layer 2/3. If an API fails, synthesize from whatever did respond. A degraded answer with an explanation ("ClinVar data was unavailable; showing results from the knowledge graph only") is always better than a blank screen. Extends the existing latency budget decision (synthesize with partial results on timeout) to cover data quality failures, API errors, and any other partial-data scenario.</details> |
| 2026-05-07 | LLM is narrative controller only; harness assembles citations and provenance deterministically from structured tool outputs | LLM handles both narrative and citations; fully template-based output with no LLM | <details><summary>why</summary>The LLM writes natural language narrative with placeholder markers ([1], [2]). The harness (code) maps markers to verified sources from tool results (source_url from graph nodes, NCBI API record URLs, PubTator section refs). If a marker doesn't match a real tool result, the harness strips it. The user never sees a citation the harness can't verify. Balance: LLM has narrative flexibility (not rigid templates), but citations are always deterministic and correct. This also reduces cost: if synth tier only writes narrative from pre-assembled data, it can use a cheaper model. Determinism = financial viability.</details> |
| 2026-05-07 | Tools are direct Python functions inside FastAPI for V1; MCP delivery format wraps the same functions behind MCP protocol separately | Tools ARE MCP servers from the start; raw API calls without tool abstraction | <details><summary>why</summary>Tools as direct functions avoids inter-process communication overhead and keeps V1 simple. The same tool code (cypher_query, ncbi_efetch, etc.) serves both the web UI agent and the MCP delivery format. MCP is a delivery format (one of 5), not the internal tool architecture. Tools follow the CLI-style contract surface pattern: narrow, strongly typed, validated arguments, formatted results. The model never sees raw API responses.</details> |
| 2026-05-07 | SOUL.md behavioral directives pattern adopted for agent domain rules; loaded into prompt every session | Hardcode rules in system prompt; no behavioral directive file; full USER.md + MEMORY.md from day 1 | <details><summary>why</summary>SOUL.md contains domain rules as natural language directives the LLM follows ("prioritize peer-reviewed sources," "always include clinical significance," "cite every claim"). Stored as a file, loaded into the prompt, versionable in git. Gives the LLM clear operating constraints while maintaining narrative flexibility. USER.md (user context) and MEMORY.md (learned strategies) are V1.1 candidates, not V1. Keep V1 focused on one behavioral directive file.</details> |
| 2026-05-07 | Verification loop between Write step and user: rules-based checks on every response, LLM-as-judge reserved for eval harness only | No verification (trust LLM output); LLM-as-judge on every response | <details><summary>why</summary>After Write step produces structured JSON output but before user sees it, deterministic checks run: every citation marker maps to a real tool result, CURIEs resolve, source_urls are valid, answer addresses query intent. Cheap and fast. In-memory checks only, no HTTP calls on the live path. LLM-as-judge is expensive and reserved for offline eval (Phase 4 golden dataset), not the live query path. This catches hallucinated citations without adding latency or cost to every response.</details> |
| 2026-05-07 | Cypher generation uses a dedicated LLM call inside the cypher_query tool, not the main agent loop; 3-stage internal pipeline (receive intent, generate Cypher, validate then execute) | LLM generates Cypher as free-form output from the main agent; fully template-based Cypher with no LLM | <details><summary>why</summary>The NL-to-Cypher separation principle (Plan step decides WHAT, tool decides HOW) requires the tool to translate structured intent into actual Cypher. The combinatorial space (14 edge types, 10 node types, multi-hop, property filters) is too large for pure templates. Solution: cypher_query tool makes its own LLM call (plan-tier model, cheap) constrained by schema slice + few-shot examples + edge label enforcement rules. Generated Cypher is validated (syntax check, forbidden keywords, edge labels required, row limit) before execution. If validation fails, one retry with the error message, then fail gracefully. The main agent never sees or generates Cypher.</details> |
| 2026-05-07 | Budget is ~$100/month (relative marker, not hard cap), not $100 total | Strict $100 total budget | <details><summary>why</summary>Clarified during objective review. Hetzner ~$30/month, Railway free, LLM inference single digits/month. $100/month is comfortable for development and testing. The original session notes said "~$100 total for 2-month build" which understated the actual budget flexibility.</details> |
| 2026-07-20 | Wired Claude Code security hooks into settings.json and ported a jq-free JSON parser (lib/_json.sh) that fails closed | Leave the two existing hook files unwired (dead code); keep scan-secrets parsing stdin with jq directly | <details><summary>why</summary>settings.json had no hooks key, so scan-secrets.sh and session-start.sh never fired. Added a PreToolUse Bash gate (block-bash-delete plus scan-secrets), a PreToolUse Edit and Write gate (scan-write-secrets), and a SessionStart gate (session-start plus scan-context-injection). Replaced direct jq parsing with lib/_json.sh, which blocks on a parse failure instead of passing every command through when jq is missing. block-bash-delete makes the file-protection rule structurally enforced rather than only model-obeyed. All five hooks verified with a 12-case test harness. Adopted from personal-os-work Tier 1 SDLC hardening.</details> |
| 2026-07-20 | Adopted Tier 2 SDLC rules and skills from personal-os-work: ai-security-standards, production-standards, production-examples rules plus eval-harness and verify skills | Keep only Tier 1 hooks and defer these to Phase 6 | <details><summary>why</summary>ai-security-standards owns the agent and LLM security layer (treat AI output as untrusted, prompt-injection defense for Layer 2 and Layer 3 retrieved data, least-privilege tools, human approval for high-risk actions). production-standards and production-examples own the code-level gates (parameterized Cypher and SQL via psycopg2 against AGE, cite-or-refuse grounding, no secrets in logs, multi-agent schema validation) with before and after pairs on our FastAPI, AGE, and React stack, including the shell-allowlist-bypass finding against our own settings.json allow rules. eval-harness gives pass@k and pass/fail/abstain scoring tied to the Phase 4 golden dataset and the Citations non-negotiable. verify gives a /verify pre-commit checklist adapted to our stack. All adapted for our repo and verified with skill-adapt-verify plus a stale-token sweep.</details> |
| 2026-07-20 | Deliverable sequence and prototype flow: Plan.md discussions produce the PRD, then the tech spec, then a 1 to 2 page strategic memo distilled from both; the prototype is built from the PRD and tech spec, then all three docs are reconciled from what the prototype teaches | Replace the long PRD with a prototype plus memo; keep a single heavy PRD with no prototype; build the prototype first before any docs | <details><summary>why</summary>The prototype is built from the docs, not instead of them, so the docs stay the source of truth while the prototype validates them. The memo is a distillation for stakeholders who need the decision, not the detail. The Step 6.2 reconciliation is a planned, bounded exception to the mid-build spec freeze in Phase 7.</details> |
| 2026-07-20 | Evaluations are sequenced and elevated into a living evaluation playbook: offline competency-question gate first for a baseline, online feedback loop second once live; the Phase 2 output becomes the evaluation playbook the tech spec references | Build only the offline eval harness; build only the online loop; bury evaluation as one tech-spec section | <details><summary>why</summary>Traces alone do not improve the system; a scored eval set plus re-scoring is the flywheel. The offline gate gives a baseline before shipping; the online loop improves routing per query after launch. A standalone playbook is reused every eval run and model swap, where a tech-spec section would go stale. model-bench covers model selection as a separate eval target from answer quality.</details> |
| 2026-07-20 | Cap the v1 competency-question set to a small, testable number, then expand after the loop is proven | Use the full large CQ set from day one | <details><summary>why</summary>We have far more CQ candidates than we need to prove the system works. A small set makes the offline eval gate fast to run and the feedback loop easy to read. Expand once the flywheel works, not before.</details> |
| 2026-07-21 | Adopt git worktree isolation for concurrent file-mutating builders in bossman-mode; keep phase-branch plus MR as the integration model layered on top | One shared working directory for all parallel agents; a worktree for every agent including read-only reviewers; drop branch-and-merge in favor of worktrees | <details><summary>why</summary>bossman-mode is an SDLC team that runs parallel builders, and multiple agents mutating the same working tree conflict on disk. Worktrees give each concurrent builder its own filesystem, already supported by the Agent and Workflow isolation "worktree" option, and are torn down after merge. Read-only agents (reviewers, researchers, judges) do not need a worktree. Worktrees are the isolation model and branch-and-merge is the integration model: they compose, since each worktree sits on its own branch and still lands via MR. Applied when bossman-mode is updated in Phase 5 (Step 5.1); used in Phase 6.</details> |
| 2026-07-21 | Step 1.6 adoption frame: habit is the goal, trust is the engine. Adopt the Hook Model loop (trigger, action, variable reward, investment) as the product-psychology frame for System 3, with the variable reward and the reason-to-return grounded in verifiable correctness and citations rather than engagement mechanics | Use the Hook Model habit frame alone (consumer engagement tricks); use the adoption-gap trust frame alone and drop habit mechanics; ignore product psychology for a research tool | <details><summary>why</summary>Reconciles the two sources instead of choosing one. For a research tool the reward that makes the loop repeat is "every time I ask, I get a verifiably correct cited answer faster than navigating five databases myself." Trust is the engine of the habit. Ties the Hook variable reward to the citations-non-negotiable rule and the Phase 2 moat test (cannot-just-Google).</details> |
| 2026-07-21 | Step 1.6 adoption metric: the goal is to become the user's default first stop and bookmarked home for NCBI-type questions, measured by return-rate-per-question-occasion, not daily-active use | Set daily-active use as the literal success metric | <details><summary>why</summary>Nir's own bar is weekly, and NCBI researchers have questions episodically, not every day. | 2026-07-22 | Step 2.4 offline eval gate defined. The 8-point rubric from 02_Tier1_eval_spec.md (0-2 across intent understanding, entity normalization, database routing, evidence quality, cross-database synthesis, freshness/versioning, safety/limits, output usability; pass threshold 13 of 16) is confirmed and wired to the eval-harness outcome model. Composition: the rubric grades a single run into one outcome, pass (>= 13/16 and no hard-fail), fail (any hard-fail or < 13), or abstain (correct refusal string); eval-harness pass@k and pass^k then aggregate those per-run outcomes across k samples. Hard-fails are checked every run: provenance = 0 (an uncited claim), safety/limits = 0 on a clinical or pathogenicity question (rendered a verdict), or missing assembly/version on a coordinate or sequence question. Targets for a must-pass moat question: the hard-fails must hold on every run (pass^k = 100%, no fabrication and no verdict ever); the 13/16 quality score uses pass@3 as the floor and pass^3 >= 90% as the reliability target. Determinism via pinned fixtures with a freshness-window allowance for live-API questions. Cite-or-refuse maps to the provenance hard-fail plus abstain-scoring (zero retrieval plus correct refusal scores as pass, not fail). The moat seven are the initial offline eval set; the Phase 4 golden 50 is the expansion. Rubric updates: add explicit abstain-as-pass scoring (assemble-not-classify is already a listed fail). Eval-spec open decisions resolved: ACMG is evidence assembly only, no live BLAST/SRA search (fast-follow with fixtures), no dbGaP controlled-access flows in Tier 1 (the seven are public-data), fixtures pin the data state; the domain sign-off for the golden fixtures is flagged as a Phase 4 process item | A single pass bar instead of the two-level hard-fail-absolute plus quality-floor-and-target structure; report a graded 0-16 score with no binary pass/fail/abstain bucketing | <details><summary>why</summary>The rubric gives nuanced quality; eval-harness gives the safety-aware pass/fail/abstain bucketing and the pass@k/pass^k reliability framing. Composing them keeps both. Treating a fabrication or a rendered verdict as never acceptable (pass^k = 100% on hard-fails) while allowing quality to be high but not perfect (pass@3 floor, pass^3 target) matches a biomedical setting where a confident wrong answer is worse than no answer.</details> |
| 2026-07-22 | Step 2.5 online feedback-loop design. Five stages: (1) capture every interaction (query, route and plan, tools called, answer, citations, rubric outcome, abstain and cite-or-refuse events, coverage instrumentation, explicit user feedback); (2) mine and cluster captured queries to surface poorly-routed queries, untouched-coverage queries, and moat wins to reinforce; (3) review, semi-automated, an automated pass proposes deduped candidate CQs and a human approves; (4) trigger rule, a cluster that recurs above a frequency threshold, passes the moat bar, and is not already covered becomes a candidate new CQ, while a cluster matching an existing CQ with new phrasings reinforces it as a few-shot variant or re-rank signal (this is where the deferred moat signals learn-from-the-system and loop-human-behavior re-enter as re-ranking inputs); (5) promote, an approved CQ becomes a few-shot routing example in the orchestrator (per the Phase 0 few-shot decision, not a classifier and not fine-tuning) and a new eval case in the offline set, human-approved and provenance-gated. The loop closes: the offline gate sets the bar, the online loop grows the CQ set from real usage, and new CQs re-enter both the offline eval set and the orchestrator routing. v1 scope: ship stages 1, 3, and 5 with a human in the loop (capture everything, a lightweight manual review ritual, hand-promotion); defer stage 2 (automated mining, clustering, auto-proposal) to a fast-follow built once enough data is captured to cluster | Build the full automated mining and auto-proposal pipeline in v1; promote via a classification layer or fine-tuning instead of few-shot examples | <details><summary>why</summary>Capture is cheap and is the raw material, and a human-in-the-loop review turns the loop immediately, so v1 proves the loop with minimal build while the expensive automation waits for data. Few-shot promotion honors the Phase 0 routing decision. The human gate on promotion satisfies the security rule that a human approves any agent-behavior change.</details> |
| 2026-07-22 | Step 2.5 feedback-loop data storage and LLM-judge placement. Storage split by function: PostgreSQL (already in the stack) is the loop's durable system-of-record, with an interactions table (query, normalized entities, route, rubric outcome, citations, coverage tags, user feedback, trace_id) and a cq_candidates table (proposed and promoted CQs with few-shot examples and provenance); LangSmith holds the raw per-run traces, linked by trace_id and consumed by graders; PostHog holds behavioral analytics aggregates (volume, feedback clicks, abstain rate, follow-up funnel) feeding the frequency threshold and the loop-human-behavior signal. Postgres is the owned source of truth, not LangSmith, so the promotion pipeline does not depend on a third-party tracing tool's API or retention. Privacy (detail in Phase 4): PII minimization, scoped access, a retention policy, and provenance on every promoted CQ; never store secrets. LLM-as-judge is always a filter upstream of the human, never the final say after: offline eval grader (v1) runs code graders then the LLM-judge then a human only on flagged edge cases; the online candidate pre-screen (deferred to the fast-follow) drafts candidate CQs with a rationale for the human to approve; the model-bench scorer for tier selection is a separate use. The human is the terminal gate on any promotion because it changes routing | Use LangSmith datasets as the system-of-record; let the LLM-judge make the final promotion decision after a human pre-filter | <details><summary>why</summary>Each store does one job well (Postgres queryable and owned, LangSmith for traces, PostHog for behavior). Keeping the human as the terminal promotion gate with the LLM-judge upstream as triage matches the human-approval-for-behavior-change rule and the maker-checker split.</details> |
| 2026-07-22 | Step 2.3 moat-bar refinement (refines the earlier 2026-07-22 structured-bar rows): the ranking dimension is renamed from "cannot just Google" to "no general-tool equivalent", and its empirical test is tightened. The test now runs each candidate against a panel of the strongest general tools, a frontier chat LLM (such as Claude, GPT, or Gemini), an answer engine with retrieval (such as Perplexity), and plain web search, not Google alone. The question passes only if none of them produces the correct answer with verifiable citations across the required databases. The pass condition is grounding, not fluency: a fluent, uncited, or hallucinated-citation answer does not count as the general tool producing it, because general LLMs hallucinate citations and cannot ground across NCBI databases with resolvable source records. The rename and tightened test are reflected in Evaluation_playbook.md; the seven must-pass questions all still pass | Keep the "cannot just Google" name and the search-engine-only framing; test against a single general tool only | <details><summary>why</summary>The original criterion nominally included "a general AI tool", but the name undersold it and the test rigor was underspecified. Frontier LLMs and answer engines are far stronger than a Google search page, so testing only against Google would falsely pass questions a strong LLM handles and inflate the moat. Testing against the strongest tools and scoring on verifiable grounding, which is System 3's actual differentiation, keeps the bar honest against 2026 tools.</details> |
| 2026-07-22 | Step 3 (PRD) risk-tier classification resolved (the pass Phase 1 Step 1.11 deferred to Phase 3): answers are gated in two tiers by stakes. Low-risk answers (factual lookups, cross-database assembly) enforce standard cite-or-refuse. Higher-stakes answers (clinical-adjacent evidence, mechanistic claims) additionally require a citation-substantiation check (the cited passage must support the specific claim) and a cross-source triangulation gate, surfaced to the user through an answer, flag, or ask trust signal. Captured in PRD.md guardrails; the tech spec implements it | Apply one uniform cite-or-refuse gate to every answer regardless of stakes; defer the risk tiering entirely to the tech spec | <details><summary>why</summary>A biomedical answer's cost of being wrong scales with its stakes, so the grounding rigor should scale too. Uniform cite-or-refuse under-protects clinical-adjacent and mechanistic claims (where roughly 39% of published citations do not support their claim), while applying the heavier substantiation and triangulation gates to every simple lookup would add latency and cost with no benefit. The two-tier split matches rigor to stakes.</details> |

| 2026-07-25 | Enforce Claude-to-Codex instruction parity with a PostToolUse sync hook on AGENTS.md, and stop ignoring `.codex` in git. `.codex` is a git-tracked symlink to `.claude` (mode 120000), so the config directory cannot drift and needs no sync process; the `.gitignore` entry for it was inert (ignore rules do not apply to tracked paths) but contradicted the committed state, so it was replaced with a comment explaining why it must stay unignored. AGENTS.md is a real copy and therefore the only true drift surface, so `.claude/hooks/sync-agents-md.sh` regenerates it from CLAUDE.md on every root-CLAUDE.md edit, preserving AGENTS.md's own tool-neutral three-line header and copying the body from line 4 verbatim | Symlink AGENTS.md to CLAUDE.md as well; leave both to the docs-sync agent convention; keep `.codex` in `.gitignore` | <details><summary>why</summary>Symlinking AGENTS.md is the strongest guarantee but would serve non-Claude agents a file headed "# CLAUDE.md" with a Claude-Code-specific intro, losing the all-agents framing that makes the file useful to Codex, Gemini, and Copilot. Convention alone was holding at the time (bodies were byte-identical) but nothing enforced it, and the ask was for a guarantee rather than a habit. The hook is idempotent, skips the write when the result is unchanged, exits 0 always so it can never block an edit, and short-circuits before the JSON parse when the payload does not mention CLAUDE.md, so it adds no measurable cost to unrelated edits. Leaving `.codex` ignored kept a live trap: one `git rm --cached .codex` would activate the rule and a fresh clone would silently lose the symlink.</details> |

| 2026-07-27 | Skip the full multi-agent `claude-security` scan (release-workflow.md Step 3) on a build phase whose diff has no real attack surface yet (no auth, no DB queries, no LLM calls, no external API calls, no secrets, no user data); rely instead on the judge's gate checks, any independent adversarial-probe pass already run that phase, and the always-on `security-guidance` layer. Run the full scan once real external-facing surface actually lands, scoped against the last point a full scan actually covered (e.g. `main` from before the skipped phases merged), not just the new phase's own isolated diff | Run the full scan on every phase regardless of content, as release-workflow.md's Step 3 currently reads unconditionally | <details><summary>why</summary>The full scan is multi-stage (inventory, threat-model, researcher sweep, three-lens adversarial panel, tally) and comparably token-heavy to a judge/fix/sign-off-verifier cycle (100k-175k tokens each, observed this same phase), so a skeleton-phase diff could cost 300k-600k+ tokens for a scan whose marginal value is low against code with no injection, secrets, or external-call surface, already adversarially probed twice independently plus reviewed continuously by the always-on layer. Phase 1.0 (this decision's origin) has no auth, no DB, no LLM calls, and no external APIs; auth (1.1), the real agent loop (2.0+), and external API or Cypher integration (2.1+, 3.x) are where the scan will earn its cost. The scan agent itself flagged the scope risk when declining: a future scan of `main..phase/1.1-branch` covers only 1.1's changes, since 1.0 already merged into `main` before 1.1 branches off, so 1.0's skeleton is never actually swept unless the range is deliberately widened to include it</details> |

Merging them also risks a second, competing chaining convention alongside the one `release-workflow.md` and `bossman-mode` Step 7 already own, which could drift out of sync with those over time</details> |

Building the separate-page version now would be solving a constraint that has not yet bitten. Revisit if ticket volume across many phases actually degrades the board's payload weight or scannability</details> |
| 2026-07-28 | Resolve the `ecdsa` CVE flag by replacing `python-jose[cryptography]` with `PyJWT` for JWT signing in build phase 1.1, rather than keeping `python-jose` and arguing the vulnerable path is unreachable | Keep `python-jose` and document that the `cryptography` backend wins the runtime import race so `ecdsa` never loads; switch to `authlib`/`joserfc`; accept the CVE a second time with a mitigation note | <details><summary>why</summary>Researcher verification established that `ecdsa!=0.15` is a core `Requires-Dist` of `python-jose` 3.5.0, not gated behind the `[cryptography]` extra, so it cannot be excluded in place: it stays installed and keeps failing `pip-audit` regardless of whether it is imported. The reachability argument is real but narrow (`jose/backends/__init__.py:11-14` prefers `CryptographyECKey` and only falls back to the `ecdsa` backend on ImportError, confirmed empirically: `ecdsa` never enters `sys.modules` after `import jose.jwt`), and it does not survive a supply-chain scan, which sees the installed tree, not the import graph. `PyJWT` removes `ecdsa` from the dependency tree outright, is the most actively maintained of the three candidates (2.13.0 released 2026-05-21, pushed 2026-07-27), and covers exactly the HS256 signing Section 15 specifies without `authlib`s broader JWE and JOSE surface. `python-jose` was last released 2025-05-28, over fourteen months stale. `authlib`/`joserfc` remains the fallback if OIDC or JWE needs emerge later</details> |
| 2026-07-28 | Build all six Section 15 tables in phase 1.1 (`users`, `auth_sessions`, `sessions`, `interactions`, `cq_candidates`, `saved_queries`), not only the three named in Section 25s parenthetical | Build only `users`, `interactions`, and `cq_candidates` as Section 25 literally lists, deferring the other three to a later phase | <details><summary>why</summary>The three named tables do not form a loadable schema on their own: `interactions` carries a foreign key to `sessions`, `saved_queries` carries one to `interactions`, and `auth_sessions` is where Section 15s refresh-token model stores its hashes, so auth cannot ship without it. Section 15 is the schema specification and Section 25s list reads as illustrative rather than exhaustive. Splitting the six across two phases would mean writing a migration that deliberately omits declared foreign keys and then a second migration to add them back, which is more work and more risk than doing it once. No capability outside Section 15 is added, so this does not cross the v1 scope boundary</details> |
| 2026-07-28 | Set the refresh-token TTL to 30 days as a build-time default, recorded here rather than left implicit in code. Revisit at the Step 6.2 reconciliation alongside the PII retention window | 7 days (tighter, forces re-login weekly); 90 days (fewer re-logins, longer window for a stolen refresh token); leave it undocumented in code as the builder originally had it | <details><summary>why</summary>Technical_specification.md Section 15 declares the `auth_sessions.expires_at` column but pins no number, so a value had to be chosen at build time and the only wrong move was leaving it unrecorded. 30 days is the common default for a refresh token that rotates on every use: rotation already bounds the damage of a stolen token to a single use (build phase 1.1 implements this as a single atomic UPDATE, so a concurrent replay cannot also succeed), which makes a long window materially less risky than it would be without rotation. 7 days would add re-login friction for the researcher persona without a matching threat reduction, given rotation. Filed by the judge as F-1.1-02, low severity, non-blocking on the ticket but blocking on phase close until written down. This is a v1 prototype value, not a production security posture; the enterprise IAM and session-expiry review is explicitly on the production track per the PRD out-of-scope list</details> |
| 2026-07-28 | Corrects the 2026-07-28 refresh-token TTL row above, which is left in place rather than edited because `decision-logging` forbids modifying an existing entry. Two lifetimes ship, not one: `_REFRESH_TOKEN_TTL` of 30 days (router.py:101) is a sliding idle bound refreshed on every rotation, and `_REFRESH_TOKEN_ABSOLUTE_TTL` of 90 days (router.py:117) is an absolute ceiling measured from the session family`s original `created_at` that does NOT renew on rotation. A continuously rotating holder is therefore cut off at 90 days from first login | Recording 30 days alone, as the superseded row did; recording 90 days only in a code comment, which is the exact defect F-1.1-02 was raised about, recurring for a new constant; editing the earlier row in place | <details><summary>why</summary>The superseded row is wrong in two ways and both were caught by the judge at F-1.1-02 re-verification. First, its rationale rested on rotation already bounding a stolen token to one use, which was FALSE as shipped at the moment it was written: adversary finding F-1.1-07 proved an attacker who rotated first could rotate indefinitely, and that property only became true with the reuse-detection fix in commit `8313d82`. The original reasoning was sound about the design and wrong about the code, which is precisely the gap between a stated property and a shipped mechanism that this phase`s LEARNINGS entry records. Second, it listed 90 days as a rejected alternative while 90 days actually ships as the outer bound of every session. Both numbers are v1 prototype values, not a production security posture; enterprise IAM and session-expiry review remain on the production track per the PRD out-of-scope list, and both are revisitable at the Step 6.2 reconciliation</details> |

The effort per tier restates Section 3.1's existing tier purposes rather than deciding anything new: Guard is classification, Plan owns decomposition and the Cypher-generation call, and Synth is explicitly forbidden from open-ended reasoning over unverified data. The `max_tokens` ceiling is the load-bearing half: reasoning tokens are counted inside `completion_tokens` and billed at the output rate, measured live at 74 of 76 output tokens on a trivial prompt, so an uncapped reasoning model is an uncapped bill. | 2026-07-29 | Set `enable_seqscan = off` on every Layer 1 graph session in `tools/graph_connection.py` | Raise the validator's injected LIMIT above the observed cliff between 10 and 20; move the LIMIT from inside the Cypher onto the wrapping `SELECT`; fix the table statistics server-side so the planner estimates correctly; accept the latency | <details><summary>why</summary>A CURIE lookup with a small LIMIT took 42 seconds. `EXPLAIN` shows the planner estimating `rows=67521` for `properties @> '{"id": "..."}'` where the true answer is 0 or 1, so a small LIMIT makes a Seq Scan look cheap on the assumption it can stop as soon as LIMIT rows are found; when fewer than LIMIT rows match, that exit never comes and it reads all 67.5 million rows. Measured: 42,034 ms with seqscan on versus 108 ms with it off, and labelled multi-hop traversal unaffected at 125 versus 129 ms, so this is not a blanket penalty on scans that are genuinely warranted. The LIMIT-raising alternative was rejected because the cliff is a statistics artifact rather than a stable boundary and because it would silently change how many rows a caller asked for. Moving the LIMIT to the outer SELECT was measured and made no difference at all, still 42 seconds, since the inner plan is chosen the same way. Fixing the statistics is the real repair but the graph is read-only from this repo and belongs to Systems 1 and 2. Worth recording precisely because this is easy to misdiagnose: it is not an absent-entity bug, a PRESENT entity with `LIMIT 5` is equally slow because one row matches and the scan keeps hunting for the other four, while `LIMIT 1` on a present entity is fast</details> |

Every blocker was fixed and the 9-test live end-to-end gate went green at 798 tests passing. That gate runs against the real graph with only the model call mocked, so it cannot be satisfied by mocks, which is exactly the weakness that produced the phase's original false green. The product owner judged the residual risk lower than the cost of a second review round against a weekly budget limit. Marking the tickets `done` was rejected outright: only a judge may set `done`, no judge has seen the rework, and this phase has already demonstrated once that a green suite can be wrong here. The gap is recorded in four places (tracker/phase_2.1.md's Phase close status section, a tracker/BOARD.md flag, a Plan.md revision entry, and the Phase 6 continuation prompt's open-items table) so build phase 2.2 opens knowing a fresh judge and adversary pass is its first task</details> |
| 2026-07-31 | Replaced `budget_for_query_class` with `budget_for_step(step, query_class)`: a model-calling step's per-step timeout now resolves against its own TIER, and only `act` resolves against query class | Keep one per-query-class figure and raise it until the slowest step fits; keep the per-tier floors added earlier the same day as a permanent stopgap; leave Section 19.1's shape alone and accept that the loop cannot complete | <details><summary>why</summary>Section 19.1 budgets a per-step timeout but selects its value by query class alone, and those are two different axes: query class describes how hard the question is, while what a step actually costs is driven by which tier answers it. No single figure bounds all three. Raising the query-class number until synth fits hands a guard classification a 45 second timeout, which is exactly where a tight one is worth having, and leaving it where synth cannot fit kills every query at Write. The floors added earlier the same day worked but were a max() bolted onto the wrong shape; this replaces them. `act` keeps query-class scaling because it is the one step whose work genuinely grows with the question, running more tool calls and traversing more graph. Product owner approved the design change explicitly on 2026-07-31, choosing to implement it now rather than defer it to Step 6.2</details> |
