Both then discard the response and emit a hardcoded stub payload, so this was a thousand tokens per step, twice per query, that nothing read. Removing the calls was rejected because they exist to prove the harness path end to end, which is real value while the classification logic is still stubbed. Absorbing the cost in the timeouts was rejected because it is not a timeout problem: the tier is specified in Section 3.1 as "sub-second, fractions of a cent", so an essay per step was wrong on latency, on cost, and on the tier's stated purpose. The instruction is deliberately not guardrail logic, and does not influence the emitted payload; real validation and classification remain build phase 3.0's job per `v1-scope-boundary`. Result: `out=1` and `out=2`. A ceiling the model hits on every call is a target, not a ceiling</details> |
| 2026-07-31 | Dropped the plan tier's reasoning effort from `high` to `none`, and left the tool budget where commit 9a3f50a widened it | Widen the tool budget again past 90 seconds; swap the plan-tier model; accept the timeout rate and ship the prototype with it | <details><summary>why</summary>F-2.1-B02 was closed on 2026-07-31 by widening the tool budget from 30 to 90 seconds. The third adversary measured that against the real model and found it had not worked: 9 of 10 queries still timed out, now burning 90 seconds and up to $0.022 each instead of 30 seconds and $0.010, so the widening bought nothing but a slower, more expensive failure. Even "Which diseases are associated with NCBIGene:672?", the simplest query this phase can resolve, timed out. The cause was not the budget. The plan tier ran at `effort: high` and spent almost its whole output on reasoning to write one line of Cypher: 1014 output tokens, 970 of them reasoning. Measured over the five shapes that were failing (two-entity compare, disease list, plain lookup, node-plus-aggregate, multi-hop), effort `high` took 163.0 seconds in total with a worst case of 84.3 on multi-hop, and effort `none` took 6.1 seconds with a worst case of 2.2. All five were correct at both settings and neither ever wrote an entity id as a literal, so the reasoning tokens bought no measurable quality on this tier's actual workload at roughly a 27x latency multiple. This is `system-design-patterns` pattern 11 in practice, iterate the harness before swapping the model: the model is unchanged. The budget stays at 90 seconds rather than being tightened back, because tightening it is a second change measured against nothing, and a generous ceiling over a 2-second call costs nothing when it is not being hit. Scope limit recorded in the code: the plan tier today generates one Cypher query against a fixed schema, which is translation rather than reasoning, so this setting is re-measured when build phase 3.x gives the tier real multi-tool decomposition</details> |
| 2026-07-31 | A generated query that references none of the caller's bound entity parameters is rejected, regardless of its shape | Keep extending the validator's literal-shape patterns to cover each new bypass spelling; accept the bypass as a known gap for v1 | <details><summary>why</summary>The third adversary defeated the F-2.1-B01 naming contract twice without ever going through it. `WITH 'NCBIGene' AS p, '7157' AS n WITH p + ':' + n AS target MATCH (g:Gene {id: target})` returned TP53 for a question about BRCA1, and `WITH 'BRCA1 DNA repair associated' AS t MATCH (g:Gene) WHERE g.name = t` returned 100 non-human orthologs with every citation resolving and not one of them the gene asked about. Both reference zero parameters, so `_build_params` returns an empty dict, `_unknown_param_names` returns an empty list, and the entire remediation never engages. The validator's literal check was also found to be catching earlier variants by accident rather than by design, since it was not quote-aware and was pattern-matching the CURIE text inside the string literal. Both halves were fixed: the validator now masks string literals and tracks alias taint, and this invariant sits behind it. Chasing spellings is a losing game because the bypass is definitionally "avoid the shape the checker looks for", whereas the invariant does not depend on spelling at all: the caller supplied entities, so a query consulting none of them is not answering the caller's question. This is `system-design-patterns` pattern 8, remove the ability rather than ask the model not to use it, applied to generated text</details> |
| 2026-07-31 | An aggregate whose derived result is entirely empty triggers one indexed existence probe on the anchor entity, and refuses when that entity is absent from the graph | Always answer the computed zero with its citation; never answer a zero; probe on every derived result rather than only empty ones | <details><summary>why</summary>`count()` over a match that found nothing returns one row holding 0, so the derived-value path added for F-2.1-B05 turned "we found nothing about this entity" into "the answer is zero, here is the source", with `trust_outcome="answer"` and a resolving citation. The existing empty-`target_entities` refusal cannot catch it, because an entity was extracted; it just does not exist. This is the wrong-answer shape a clinician is least equipped to catch, since zero is a plausible biomedical result and it arrives cited. Answering the zero unconditionally was rejected because "zero associations are recorded for this gene" and "this identifier is not in the graph" are different answers and only the first is a finding. Refusing every zero was rejected because a true zero over a real entity is a legitimate answer and refusing it would trade a wrong answer for a missing one. Probing on every derived result was rejected as an unnecessary second round trip: an empty result is the only case the two states are confusable. The probe's label comes from inverting the schema's own `LABEL_CURIE_PREFIXES` map, so it is code-controlled and never assembled from caller text, and the CURIE still travels as a bound parameter. A probe that cannot run returns "undetermined", which is not treated as absence, so a failed check never refuses a real answer</details> |
| 2026-07-31 | Bound Layer 1 memory per session in the client (`max_parallel_workers_per_gather = 0`, `work_mem = '32MB'`) rather than changing server configuration or restricting query generation | Raise the host's RAM; change postgresql.conf on the graph server; block `DISTINCT` or unlabelled traversals at the validator; accept the risk and only monitor | <details><summary>why</summary>A query this tool generated took the graph database down for every user. The real plan model, asked "What are the NCBIGene:672-associated diseases?", produced an unbounded `orthologous_to` traversal with DISTINCT; AGE ran it across four parallel workers and the kernel OOM killer killed the backend at roughly 3 GB resident, causing an abnormal shutdown. Recovered from the host's own `postgresql-15-main.log` and `dmesg`, not inferred. Neither existing bound helped: `LIMIT 100` was present and DISTINCT materializes before the limit applies, and `statement_timeout` bounds time while this was memory. Measured on the server before choosing a value: `work_mem` 64 MB, `hash_mem_multiplier` 2, `max_parallel_workers_per_gather` 4, so a single query could reach five processes at 128 MB per hash node on a 15 GB host already holding 4 GB of shared_buffers. The session-level route was chosen because it is the only one of the four that is reversible, affects no other database user, requires no coordination with Systems 1 and 2 who own the host, and takes effect immediately. Changing `postgresql.conf` was rejected as a change to infrastructure this repo does not own. Blocking DISTINCT at the validator was rejected because DISTINCT is legitimate and the real problem is unbounded traversal breadth, which the validator cannot see from the query text alone. Measured cost across the phase's four query shapes: 125 to 124 ms, 263 to 277 ms, 154 to 154 ms, 696 to 771 ms, so noise except the aggregate at roughly 11 percent. Recorded honestly as a mitigation and not a fix: it bounds what ONE query may spend, and does not stop the model generating an unbounded traversal nor bound several expensive queries at once. Both belong to build phase 2.2 as F-2.1-C15</details> |
| 2026-08-01 | The build cadence gains a twelfth stage: write the premise gate and watch it FAIL, blocking all builder work, mandatory for any phase whose deliverable is model-generated output (build phases 3.1 to 3.5) | Leaving it as strong guidance the Lead applies per phase; making it required but non-blocking so builders run in parallel; scoping it to tool phases only | <details><summary>why</summary>Build phase 2.1 failed four consecutive reviews with a green suite, ending at 879 passing tests and 3 of 8 real questions answered correctly. The cause was a schema slice that handed the generator no Disease label, visible from day one and found on round five. Guidance was already available and was not enough: the Lead had the evidence after round two and kept fixing the layer below. Non-blocking was rejected because a gate written after the code it grades encodes that code's behavior as correct, and watching it fail first is the only thing that proves it can fail. Measured cost is about 40 minutes and $0.013 per run against ten review passes of 20 to 30 minutes; it already caught two regressions before a reviewer did. Product owner's decision, 2026-08-01</details> |
| 2026-08-01 | Process lessons from a failed phase are encoded as blocking stages and mandatory tickets, not as guidance in a rule that an agent is expected to recall under pressure | Adding the four phase 2.1 lessons to the rules only, which is where three of them already partly lived | <details><summary>why</summary>The guidance already existed and did not fire. `attack-the-constraint`, `goal-contracts` and `self-eval-loop` between them already covered finding the bottleneck first, rigor pointing at the wrong layer, and maker-cannot-check. The evidence that the parameter binder was the wrong layer was available after review round two, and three further rounds were spent fixing the binder anyway. A lesson that depends on being recalled at the moment its owner is most invested in the failing approach is not a control. Same principle this repo already applies to code in `system-design-patterns` pattern 8 (remove the ability rather than ask) and `ai-security-standards` (Layer 1 read-only by credential, not by instruction), turned on the build process itself</details> |
| 2026-08-02 | Adopt one owner per fact across the documentation, and enforce it mechanically with `tracker/check_doc_drift.py --check` wired into `/verify` and the `/phase-checkpoint` exit checklist | Keeping the convention as prose guidance in the checkpoint skill; wiring the check as a PreToolUse hook; writing the check but running it by hand | <details><summary>why</summary>The repository had drifted badly, and the measurement pointed at one cause. `CLAUDE.md` and `AGENTS.md` are the only pair in the repo that never diverged, and they are the only pair with a sync hook behind them. Every other pair kept in sync by discipline had drifted: seven values circulated for one Python test count, `Plan.md` called build phase 2.1 next while its own status table called it merged, and `requirements/phase_6/Continuation_prompt.md` described that phase in five contradictory sections. Guidance was rejected because guidance is what had already failed; a hook was rejected because it touches the security-enforcement layer and needs its own review; run-by-hand was rejected on the repo's own stated principle that a control depending on someone remembering it is not a control. The script earned its place immediately, catching four stale spots that seven human-equivalent review passes had missed, including one in `CLAUDE.md` itself</details> |
| 2026-08-02 | Scope `/phase-checkpoint`'s append-only constraint to DECISIONS.md and session documents only, and state explicitly that the continuation prompt is rewritten in place with superseded sections deleted | Leaving the checklist item unscoped and relying on the skill's other items to force currency | <details><summary>why</summary>This was the mechanical cause of the drift, not a contributing factor. The skill's constraints line already scoped append-only correctly, but its exit checklist restated it with no scope at all, while step 4 required refreshing the continuation prompt's status line. Faced with that contradiction, five consecutive build-phase checkpoints resolved it the safe-looking way and appended a new section instead of correcting the false one above it. Two other checklist items demanding current status and no stale counts were unsatisfiable the whole time</details> |
| 2026-08-02 | Delete `CHANGELOG.md` and recreate it at the v1.0.0 release rather than backfilling it | Backfilling entries for build phases 2.0, 1.2 and 2.1 and keeping it current per phase | <details><summary>why</summary>A changelog documents releases and nothing has been released. It had missed three of five build phases and still asserted that the agent loop, the tools and real query behavior did not exist yet, all three of which had shipped. Backfilling was rejected because it would add an eighteenth update obligation to every future phase for a record whose facts already live in `Plan.md`'s revision history and the tracker phase files. Product owner's decision</details> |
| 2026-08-02 | Consolidate the seven never-cited rule files into surviving rules rather than deleting them, accepting that this saves no always-loaded context | Deleting the ten uncited rules outright; leaving `.claude/rules/` untouched | <details><summary>why</summary>Ten of 28 rules had never been cited by any of five completed build phases. Consolidation was chosen over deletion so no requirement could be lost, and an independent audit against a 259-line requirement inventory confirmed zero requirements missing. The honest result is that the byte total did not fall: 132,181 before, 134,463 after across 22 files instead of 28. Consolidating moves content rather than removing it, so the always-loaded layer was not reduced. Recorded as a measured negative result rather than presented as a saving. The `communication-style` merge into `CLAUDE.md` was reverted the same day for the same reason: both surfaces load on every turn, so moving content between them is not an optimization, and it pushed `CLAUDE.md` past its roughly 200-line budget while contradicting its own line stating that rules need no duplication there</details> |
| 2026-08-02 | Drop reasoning effort to medium for every cadence role except the judge, the adversary, the decomposition and the premise gate, and demote the mechanical roles from the depth tier to balance | Leaving the tiering as it was; dropping every role including the judge and adversary | <details><summary>why</summary>Two independent measurements agreed. Externally, high to medium gave 76 percent fewer output tokens at the same task-completion rate, each tier costs roughly twice the one below, and thinking tokens reach up to 40 percent of output spend while being replayed as input on every later turn. Internally, this repo had already measured the plan tier spending 970 of 1014 output tokens on reasoning and taking 163.0 seconds against 6.1 at the lowest setting, a 27x latency multiple, with all five test queries correct at both settings. That measurement sat in `LEARNINGS.md` unacted on. The judge, adversary, decomposition and premise-gate roles keep the higher tier because build phase 2.1 is direct evidence that weak review costs whole rounds, and because a missed defect there is expensive rather than merely slower</details> |
| 2026-08-02 | Leave the `context7` marketplace plugin enabled globally, and leave the eight claude.ai account connectors unauthenticated | Disabling `context7` account-wide; authenticating the connectors that look useful; configuring project-scoped MCP servers for this repository | <details><summary>why</summary>The audit found this repository configures no MCP servers at all, so it pays no project-scoped schema cost today. The external figure that motivates MCP pruning, up to 16,000 tokens per turn for 10 servers exposing 50 tools, assumes every schema is loaded on every turn. This harness defers tool schemas and loads them on demand, so `context7`'s two tools cost nothing until called, which removes most of the case for disabling it. Its usage counter shows 5 calls somewhere in the account and none provably in this repository, but disabling it is account-wide and would remove it from every repository for a saving that is close to zero here. The eight connectors cost nothing while unauthenticated. Standing condition attached: if the claude.ai PubMed connector is ever authenticated and reached for while building this repository's own `ncbi_efetch` family of tools, that runs directly against the 2026-07-24 decision to keep NCBI access in vetted in-repo tool code rather than a third-party MCP server, and that decision must be revisited explicitly rather than bypassed</details> |
| 2026-08-02 | The two personal-productivity lines removed from `attack-the-constraint.md` stay removed, recorded as a deletion rather than a consolidation | Restoring them; rewriting them into a software-repository equivalent | <details><summary>why</summary>The lines were "of the options in my cluster (Build/Learn/Practice)" and "Recovery/rest days, not everything needs to be optimized", both imported from a personal operating system and neither meaningful in a repository that builds one software system. An independent auditor correctly flagged that this was a deletion inside a pass whose stated promise was consolidation without loss, and escalated it for explicit sign-off rather than letting it pass as a merge. Product owner approved the deletion on 2026-08-02. Logged so the audit trail shows a decision rather than a silent removal, which is the distinction that made the flag worth raising</details> |
| 2026-08-03 | Cite a vocabulary-artifact row on its CURIE rather than on the artifact value, keeping the `hedged` confidence downgrade and the `vocabulary_artifact_fields` payload marker | Keeping build phase 2.1's behavior, which cited `name=MeSH` and hedged it; suppressing the row entirely; adding a name frequency table to detect the artifact more precisely | <details><summary>why</summary>This reverses a build phase 2.1 decision, so it is logged rather than absorbed. A `Disease` row whose stored `name` is "MeSH" is a documented MedGen ETL defect. 2.1 cited the artifact and hedged, on the principle that a corrupted value should be shown rather than hidden. Under a grounding pass that principle inverts: handing Synth `field_value="MeSH"` gives it two options, state something false, or state the CURIE and have the grounding pass strip it as unsupported, and the 2.1 behavior is unreachable anyway because a claim citing "MeSH" would have to SAY "MeSH" to survive. The CURIE is the strongest true statement the row supports and resolves to a real record. Nothing is hidden: the hedge is unchanged and the artifact is still reported verbatim on the finding payload, both asserted by tests. This also resolves the continuation prompt's "vocabulary-artifact rule would be strictly better as a name frequency table" item differently than that note guessed, since the frequency table was an idea for detecting the artifact better and the actual defect was what the code did after detecting it. Product owner confirmed 2026-08-03, delegating the call to the lead's recommendation. The deciding argument on review: the 2.1 behavior is not merely worse under a grounding pass, it is unreachable, since a citation's claim_text is now a sentence the model actually wrote that survived matching, so citing the artifact would require the model to write "BRCA1 is associated with MeSH" and have that false sentence grounded</details> |
| 2026-08-03 | Add `numbers_are_supported` as a second grounding check, requiring every standalone number in a claim to appear in the cited value or in the user's own question | Implementing Section 8.2 step 5 exactly as written and filing the hole as a finding only; rejecting any claim longer than its field value; a general token-overlap ratio | <details><summary>why</summary>Section 8.2 step 5 accepts a claim when "one is a substring of the other", and the `field_value in claim_text` direction lets invented text ride along: given a finding worth "15310", the clause "BRCA1 has 15310 variants and 400 orthologs [1]" grounds cleanly with the ortholog count fabricated, a real marker, and a resolving URL. That is build phase 2.1's fluent-wrong-answer shape relocated into synthesis. Numbers were chosen as the target because a count, position, or frequency is the highest-risk invented content in a biomedical answer and is unverifiable by eye, while names and identifiers are already constrained by the substring rule itself. Question-supplied numbers are allowed because restating the subject of a question asserts nothing new; a first version without that allowance stripped the one sentence that actually answered the flagship question. This only ever rejects claims the spec would accept, so it cannot weaken the gate, but it is a tightening of a LOCKED specification and is filed as F-2.2-02 for the Step 6.2 reconciliation rather than treated as settled</details> |
| 2026-08-03 | Render the record type and identifier in the Synth findings block, not `field` and `field_value` alone | Rendering only the two Section 8.1 schema fields; adding the entity type to the Section 8.1 wire schema; passing the raw rows to the model | <details><summary>why</summary>Measured, not reasoned: the field-only rendering produced "[1] curie: MedGen:C0346153" four times over, and a real model asked "which diseases are associated with NCBIGene:672?" replied "I could not find information on this." and was right to, because four opaque identifiers under a field named `curie` do not say they are diseases. The correct answer was not expressible from what the model was given, so seven premise-gate questions failed and all seven pointed at synthesis. Found in one probe by printing the exact prompt, which `attack-the-constraint` names as the first move and which build phase 2.1 took five rounds to do. The type is carried as a local field on `SynthFinding` rather than added to Section 8.1's wire schema, since that schema is what a model reads and every field in it is a field a model can misread</details> |
| 2026-08-03 | Build the Section 8.4 refusal link from the resolved entity string rather than the raw query text | Using the raw query text, which Section 8.4 step 2 also permits | <details><summary>why</summary>Both are spec-permitted, so this is a choice between two allowed options rather than a deviation. The raw text is echoed back to the user inside a rendered link, so a question carrying an injected instruction produced a refusal link containing "...causes%20Marfan%20syndrome...". Nothing is executed and nothing is asserted, so this is not the injection defect itself, but reflecting attacker-supplied text into a user-visible link is a gap worth not having, and the resolved entity is the better search term regardless. Falls back to the raw text when nothing resolved, since a link built from an empty term is a link to nothing. Found by the premise gate rather than by a reviewer</details> |
| 2026-08-03 | Close build phase 2.2 after three review rounds with F-2.2-T-01-residual open, rather than run a fourth round | Running round 4 on the same fix loop; shipping without the round-3 fixes; reverting the phase | <details><summary>why</summary>Three consecutive rounds each found their worst defect in the previous round's fix, which is the exact pattern `LEARNINGS.md`'s build phase 2.1 retrospective predicts. The dangerous direction is closed and verified: every adversary, judge and re-review exploit refuses, both round-3 gates were mutation-tested, the premise gate is 11 of 12 and the eval gate passed 12 of 12 runs. What remains is the SAFE direction of failure, a comma-spliced injection inside a single wh-question that licenses its own words, pinned by a strict xfail so it cannot be silently forgotten. A fourth round would be the fourth request to wait on one phase, and Step 6.2 is a scheduled reconciliation pause that already sits between 2.2 and 3.0, which makes it the right place to weigh the residual against the rest of the plan rather than iterating in place. Product owner's decision</details> |
| 2026-08-03 | Fix a grounding false-reject at the prompt rather than by widening the licensed token set | Inverting the one-entry gene-symbol seed table so a CURIE also licenses its symbol; adding a synonym list; accepting the false-reject rate | <details><summary>why</summary>The model answered a question about `NCBIGene:672` by writing `BRCA1`, a token in neither the question nor the retrieved rows, so the content check stripped the clause and the user lost one of four diseases with nothing saying so. Inverting the seed table would have fixed the single question the premise gate asks and nothing else, since that table holds exactly one entry, which is tuning to the test rather than fixing the defect. Widening by guessing at synonyms would reopen the question-seeding hole, since the question is attacker-controlled. Reading the input first showed the gate was RIGHT and the model was violating its own instruction, since a symbol supplied from model memory is not retrieved data. One added instruction rule fixed it with no check loosened and no token allowlisted. Verified on three consecutive live runs at `stripped=0` each time, and the two-hop eval case went from intermittent to 3 of 3</details> |
| 2026-08-03 | Guard the `n/a` sentinel as a compiled pattern rather than narrow the repo-wide model-id shape guard | Adding the sentinel to the frozenset and relaxing `test_no_model_id_shaped_string_outside_the_default_table` to admit short segments | <details><summary>why</summary>Three parallel workers each made an individually correct change, and together they broke a guard none of them could see: `system-design-patterns` pattern 11 forbids a bare vendor-slash-model literal outside `harness/tiers.py`, and the sentinel matches that shape exactly. Narrowing the guard to admit short segments would have made it start missing the degenerate hardcoded-model-id case it exists to catch, trading a real architectural control for convenience. A compiled pattern keeps the guard at full strength and also accepts the spaced variant an ETL can emit. Only the full suite could see this defect; three green workers could not, which is why the composition check is run rather than inferred</details> |
| 2026-08-03 | Detect non-renderable finding values with a type allowlist rather than extend the named-type blocklist | Adding float specials, bytes and object as further named degenerate cases | <details><summary>why</summary>Three rounds of this phase watched a blocklist of bad shapes lose to the next shape nobody enumerated: first a literal null, then the ETL sentinel strings, then containers, then float specials and arbitrary objects, the last rendering a memory address as a disease name. `LEARNINGS.md`'s 2026-08-01 validator entry records the same lesson from the same repo: a blocklist of unsafe shapes is infinite while an allowlist of safe ones is finite. Only a string or a finite number can be rendered as a citable value, and everything else routes to the CURIE fallback. The zero carve-out is preserved deliberately, since a zero-valued count is real data rather than an absence</details> |
| 2026-08-03 | Defer the `main` to `develop` default-branch rename to Step 6.2, and do the GitHub rename and the docs sweep as one change there | Renaming immediately after build phase 2.2 merged; renaming on GitHub now and sweeping the docs later; leaving the branch named `main` permanently | <details><summary>why</summary>Two constraints both resolve at that checkpoint and neither resolves before it. `requirements/Technical_specification.md` Section 24 states that Railway's GitHub integration watches `main` only and that a merge to `main` triggers the deploy, and that document is locked until the Step 6.2 reconciliation, so renaming earlier would mean either editing a frozen document or knowingly leaving three wrong lines inside it. Separately, Step 6.2 is already sweeping the spec and the rules, so folding the branch sweep in costs almost nothing where doing it separately is a second full pass over the same files. Nothing is live on the old name, since there is no `railway.json`, no `.github/workflows/` and no connected deploy, so the deferral risks nothing operational. Recorded with the exact rename command and the list of 8 genuine branch references in Plan.md Step 6.2, so the sweep does not have to be re-derived, and so the many `maintain`, `domain`, `main agent` and `main loop` matches are not swept by mistake. Product owner delegated the call to the lead's recommendation</details> |
| 2026-08-03 | Move Step 6.2 to run after the 3.x tool phases rather than immediately after build phase 2.2, and continue in build order from 3.0 | Running Step 6.2 now, as the plan originally sequenced it; jumping straight to 3.1 to unblock a demo and skipping 3.0 | <details><summary>why</summary>Step 6.2's own written justification argues against its own position. It explains that the security scan waits rather than running per phase because "scanning per phase would pay repeatedly for the cheap half of the surface while the genuinely dangerous code (Cypher generation against the live graph in 2.1, LLM calls and the agent loop in 2.0, untrusted NCBI payloads reaching synthesis in 3.x) had not landed yet." That names 3.x as the dangerous code, so scanning before 3.x scans everything except the thing the scan is most for. The same holds for the reconciliation half: Step 6.2 exists to update the frozen documents from what the prototype taught, and reconciling after the tool phases is strictly better input than reconciling before them. The blocking argument for going first was that agents would build against known-wrong documentation, and that argument was checked rather than assumed: `.claude/rules/production-examples.md` and `docs/ncbi/Tool_implementation_mechanics.md` are both already corrected, and the one document still carrying the wrong claim is the locked tech spec's Section 6.1, which describes `cypher_query`, a tool already built. Build phase 3.1 reads Section 6.2 instead. Two conditions attached rather than left implicit: the whole-repository security scan must run before anything is deployed or before a real user touches the system, since its real trigger is exposure rather than a position in a sequence, and the frozen-spec findings stay logged in `tracker/phase_2.2.md` and Step 6.2's own list so deferral cannot quietly become forgetting. Product owner's decision, after the lead initially defended the original sequence from the plan's authority rather than testing whether it still held</details> |
| 2026-08-03 | Treat "a working prototype you can show people" as an explicit, separate bar from Step 6.1's written goal | Declaring the prototype done because Step 6.1's stated goal was met; redefining Step 6.1 retroactively | <details><summary>why</summary>Step 6.1's goal is written as "something you can see and feel end to end, one real query through the agent loop with a real answer and citations", and that bar is genuinely met: the premise gate proves a real question reaches the live graph and returns a grounded cited answer. The product owner asked whether it is a prototype that can be SHOWN to people, which is a different bar, and testing it rather than asserting it showed the gap immediately. `_KNOWN_GENE_SYMBOL_CURIES` holds exactly one entry, `BRCA1`, so "What diseases are linked to TP53?" resolves to nothing, and so does every disease-name or plain-language question. A visitor can ask about one gene or type raw NCBI CURIEs. The loop works and the product does not yet. Recorded because the two bars were conflated in every status document at once, and the fix is not to rewrite Step 6.1's goal but to name the second bar separately: symbol resolution beyond the one-entry table is finding F-2.1-07, already owned by build phase 3.1, and that phase is what actually stands between this repo and a demo</details> |
| 2026-08-03 | Pause the whole-repository security scan indefinitely, and drop it as a hard prerequisite for Step 6.3 | Running it at Step 6.2 as scheduled; running it once after the 3.x tool phases; running a reduced-scope scan over only the dangerous surfaces | <details><summary>why</summary>Cost. The multi-agent scan is token-expensive and the product owner is not funding it for prototype code. The risk this accepts is genuinely bounded today and the reasons are the same ones that justified deferring it three times already: the Step 6.1 prototype holds no real user data, is not deployed, has no CI or CD wired, and is never exposed. Layer 1 access is read-only by credential rather than by instruction, so the worst input cannot mutate the graph. What does NOT change, and is the reason this is a defensible pause rather than dropping security: the five hooks stay armed (secret scanning on Bash and on config writes, the deletion block, the sensitive-read block, the context-injection scan), `production-standards` and `ai-security-standards` still gate every line written, and `pip-audit` plus `ruff` are already installed and cost nothing to run. The one condition that survives the pause: exposure re-triggers it. If this is ever deployed, given a public URL, or shown to a user who is not the product owner, the scan runs first. That trigger is written into Step 6.2 and `tracker/BOARD.md` so the pause cannot quietly become a permanent hole. Product owner's decision</details> |
| 2026-08-04 | Give the guardrail's premise gate two arms, and anchor the admit arm on the v1 must-pass moat questions | A reject-only gate, as every prior premise gate in this repo has been; asserting refusal counts without asserting admissions | <details><summary>why</summary>Build phase 2.2 had one safe direction of failure, so its gate could lean on a single direction and still be sound: withholding an answer was acceptable and asserting a false one was not. A guardrail has no safe direction. Under-blocking is a security hole, and over-blocking is a product-killing defect that is invisible to every security test ever written, because `return refuse` scores one hundred percent on the reject arm. A gate with only a reject arm therefore cannot tell a working guardrail from a guardrail that refuses everything. Nine of the eighteen cases are legitimate questions that must get through, including three collision traps where a real biomedical question shares a word with a block rule: "deletions" is a Cypher write verb and the commonest structural variant type, "treatments" is a medical-advice trigger and exactly what must-pass question Q4 routes to ClinicalTrials for, and "diagnostic" is an advice trigger and an ordinary evidence noun. The admit arm caught real defects the reject arm could not: the first allowlist refused the flagship question "Which diseases are associated with BRCA1?" because it carried `disease` and the question said `diseases`</details> |
| 2026-08-04 | Let the Section 10.2 pre-filter refuse or abstain, never admit, and make abstention the default wherever evidence is ambiguous | Letting the pre-filter admit a query outright when it clears the allowlist and matches no block pattern; tightening its patterns until they are precise enough to admit on | <details><summary>why</summary>Section 10.2 calls itself "a coarse, fast net" and says it "is not expected to catch everything". Making it unable to admit converts every gap in it from a security bypass into a wasted model call, which is the difference between a defect and a cost. That asymmetry then decides every subsequent tuning question in the module: the symbol pattern is deliberately over-broad and matches USA as well as BRCA1, because a false match costs one Guard-tier call while a false miss silently refuses a real scientist; the injection patterns were NARROWED after the adversary showed "Ignore the previous cohort" refused as prompt injection, since that sentence and "ignore the gene above" are the same shape and no regex separates them; and the off-topic check abstains on any query showing no sign of English, since a keyword miss then says only that an English vocabulary cannot read the language. The load-bearing corollary, learned the hard way in judge finding JUDGE-01: when a deliberate weakness is justified by saying another layer covers it, that claim is about a different module and must be verified there. The comment excusing the symbol pattern said the classifier would refuse the query anyway, and the classifier judged only injection, so genuinely off-topic queries were admitted</details> |
| 2026-08-04 | Assert the write-seeking refusal in the premise gate WITHOUT pinning a category, rather than choosing one | Pinning `off_topic`, which is what the code emits for the direct phrasing; pinning `injection`, which is what the classifier emits for the indirect phrasing; adding a write-shaped enum member to the contract now | <details><summary>why</summary>Section 10.5 requires that a write-seeking request be "rejected outright" and names no `GuardPayload.category` for it, and the contract's six members contain nothing write-shaped (finding F-3.0-01). Measured against the real model, the direct phrasing lands on `off_topic` through the Section 10.5 screen and "...Go ahead and apply it" lands on `injection` at the classifier, because that phrasing genuinely is an imperative aimed at the system. Both refuse, neither is wrong, and the category is contested only because the spec left it undefined. The gate therefore asserts what the spec actually requires, that the query is refused and never reaches Think, plus the weaker constraint that whatever category it carries is one the contract can express. Pinning a value no document specifies would mean the person resolving F-3.0-01 has to edit a premise gate to land a decision the gate was never entitled to make. This is deliberately NOT a weakening of the verify surface: the gate still fails if a write request is admitted, which is the security property</details> |
| 2026-08-04 | Re-home build phase 3.0's carried findings to owners that will actually fire, and give the C15 generation bound a dated position immediately after build phase 3.1 | Leaving each item with the owner it was first given; folding all of them into Step 6.2; leaving the C15 bound undated until exposure forces it | <details><summary>why</summary>Three of the four carried items had a phase number written next to them that would never have triggered. T-3.0-08 read "build phase 3.1, whichever phase next touches generation", and 3.1 is `ncbi_efetch`, a Layer 2 tool: neither it nor 3.2, 3.3 or 3.5 ever opens `tools/cypher_generation.py`. ADV-03, ADV-06 and ADV-07 read "the next round", and no next round was scheduled. This is the ownerless-requirement shape `attack-the-constraint` names, and this repo already carries a live example of where it leads: `release-workflow` is marked "mandatory, no skips" and has 0 of 6 real dispatches. A requirement nobody owns is not deferred, it is abandoned with extra steps. Resolved as: F-3.0-01 at Step 6.2 (a frozen-document conflict, which is what that pause is for), ADV-03/06/07 at build phase 6.1 (defense-in-depth belongs in the hardening pass), T-3.0-07 environment-gated rather than phase-gated (it needs an SSH tunnel no phase requires), and T-3.0-08 immediately after 3.1 merges. The last one was dated deliberately rather than left as "a dedicated branch", since a branch name says who and not when, and F-2.1-C15 is the finding where a generated query took the graph server down for every user. All four remain governed by the same exposure trigger the paused security scan carries: none can affect anyone while nothing is deployed and no user who is not the product owner has access</details> |
| 2026-08-04 | Create `PROGRESS.md` at the repo root as a plain-language project update, and make refreshing it a mandatory step of `/phase-checkpoint` | Extending `README.md`, which is the technical overview; extending the Phase 6 continuation prompt, which is written for the next agent; keeping the status only in `tracker/board.html`, which renders phase names and ticket ids | <details><summary>why</summary>Every artifact this project maintains is written for a builder or for an agent, and none of them answers "what does this do, what works, and what is next" for someone who has never seen the code. `tracker/board.html` is the closest and still assumes the reader knows what a phase number means. The document is deliberately the one exception to the skill's own one-owner rule: it restates facts that live elsewhere, in different words, because its reader cannot follow a pointer into `tracker/phase_N.M.md` and get anything from it. The protection against the drift that rule exists to prevent is that it is refreshed at a numbered step of every checkpoint from the same sources, rather than edited ad hoc. Its writing rules are stricter than anywhere else in the repo: no jargon unless explained in the same sentence, no internal finding identifiers in the body, concrete over abstract, and the failures stay in, since a sanitized highlight reel would defeat the purpose</details> |
| 2026-08-04 | Add a metered alternate model backend to the build harness via OpenRouter, scoped by ROLE rather than applied per session, with the depth-tier stages excluded from failover | A whole-session swap, which is what the existing local ops note already described; direct per-vendor endpoints (Moonshot, Z.ai) instead of one gateway; a local LiteLLM proxy translating between formats; switching to a different agent CLI entirely | <details><summary>why</summary>The weekly limit on the primary provider stops the build outright, and build phase 3.1 is open with a board date on 2026-08-26. Swapping the model behind an unchanged Claude Code CLI keeps the five security hooks, the permission engine, the rules, the skills and the sub-agents intact, because all of those belong to the CLI rather than to the model; CLAUDE.md's portability audit is about losing the CLI, which this deliberately does not do. OpenRouter was chosen over per-vendor endpoints because it now serves an Anthropic-format endpoint directly, so one key reaches every candidate model with no proxy to run and no second translation layer to debug. The role scoping is the load-bearing half and is not a refinement: `CLAUDE_CODE_SUBAGENT_MODEL` is global within a session and overrides both per-invocation model parameters and subagent frontmatter, so a single-session swap necessarily puts the judge and adversary on the builder's model. This repo has already measured what that costs, since build phase 2.1 failed four consecutive judge and adversary reviews behind a fully green test suite, which is the same evidence that held those two roles at the depth tier on 2026-08-02. So decomposition, premise gate design, judge and adversary never fail over; a review run on the fallback records findings and closes nothing, and no phase reaches merge until those stages have run on the primary provider. Implemented as launch wrappers on PATH rather than shell-profile exports, because an export in `~/.zshrc` would route every session in every repository through the gateway and bill this key for all of it, and because a global export cannot be one model for the judge and another for the builders at the same time. Nothing under `.claude/` is modified. The accepted risk is stated rather than hidden: Anthropic does not support routing Claude Code to non-Claude models through any gateway, and OpenRouter guarantees reliable tool use only on Anthropic's own models, which in a harness where every action is a tool call is the failure mode that matters, so tool-call fidelity is an explicit verification gate before any profile is trusted</details> |
| 2026-08-04 | Put the alternate backend's depth band on an Anthropic model rather than an open one, and reject the assumption that a frontier-class open model is the cheap option there | Kimi K3 on the depth band, which was the original premise; GLM 5.2; leaving the band on the same open model as the builders | <details><summary>why</summary>Measured against the live OpenRouter catalogue on 2026-08-04, not assumed. Per million tokens, input and output: Kimi K3 costs 3.00 and 15.00 against Opus 5 at 5.00 and 25.00 through the same gateway. Kimi K3 is therefore only about 40 percent cheaper for the one role where a missed defect costs an entire review round, and it carries the tool-use risk both vendors warn about. Paying the remaining 40 percent removes that risk completely, which is the better trade at that price gap even though it is the more expensive line item. The same catalogue read corrected a second inherited assumption: DeepSeek V4 Pro at 0.435 and 0.87 undercuts both Kimi K2.6 (0.589 and 2.48) and GLM 5.2 (0.76 and 2.42) on both sides while carrying a larger context than either, so the middle band moved to it. The band-to-model mapping in `harness/tiers.py` that this originally mirrored is itself explicitly a placeholder pending the build phase 7.0 model-bench, so mirroring it was convention rather than evidence. The selection metric is recorded as dollars per completed ticket rather than dollars per token, because a cheaper model that causes one extra review round costs more than a dearer one that lands first time, and this repo has the 2.1 round count to prove the size of that term</details> |
| 2026-08-04 | Re-choose the alternate backend's model set on three explicit criteria, maximum capability, maximum context, lowest price, and move the depth band off the Anthropic model to `x-ai/grok-4.20`. SUPERSEDES the row above, which argued the depth band should stay on `anthropic/claude-opus-5` | Keeping Opus 5 on the depth band; `openai/gpt-5.6-sol`, the product owner's proposed substitute; `x-ai/grok-4.5`; `google/gemini-3.1-pro-preview`; leaving the whole set as inherited convention | <details><summary>why</summary>The earlier row reasoned from a two-way comparison (Opus 5 against Kimi K3) and reached the right answer for that pair while missing the field. A full catalogue read on 2026-08-04 priced the alternatives properly: `gpt-5.6-sol` costs 5.00 / 30.00 against Opus 5's 5.00 / 25.00, so the proposed substitute is 20 percent MORE expensive, and `gpt-5.6-terra` at 1.00 / 6.00 is roughly seven times the output price of the configured balance model. Both premises behind "use the other vendor to save money" were false. What was true is that better options existed and neither prior row had looked: `x-ai/grok-4.20` carries a 2,000,000 token context, the largest in the catalogue, at 1.25 / 2.50, which is one tenth of Opus 5's output price with double the context. The argument that makes the depth swap defensible rather than a gamble on the highest-stakes role is structural, not economic: a review produced on the fallback is non-binding by construction, since it records findings, closes no ticket, and stages 8 and 9 re-run on the subscription before anything merges. Paying a premium for a pass that is never final was the weak position, and the binding review is still the strongest model available, just the one already paid for. The 2M context earns its place separately, since a judge reads a whole phase diff at once. The speed band also moved, to `qwen/qwen3.7-flash` at 0.030 / 0.130 with a 1M context, the cheapest model in the catalogue carrying both a million-token context and tool support, roughly 4.7x cheaper on input than the prior choice. The balance band did not move: nothing at that context class is both cheaper and plausibly as capable. Nine candidates were probed for tool-call fidelity before any of this was decided and all nine passed, which is why price and context, rather than tool use, became the discriminator. Stated as a limit rather than buried: passing that probe proves a model emits a well-formed tool call, not that it reasons well, so capability remains a gate-5 and cost-per-completed-ticket question</details> |
| 2026-08-05 | Scope build phase 3.1's premise gate so its blocking half asserts resolution up to the CURIE, and carry the full end-to-end assertion as a separately skipped sixteenth case | Making the whole gate depend on the graph tunnel, which is what a strict reading of the phase premise requires; dropping the end-to-end assertion entirely; asserting end to end against a mocked graph | <details><summary>why</summary>The phase premise reads "a question about a gene the system has never been told about resolves to the right gene", and proving that strictly means resolution, then `cypher_query`, then the graph, then an answer. The graph needs an SSH tunnel that cannot be opened from this environment, and both reasons are controls working as designed: the Layer-7 proxy cannot tunnel raw SSH, and `block-bash-delete.sh` blocks `ssh` as an execution wrapper (T-3.0-07, `tracker/BOARD.md:79`). A blocking gate that cannot run is worse than a narrower one that can, since stage 5's whole purpose is to be watched failing before code exists. Mocking the graph was rejected outright because it reproduces build phase 2.1's exact failure, where 879 green tests all mocked the model call and therefore could not see a generation defect. So cases 1 to 15 need NCBI and not the graph, and case 16 carries the full premise behind its own reachability skip, runs with no edit the moment the tunnel is up, and the resulting hole is stated in the gate's own docstring rather than left to be discovered: a green blocking half proves TP53 resolves, never that a TP53 question is answered well</details> |
| 2026-08-05 | Give `ncbi_efetch` the signature `ncbi_efetch(tool_input)` with no `harness` parameter, breaking symmetry with `cypher_query(harness, tool_input)` | Matching `cypher_query`'s two-argument signature for consistency; passing the harness so the tool can enforce its own timeout | <details><summary>why</summary>`cypher_query` takes a harness because it generates Cypher with a model call, and the harness is what owns model identity and per-step budget. `ncbi_efetch` makes no model call: it is a deterministic HTTP client across three API families. The Act step already wraps every tool invocation in `harness.enforce_timeout`, and the tool carries its own 15-second per-call budget internally per Section 6.2, so the parameter would be unused. Adding it to preserve a shape would be cargo-culting a dependency the tool does not have, and an unused parameter in a tool signature is the kind of thing a later reader treats as meaningful. Consistency is worth having where it reflects a real shared requirement, and this is not one</details> |
| 2026-08-05 | Have the premise gate build tool inputs through `NcbiEfetchInput.model_validate` on plain dicts taken from the specification, rather than through per-action constructors | Constructing each of the seven action models directly; defining the discriminated union in the gate; having the gate call an internal transport function | <details><summary>why</summary>The gate is written before the tool exists, so anything it imports is a design it imposes on the builders rather than a contract it tests. Section 6.2 documents the input as a JSON shape discriminated on `action`, and that shape is what a caller actually sends. Validating a dict pins exactly that and leaves the internal structure of the union, whether it is a Pydantic discriminated union, seven sibling models, or something else, as a builder's decision. This keeps stage 5 doing its job, which is to state what must be true, without doing stage 6's job of deciding how</details> |
| 2026-08-05 | Add T-3.1-13 for F-2.1-B10 and fold the candidate filter and the async ripple explicitly into T-3.1-11, rather than letting all three ride as unnamed obligations of "real gene-symbol resolution" | Leaving the twelve-ticket decomposition as cut on 2026-08-04; deferring F-2.1-B10 to a later phase; filing the candidate filter as a follow-up after the resolution change lands | <details><summary>why</summary>A research pass before builder dispatch found three obligations with no owner inside a ticket map that looked complete. F-2.1-B10 does not close when F-2.1-07 closes, because perfect resolution still leaves typos, non-human genes and disease names typed where a symbol was expected, and those reach the graph as an unbound parameter and surface as "the graph query failed" when the truth is the system never recognized the entity. The candidate filter cannot be a follow-up, because the ticket that would create the follow-up is the same ticket that arms the defect: `_GENE_SYMBOL_TOKEN_PATTERN` matched against `query_text.upper()` fires on every word, and it is harmless today only because the lookup behind it is a one-entry dict. That is LEARNINGS.md row 38 exactly, a dormant limitation being a scheduled defect whose dormancy depends on another bug. The async ripple was named because it touches two production call sites and six test call sites across four files including build phase 2.1's own premise gate, which is the kind of blast radius a builder discovers halfway through rather than plans for</details> |
| 2026-08-05 | Repartition build phase 3.1's action tickets across four modules split by API family, rather than letting seven tickets all write `tools/ncbi_efetch.py` | Keeping the one-file layout and serializing the seven action tickets; keeping the one-file layout and relying on worktree isolation to make concurrent writes safe; one module per action, giving seven modules | <details><summary>why</summary>The ticket map cut on 2026-08-04 named `tools/ncbi_efetch.py` as the file for T-3.1-04 through T-3.1-10, which is seven builders aimed at one file. `bossman-mode`'s own rule is that worktree isolation makes concurrent writes safe without making an overlapping decomposition correct, and that the partition comes first. Serializing the seven would have cost the phase its parallelism for a layout choice nobody had defended. The seam chosen is the one the tool actually has and the one the premise gate already tests: E-utilities decides ok versus empty versus error by inspecting the response body while ignoring an HTTP 200, and Datasets v2 and PubChem branch on status directly. Splitting there puts the two conventions in different files, which makes the trap `Tool_implementation_mechanics.md` records at lines 117 to 122 structurally hard to commit rather than merely documented, since error handling written once can no longer accidentally span both families. Resulting layout: `ncbi_transport.py` (shared HTTP, timeout, retry, throttle), `ncbi_efetch_schemas.py` (the discriminated input and the single output shape), `ncbi_eutils_actions.py` (search, summary, fetch, link, coordinate_overlap, the body-inspecting family), `ncbi_datasets_actions.py` and `ncbi_pubchem_actions.py` (the two status-branching families), and `ncbi_efetch.py` reduced to the dispatcher. One module per action was rejected as the opposite error, seven files with no seam between them</details> |
| 2026-08-05 | On `write_node`'s refusal-branch fallback-link build, reuse `plan_node`'s already-resolved `target_entities` off `state["tool_calls"]` instead of re-calling entity resolution | Leaving the existing `resolved = _extract_target_entities(query.text)` call in place and just awaiting it; re-resolving with a shorter internal timeout; caching only inside `resolve_symbol_to_curie` and accepting the second call as a cache hit | <details><summary>why</summary>T-3.1-11 turned `_extract_target_entities` from a free, deterministic function into a live NCBI call. Before that change, re-running it in `write_node` to build a refusal's fallback link cost nothing; after it, every refusal would pay a second network round trip and its latency purely to construct a link, when `plan_node` already computed the identical result earlier in the same request. Every branch that reaches this line already ran `plan_node` (`cap_exceeded` and `step_error` both return earlier, and the new `unresolved_entity_symbols` early exit does too), so `state["tool_calls"][0].cypher_input.target_entities` is always the same value a second resolution would produce, just without the second call. Relying on the in-process `_SYMBOL_CURIE_CACHE` to make that second call cheap was rejected: it still spends an `await` and a network round trip on a cache miss (a symbol never seen before this request), and a refusal path is exactly the wrong place to add a network dependency for something already known</details> |
| 2026-08-05 | Refuse a query whose only candidate entity fails live resolution before `act_node` ever dispatches a tool call, rather than letting an empty `target_entities` list reach `cypher_query` as before | Leaving the existing behavior (empty `target_entities` reaches `cypher_query`, which either declines to generate or lets the model invent an unbound parameter); adding the check inside `cypher_query` itself; downgrading the trust signal instead of refusing outright | <details><summary>why</summary>T-3.1-13/F-2.1-B10: today an unresolved symbol still reaches `cypher_query`, the model still writes Cypher referencing an unbound parameter, and AGE fails with `UndefinedParameter` after two model calls and roughly 21.7 seconds, surfaced as "the graph query failed", which is never true since the graph was never the thing that broke. Fixing this inside `cypher_query` was rejected because the tool has no way to distinguish "no entity was ever found" (answerable without one, for example a disease named in plain English) from "an entity was found and confirmed absent" (not answerable) without `core.graph` telling it which case this is, and that distinction only exists at the resolution layer. `_select_planned_tool_call` returning a distinct `_UnresolvedEntityRefusal` when at least one candidate was tried and failed, with none other rescuing the query, lets `plan_node` refuse immediately: zero synth calls, zero graph calls, and a refusal message ("I could not identify that gene") that names the truth instead of blaming a component that never ran</details> |
| 2026-08-05 | Use a per-call `httpx.AsyncClient` in `ncbi_transport.py` rather than one module-level singleton shared across the process | A module-level `httpx.AsyncClient` singleton, reused across every call for connection pooling; a singleton created lazily on first call and cached in a global | <details><summary>why</summary>A module-level singleton binds to the event loop that creates it, and this repo's test suite, its premise gate, and its production ASGI server can each start a new event loop, so a singleton created under one loop raises on first use under another. Trading connection reuse for a client that structurally cannot outlive its event loop removes a whole class of environment-dependent flake for a tool bounded at 15 seconds per call by `.claude/rules/tool-call-budgets.md`, where interactive latency matters and sustained throughput does not. The cost is one new TCP and TLS handshake per call rather than a warm pool</details> |
| 2026-08-05 | Add a session-scoped autouse pytest fixture in `tests/conftest.py` that hard-fails any outbound HTTP request in the unit suite, exempting only the premise gate | Leaving the unit suite free to make live calls and catching drift by manual review; mocking `httpx` per test file as each test is written; a lint rule scanning test files for a missing mock | <details><summary>why</summary>A judge round proved with an `httpx` spy that `tests/.../adapters/web_sse/test_streaming_endpoints.py:146` made a live NCBI call, and the suite's green depended on NCBI being up, which caused one spurious failure. Manual review and per-file mocking both depend on someone noticing a missing mock, the same shape of control `goal-contracts` and `ai-security-standards` already reject for the security hooks: a control that depends on remembering is not a control. One autouse guard covering the whole unit suite makes a missing mock fail loudly at the point it is written, rather than intermittently whenever NCBI happens to be slow or down. The premise gate is the one deliberate exemption, since it exists precisely to hit real NCBI endpoints per `tool-call-budgets.md` and would fail its own purpose if blocked</details> |
| 2026-08-05 | Merge build phase 3.1 as PR #22 without running the adversarial pass over its own fix round, which was the stated pre-merge condition | Holding the pull request unmerged until a primary-provider session could re-review the twenty-six fixes; running a review on the metered backend and treating it as binding | <details><summary>why</summary>The product owner's call, recorded rather than hidden. Merging keeps the tool roster moving; the cost is that twenty-six fixes are landed on the fixer's word. Tracked as the `3.1 re-review outstanding` flag on `tracker/BOARD.md` rather than treated as done</details> |
| 2026-08-05 | Order the next primary-provider session as re-review 3.1, then the F-2.1-C15 generation bound, then open build phase 3.2 | Opening 3.2 first and carrying both the unverified fixes and C15 forward; doing C15 first because it is the older dated commitment | <details><summary>why</summary>The re-review is the only thing that converts twenty-six `fix-landed` findings into closed ones, and six of them were filed critical, including a cite-or-refuse breach where `summary` returned a schema-valid citation for a record that does not exist. | 2026-08-05 | Give `tracker/phase_N.M.md` findings a three-state vocabulary, `fix-landed`, `carried` and `closed`, replacing the single word `open` | Leaving one `open` state and relying on each finding's prose detail section; marking fixed findings `closed` at fix time | <details><summary>why</summary>One word was carrying two different situations, genuinely not fixed and fixed by a commit nobody has independently checked, and a reader could not tell them apart from the table. The distinction is load-bearing here because commit 59944bc landed 669 insertions and 32 tests while touching the ledger by only seven lines, so every per-finding detail section still read `open, unfixed` and was stale. `closed` stays reserved for an independent confirmation, preserving the finder-is-never-the-closer rule</details> |
| 2026-08-07 | Verify a resolved gene's own official symbol against the searched term before trusting a single-candidate match, on both the Datasets and ESearch resolution paths | Trusting a single non-ambiguous id as sufficient confirmation, matching the existing `len(idlist) != 1` ambiguity guard's own logic; restricting the fix to whichever path an adversary happened to reproduce it on | <details><summary>why</summary>Live-verified that NCBI's `[sym]`-tagged ESearch and the Datasets `gene/symbol/{symbol}/taxon/{taxon}` endpoint both match on gene aliases and synonyms, not only the approved symbol, so a single-id "unambiguous" match can still be a confidently WRONG gene (`HG38[sym]` resolves to one id whose real symbol is `LGR5`; same shape for `MRI`->`CYREN`, `CAN`->`NUP214`, `ALL`->`BCR`). The existing ambiguity guard catches multiple candidates, never one confidently wrong one, and this is the fabricated-citation shape the whole cite-or-refuse gate exists to prevent, one layer upstream of where F-2.1-B10 looked. Fixed on both paths since both share the same underlying alias-matching behavior; fixing only the one an adversary happened to probe would have left the other silently vulnerable</details> |
| 2026-08-07 | Supplement EInfo's live field list with a small, explicitly-labeled allowlist for tags known to work despite EInfo's silence on them, rather than trusting EInfo's fieldlist alone or dropping field-tag validation | Validating strictly against EInfo's fieldlist once its list/dict parsing bug was fixed; removing field-tag validation and trusting whatever NCBI's live query parser accepts | <details><summary>why</summary>Fixing the parsing bug alone would have made things worse: EInfo's own `db=gene` fieldlist has no entry whose abbreviation is `sym`, yet `BRCA1[sym]` is real, working, live-verified Entrez syntax and this module's own canonical example throughout its code and tests. Strict EInfo-only validation would have started rejecting the one tag this codebase actually depends on, the same "no safe direction of failure" mistake build phase 3.0's guardrail premise gate exists to catch, one layer down at field tags instead of admission. The allowlist is seeded with exactly one entry, `sym`, not a general attempt to enumerate every tag EInfo omits</details> |
| 2026-08-07 | Dispatch one more independent fresh-context reviewer against the full PR before merging, rather than merging on the strength of same-session self-verification or requiring a full human review | Merging directly once every fix carried its own live reproduction; holding the PR for a human reviewer | <details><summary>why</summary>Same-session self-checks had already missed something real three times in the same day: the original merged commit shipped two critical regressions past its own author, six parallel builders and one integration pass missed the gene-alias soundness gap that a fresh pass then caught, and two of the lead's own individual fixes (`retry_after`, the wait-budget tracking) each had a bug on the first attempt, caught only by a test written against a fresh read of the requirement rather than by the same session that wrote the fix. A full human review was judged not worth the cost for a solo project with no live users yet; one more cheap, independent agent dispatch was judged worth it given the measured same-day hit rate</details> |
| 2026-08-07 | Close F-2.1-C15's generation half with a standalone regex matched directly against the quote-masked query string (`_UNBOUNDED_TRAVERSAL_PATTERN`), rather than a check scoped to `_RELATIONSHIP_HOP_PATTERN`'s captured bracket interior | The hop-scoped version, which a fresh-context review found bypassable via a nested bracket (a list-valued property) alongside a `*range` spec, since `_RELATIONSHIP_HOP_PATTERN`'s non-nesting capture never matches that hop at all; a full nesting-aware relationship-hop parser generalizing `_iter_paren_contents` (F-2.1-A9's fix for node patterns) to relationship hops | <details><summary>why</summary>The standalone pattern closes the bypass without needing to correctly delimit a hop's true closing bracket at all: it anchors on the literal `[` and requires an explicit, wildcard-free sub-pattern before the `*`, so nothing after the `*range` spec, nested bracket included, is ever relevant to whether it matches. A full nesting-aware hop parser would also have closed it, and would additionally fix the same non-nesting blind spot for the pre-existing edge-label validation, but was rejected as more invasive than this ticket's scope: it touches the one relationship-hop-matching primitive every check in this file's hop loop depends on, in a file with an exceptionally long history of findings, reviewed in the same single pass as a critical safety fix. That broader fix, if the non-nesting gap in edge-label validation itself is ever traced to a real incident, is a separate, dedicated ticket, not a scope expansion of this one. Two independent fresh-context reviews confirmed the chosen approach: the first found the hop-scoped version's bypass, the second confirmed the standalone version closes it, found no new bypass, and found no ReDoS risk in the pattern (linear scaling across six adversarial timing inputs, no nested quantifier over the same character class)</details> |
| 2026-08-07 | Reject every Cypher variable-length relationship form outright (`*`, `*n`, `*n..m`, `*n..`, `*..m`), rather than only an unbounded one (bare `*` or a range with no upper number) | Distinguishing a "safe" bounded range from an unbounded one and rejecting only the latter; adding an anchor-requirement rule instead (rejecting a MATCH pattern with no node bound to a caller parameter), which was the other candidate mechanism considered for F-2.1-C15 | <details><summary>why</summary>`schema_slice.py`'s fixed-hop design expresses every multi-hop question as chained, explicitly-typed MATCH clauses and never asks for a variable-length range of any kind, so this system has no legitimate query shape to calibrate a bound threshold against, and no finding traces a bounded range to any incident; rejecting the whole construct is both simpler and has no legitimate case to false-positive against. The anchor-requirement alternative was rejected because every query this pipeline generates is already forced through a bound entity parameter before generation runs (`_run_pipeline` refuses outright with no entity extracted, F-2.1-B10), so an unanchored top-level scan is not a shape this system can currently produce at all; a rule guarding against a shape that cannot occur would be unjustified by any traced failure mode, the same over-reach shape the reverted first attempt at this ticket is the cautionary tale for</details> |
| 2026-08-07 | Leave F-2.2-01 (a generation-side flake, roughly 1 run in 10, where a missing-parentheses Cypher shape reaches the graph and gets no retry) open on `fix/c15-generation-bound`, rather than adding a retry in the same branch | Adding a retry on a graph-side parse rejection in `cypher_query.py`'s `_run_pipeline`, which its own ticket's acceptance criteria allowed as the other valid disposition | <details><summary>why</summary>The ticket's acceptance criteria explicitly named both options as equally valid. A retry would touch `_run_pipeline`'s error-handling path, a function with a documented history of regressions, in the same review pass as a critical safety fix, which is the composition risk `self-eval-loop`'s "review a fix harder than new code" section warns against, applied to scope rather than to a single function. Cannot be re-measured live from this environment either way, since it needs the same graph tunnel T-3.0-07 cannot reach</details> |
| 2026-08-08 | In `ncbi_dbsnp`, substitute the live-working `/spdi/{spdi}/contextual` endpoint for Section 6.3's named `spdi/{spdi}/canonical_representative`, which is confirmed broken server-side | Retrying or waiting out `canonical_representative` as a transient fault; implementing client-side canonicalization instead of calling Variation Services at all; calling `/spdi/{spdi}/all_equivalent_contextual`, also confirmed broken; blocking the `spdi` query type entirely until NCBI fixes the endpoint | <details><summary>why</summary>Live pre-build probing returned HTTP 500 on every well-formed input tried against `canonical_representative`, including NCBI's own documented example, so this is a server-side defect, not a client request-shape issue. `/contextual` is a live-working sibling endpoint that performs genuine SPDI normalization, confirmed self-consistent across multiple distinct real SPDIs by the adversary round, but the premise gate's `spdi` coverage is error-path only, so the substitution is unverified beyond not crashing and correctly rejecting malformed input. Blocking the query type outright was rejected as strictly worse than a working, if unproven-equivalent, substitute. Filed as F-3.2-03 and carried to the Step 6.2 reconciliation of Section 6.3 rather than silently absorbed, since editing the locked technical specification is not this ticket's call</details> |

All three map cleanly onto existing tools, so the answers, sources, timings and layout are byte-identical and only the labels moved; cutting the calls would have lost the genotype table and the inheritance model, which are the strongest parts of that answer</details> |
| 2026-08-12 | Give the prototype's four canned questions deliberately different answer depths rather than making them uniform: ALS sparse (1 call, 1 source, plain prose), BRCA1 and GCK medium (5 calls, 6 and 5 sources, sub-headings, one compact table on GCK), rs334 full (7 calls, 12 sources, large table, cross-layer conflict callout) | Expanding all four to the depth of the rs334 answer; leaving the original uneven spread of 3, 12, 2 and 1 sources, which was accidental rather than designed | <details><summary>why</summary>A demo that shows only deep answers misrepresents the product, since most real questions are not seven-call questions, and it leaves the sparse case visually untested. Every identifier used is a real NCBI or OMIM record (Gene 672, Gene 2645, MedGen C0677776, OMIM 617883, OMIM 125851, OMIM 602485), and counts are phrased as of the query rather than as fixed figures, since they move</details> |
| 2026-08-13 | Label the source card's provenance field `TOOL` (the tool that produced the row) rather than `EDGE` (the graph relationship), a deliberate departure from the design system's source-card component | Follow the design card exactly and label it `EDGE`; show both fields | <details><summary>why</summary>The design card was drawn for a Layer 1 graph edge, where `EDGE` is the honest name. Second product-owner-directed exception to Section 25's locked order, after build phase 4.8's own insertion on 2026-08-11</details> |
| 2026-08-14 | Fix the `inkFaint` contrast defect (F-4.8-D-08) in the design system itself rather than continuing to patch it per call site in code | Keep stepping the token up to `inkMuted` in code wherever a surface makes it fail | <details><summary>why</summary>`--ink-faint` (#71767A) passes AA on `--surface` at 4.73:1 and fails on both `surfaceSunk` (4.31:1) and `--l1-wash` (3.92:1, measured by axe). The code keeps its local step-ups until the design change lands</details> |
| 2026-08-14 | Bring the landing's search action and depth control to the prototype now rather than bundling them with the answer-screen fidelity pass | Schedule them with F-4.8-D-01 to D-05 | <details><summary>why</summary>They are on the first screen every visitor sees, and the change is contained. The "Ask" label was itself a deviation taken for a real reason (F-4.8-L-03), and the correct remedy was on the accessible name, not the visible one</details> |
| 2026-08-14 | Schedule the five answer-screen fidelity gaps (F-4.8-D-01 to D-05) as their own named pass, build phase 4.9, rather than folding them into unrelated phases or leaving them undated | Fold each into whichever phase next touches the answer screen; or keep them as undated open flags | <details><summary>why</summary>An undated flag with no owner is how F-2.0-15 survived twelve phases, which `LEARNINGS.md` already records. | 2026-08-14 | Delete the eight orphaned build-phase-1.2 modules and their eight test files rather than keeping them for reference | Keep them, or move them to an `attic/` directory | <details><summary>why</summary>36 tests were exercising code the application no longer renders, inflating the suite's number into a claim it did not support. | 2026-08-14 | Run a judge round and an adversary round on build phase 4.9 BEFORE merging, then a third round on the fix round itself | Merge on the strength of the lead's own gate and review afterwards | <details><summary>why</summary>The lead found three real defects after all twelve gate clauses were green, which is the condition `self-eval-loop` names: a same-session self-check is not an independent review. | 2026-08-14 | Merge build phase 4.9 after three review rounds rather than running a fourth | Keep going until a round comes back clean | <details><summary>why</summary>Rounds two and three each found real defects in the previous round's fix, so a fourth would likely find something too. The criticals are closed and every remaining finding has a named owner in `tracker/phase_4.9.md`. Build phase 4.10's own rounds cover the carried items. Product owner's call</details> |
| 2026-08-14 | Give review agents their own git worktree rather than sharing the lead's working tree | Keep dispatching them into the shared tree | <details><summary>why</summary>Measured the hazard live: the adversary left the tree checked out on `develop`, and the judge mutated source files while the lead was running tests, so the lead read failures that were the judge's mutations rather than real defects. | 2026-08-14 | Fix the design system's contrast and focus-nesting defects at source, and add a permanent axe gate over all 21 cards | Keep deviating per call site and filing each one | <details><summary>why</summary>Three deviations in two build phases for one underlying cause. | 2026-08-15 | Shrink CLAUDE.md's Priority-2 build cell from 19,063 characters to 1,326, keeping the verified counts and replacing the accumulated narrative with a pointer to each fact's owner | Leave it, or split CLAUDE.md into several files | <details><summary>why</summary>The cell was 56 percent of a file loaded into EVERY turn, grown by accretion across twenty build phases each appending its own history.
