| 2026-08-15 | Collapse each row's long column behind its own dropdown in DECISIONS.md and LEARNINGS.md, rather than grouping rows into collapsible months | Month-level grouping (tried first and replaced), thematic grouping, or leave the tables plain | <details><summary>why</summary>Month grouping did not solve the problem it was meant to: expanding August still gave 130 rows of 500 to 900 characters. Per-row collapse makes the table scan one line at a time. Chronological order is preserved either way, which matters because both files are append-only and a thematic regrouping would reorder the record. The tradeoff is real and is written into both files rather than hidden: a collapsed block is invisible to find-in-page, so the theme index exists to narrow the search first</details> |
| 2026-08-15 | Enforce the append-only table format with a check in `check_doc_drift.py` rather than relying on the format written into `decision-logging.md` and the `learnings` skill | Leave it documented, or add a git pre-commit hook | <details><summary>why</summary>CLAUDE.md already states the principle this repository runs on: the hooks are the only structural enforcement and everything else is an instruction a model chooses to obey. A documented format had just failed twice in one day, once by an append that would omit the wrapper and once by a blank line that silently truncated the rendered table while every source-level check passed. The check is mutation-tested against all four failure shapes and immediately caught a stray row left behind by its own mutation run. A git pre-commit hook was not chosen because `.git/hooks` is untracked, so it would not travel with the repository</details> |
| 2026-08-15 | Bound anonymous denial of service by each SOURCE's share of the day (a tenth, derived from `ANON_DAILY_RUN_CAP`), after three rounds of per-caller bounds were each defeated | A per-principal cap (round 1), a per-identity attempt ceiling (round 3), a tighter mint throttle, proof-of-work on minting, or accepting the exposure and deferring to build phase 6.0 | <details><summary>why</summary>Every earlier bound was keyed on something the caller can mint more of, so each was defeated by minting more: per-principal fell to 40 mints at 157 pipelines/second, and the per-identity attempt ceiling of 10 fell to 20 identities taking the whole 200-run day in 1.56 seconds, which was the same outcome and nearly the same wall time as the defect it was built to fix. Tightening the mint throttle is structurally closed off: refusing 20 mints needs a per-window limit below the 25 consecutive mints the premise gate's admit arm requires from one shared office address, and that arm is not negotiable because shared NAT is exactly where this product gets demonstrated. A share of the day is the first bound minting does not increase: 20 identities or 200 buy the same 20 runs. Measured after: 200 accepted pipelines fell to 20, a visitor at a different source went from 429 to 202, and four office visitors at five searches each still complete. Derived as a fraction rather than configured separately so the two numbers cannot drift into a state where the share IS the day, which is the F-4.10-R-11 failure one level up. Residual, declared in the gate's own coverage statement rather than buried: a caller with genuinely many source addresses gets one share per address and is bounded only by the day again, and the money was always bounded by that day regardless.</details> |
| 2026-08-15 | Refund a guest's ANSWER for a guardrail refusal or a fatal error, never the ATTEMPT, and refund the shared day only when no model call was made | Charge for everything (the pre-4.10 behaviour), refund both counters, or refund nothing on failure | <details><summary>why</summary>Product-owner decision that a visitor should not be charged for a question the guardrail refused. Refunding only the answer is what keeps that true without reopening the hole: the attempt ceiling is the per-identity bound, so giving attempts back leaves a refusal loop unbounded, which is precisely how F-4.10-R-01 turned the first version of this refund into a worse denial of service than the one it closed. The shared day is refunded only for a free pre-filter refusal, because a refusal that reached the classifier really did pay for a Guard-tier call, and making every refusal free on both counters would turn unlimited garbage into free compute. Stopped runs are deliberately excluded: every token was already streamed, so refunding one would let a caller read an answer to its last sentence, stop before `done`, and get the search back.</details> |
| 2026-08-15 | Give an anonymous visitor a signed guest token minted on first ask, accepting that clearing it yields a fresh allowance | Device or browser fingerprinting, an account-only product, or a server-side cookie session | <details><summary>why</summary>Product-owner decision taken with a prototype mindset: the point of the phase is that someone can try the product without an account, and every alternative either adds friction to that exact moment or tracks people. The clearability is accepted, not overlooked, and the premise gate asserts it as known behaviour so a later reader finds the decision attached rather than filing it as a bug. What that acceptance did NOT cover, and what the adversary round had to establish separately, is a script minting identities in parallel: one person clearing storage and a loop doing it differ by a measured 157 paid pipelines per second, and conflating them is how the phase's worst finding got waved through in a code comment.</details> |
| 2026-08-15 | Keep the guest token across sign-out rather than clearing it, and record a browser-local marker when a guest identity is migrated into an account | Clear the token on sign-out (the shipped behaviour), or clear it and accept the fresh allowance as covered by the clearable-token tradeoff | <details><summary>why</summary>Signing out is an ordinary button, not an act of clearing anything, so handing out five fresh searches for pressing it made the accepted tradeoff reachable without the effort that justified accepting it. Keeping the token means a visitor returns to the identity they had, with whatever remained. The marker is needed because a migrated identity is revoked server-side and must not be resurrected, so the browser has to remember it converted one and go straight to the sign-in wall rather than presenting a dead credential. That in turn forced a backend change: a revoked session and an expired token were both bare 401s, and the client must re-mint on one and must not on the other.</details> |
| 2026-08-15 | Set ANON_DAILY_RUN_CAP to 200 runs per UTC day as the shipped default | 50/day, 1000/day, or no system-wide ceiling at all | <details><summary>why</summary>Product-owner decision. Roughly forty visitors getting their full five searches: enough for a demo day or a link shared with a team, small enough that a bad day costs little. It is the only bound on TOTAL anonymous spend, because the two pre-existing cost caps cannot reach a caller with no account, so the number is the real ceiling on what strangers can spend rather than a soft target. Raising it is one environment variable and no code change, which is the property that made a conservative starting value cheap to choose.</details> |
| 2026-08-15 | Fold F-3.1-41 (acronym-shaped gene symbols) and F-3.1-42 (lowercase gene mentions) into build phase 4.7's entity-resolution design rather than deciding them against today's heuristic | Decide both now against the shipped stopword list, or keep carrying them undecided | <details><summary>why</summary>Both questions are about the hand-maintained stopword list in `core/graph.py`'s gene-candidate extractor, and that extractor exists only because `think_node` is a build-phase-2.0 stub. Build phase 4.7 owns F-2.0-15 and replaces the whole mechanism with real query-intent classification and entity resolution, so answering now means designing the behaviour twice and discarding one. The code's own comment already calls the list "a filter at the wrong layer: it can only ever be as complete as the last defect someone noticed," which is an argument against tuning it further rather than for it. Verified before deciding that the list really does sit in the path 4.7 replaces, rather than assuming it.</details> |
| 2026-08-15 | Report `risk_tier` as `unknown` on the MCP surface's silent-fallback branch instead of the hardcoded `low` (F-4.1-J3-02) | Leave it to build phase 6.1, or omit the field entirely in that branch | <details><summary>why</summary>A hardcoded `low` states a safety judgement the system never made, to a caller that is usually another program and will act on it. That is the manufactured-assertion failure this project's trust rules exist to prevent, and it is worse than silence because it is actionable. A new enum value is additive and therefore v1-legal, where omitting the field would change a required field's presence and needs v2. The sibling half of this finding was already closed so a zero-citation answer cannot call itself grounded; this completes it.</details> |
| 2026-08-15 | Mark which parts of an MCP response are relayed third-party source text rather than the system's own words (F-4.1-A-10), scheduled at build phase 6.1 | Fix before build phase 4.2 while the surface has no consumers, or accept and document the limitation | <details><summary>why</summary>A PubMed abstract reaching an MCP caller byte-identical to the system's own narrative is a prompt-injection path into somebody else's agent, which is why it outranks its apparent size. It is not urgent today because nothing external consumes that surface, so 6.1's hardening pass is the honest home rather than an interruption to the delivery-surface sequence. Documenting the limitation instead was rejected: it moves the burden onto a consumer who has no way to act on it mid-response.</details> |
| 2026-08-15 | Close the `ncbi_dbsnp` rsid-shape residual as correct behaviour rather than fixing or carrying it | Warn when the digits also match a plausible gene id, or keep it open | <details><summary>why</summary>`rs3043` is a well-formed, real variant identifier, so fetching it is right. The tool cannot know a caller meant gene 3043, and the output already names which variant it actually fetched, which is the disclosure that matters. A warning keyed on the digits being a plausible gene id would fire on legitimate variant lookups and guesses at intent the system does not have. Closing it keeps the open-flag list a list of real problems rather than a permanent row describing correct behaviour.</details> |
| 2026-08-15 | Adopt one rule for withheld or shortened values: if the system drops or truncates anything, it discloses that it did (closes F-3.3-J-04 and F-3.5-10) | Disclose only where the dropped value carries a claim, or leave each tool's behaviour as its own author chose | <details><summary>why</summary>Three tools built across two phases behave three ways: `litvar2_lookup` discloses every withholding, `pubtator_annotate` drops silently, `clinicaltrials_search` truncates a display field silently. Each is defensible in isolation, which is exactly why a rule is needed rather than another case-by-case judgement: the per-tool judgement is where the inconsistency came from. The claim-versus-display distinction was rejected as the rule because it requires that same judgement on every new tool, and a silently dropped clinical vocabulary term is precisely the case where a reader cannot tell anything is missing.</details> |
| 2026-08-15 | Move the off-host citation warning outside the collapsed source disclosure (F-4.9-A-09) | Put it one level deep rather than two, or leave it nested | <details><summary>why</summary>Build phase 4.9's source collapse was right for the rest of the card and made this warning opt-in as a side effect. The person a warning about an off-site link protects is precisely the person who would not think to open two disclosures to look for it, so nesting it inverts who it reaches. A mitigation nobody sees is close to no mitigation, which is the same reasoning that makes a silent withholding unacceptable in the row above.</details> |
| 2026-08-15 | Have the client wait for the backend's `cancelled` event before closing the stream, so a stopped run gets a terminal signal (F-4.9-A-08) | Show a local stopped state immediately without waiting, or defer to a later UI phase | <details><summary>why</summary>The backend already emits a purpose-built `cancelled` event and the client aborts before it can arrive, so the signal exists and is being discarded. That makes this a wiring fix rather than a new capability. Asserting a stopped state locally was rejected on principle: it is the page claiming something it has not confirmed, which is the same class as the fabricated answer build phase 4.8's judge round filed as its first critical. A stop control that gives no confirmation teaches a user it did not work.</details> |
| 2026-08-15 | Render five dots from a local default before a guest identity exists, switching to the server's real count on the first question (F-4.10-A-13) | Mint the guest identity on page load so the count is real immediately, or leave the dots hidden until after the first search | <details><summary>why</summary>The free-search offer is the reason a stranger tries the product at all, so the thing advertising it cannot be invisible until the offer has been partly taken. Minting on page load was rejected because it writes a database row for every visitor who bounces, including bots, which is the cost the deliberate laziness avoids. The residual risk is that the dots briefly show a number the server has not confirmed, so the switch to the real count on the first ask is not optional: an unconfirmed number that never gets corrected is the client-side counter this phase just removed.</details> |
| 2026-08-15 | Raise the `clinical_significance` item cap from 40 to 64 characters, in both the locked specification and the tool schema | Keep 40 and rely on the existing withheld-field disclosure, or drop the cap entirely | <details><summary>why</summary>40 rejected standard ClinVar vocabulary, measured at build phase 3.2 against a live 800-record clinical sample at roughly 10 percent of real terms, including `conflicting-interpretations-of-pathogenicity` (44) and `no-classifications-from-unflagged-records` (41). A cap that refuses a tenth of the real vocabulary is not bounding a blast radius, it is withholding facts a clinician needs, and the reader sees a withheld-field note instead of the term about one time in ten. Dropping the cap was rejected: the multi-agent pipeline gate requires a bound on every string field, and 65 is still refused. The existing `fields_withheld` disclosure stays, so the honesty guarantee is unchanged and the cap becomes defence in depth against absurd input rather than the primary control. The test that pinned 40 was re-pinned to 64 and STRENGTHENED to assert the two real terms validate, so a future tightening cannot pass review by looking like a reasonable smaller number.</details> |
| 2026-08-15 | Authorize factual corrections to the locked technical specification as they are found, each logged as a decision, rather than batching them into a second reconciliation window | Schedule a Step 6.2b, or fold spec corrections into build phase 6.1's hardening pass | <details><summary>why</summary>Step 6.2 was the only sanctioned window for editing the locked documents and it closed on 2026-08-10, which left every spec-conformance finding pointing at a finished event. Five flags sat that way, ownerless in practice, which is the exact failure this repository measured when the `release-workflow` mandate went six phases unenforced. The authorization is deliberately narrow: it covers text that is demonstrably untrue about already-shipped code, never a design change, a scope change, or anything on the v1 out-of-scope list, and each correction is logged. Folding them into 6.1 was rejected because it puts documentation work inside a phase scoped for security and release hardening, which is where items get dropped when that phase runs long.</details> |
| 2026-08-15 | Correct two factual errors in the technical specification under that authorization: Section 6.3's SPDI endpoint and Section 25's build-order line for phase 3.2 | Leave both, since the code already routes around them | <details><summary>why</summary>Section 6.3 named `spdi/{spdi}/canonical_representative`, which build phase 3.2 live-confirmed returns HTTP 500 on every well-formed input tried, including NCBI's own documented example; the shipped tool has always used `/contextual`, so the spec text was the thing that was wrong. Section 25's line for build phase 3.2 also listed the dbVar coordinate-overlap sub-tool, which had already shipped in 3.1. Leaving both was rejected for the same reason a stale comment is a liability: a specification that describes an endpoint returning 500 as the one to use is worse than no specification there, because the next reader trusts it.</details> |
| 2026-08-16 | Build phase 4.2: authenticate the CLI with the caller's own bearer JWT rather than the bearer API key Section 13.3 names | Building an API-key entity, meaning a table, an issuance route, and a per-key cost bucket | <details><summary>why</summary>No API-key entity exists anywhere in the codebase, verified by direct read. Build phase 4.1 hit the identical line in Section 13.2 and made the same reading, recorded in `tracker/phase_4.1.md`. Building one mid-phase would be new architecture, which is exactly what the scope rule exists to stop, and no fast-follow trigger names it. The caller's "own bearer API key" is read as the caller's own account authenticating with the bearer-JWT mechanism every other surface already uses.</details> |
| 2026-08-16 | Build phase 4.2: ship `s3 login` even though Section 13.3's command table does not list it | Requiring the user to hand-write a token into `~/.system3/credentials` | <details><summary>why</summary>Section 13.3's table assumed a pasteable long-lived API key. With only a 15-minute access token and a rotating refresh token available, there is no value a user could paste that keeps working, so a login command is the substitution that makes the stored-credential design in that section function at all. Recorded as a substitution rather than an addition, per `.claude/rules/v1-scope-boundary.md`'s requirement to flag rather than silently substitute.</details> |
| 2026-08-16 | Build phase 4.2: stream the answer live and print the trust label after it, rather than holding the answer back so the label can precede it | Buffering the whole answer so `[answer]`, `[flag]`, `[ask]` or `[refuse]` could print as a true prefix, as Section 13.3's wording asks | <details><summary>why</summary>Section 13.3 asks for two things that cannot both happen. It wants the trust label as "a one-line prefix on the answer body" and it wants `token` events printed "as the growing answer body", live. The answer-level trust outcome is not known until every claim resolves, so a genuine prefix means withholding the entire answer until the run finishes, which destroys the streaming the same section requires and pushes time-to-first-token from under a second to the full run duration. Product-owner decision, 2026-08-16: stream live, label after. The label is emitted on its own line so it is never glued to the last token, and the gate clause now asserts its POSITION rather than merely its presence, so the choice is pinned rather than left to drift. Recorded because the two sentences in Section 13.3 remain in tension and a future reader will otherwise re-litigate it.</details> |
| 2026-08-16 | Build phase 4.2: leave the CLI's status lines without the persona name, and fix it together with the web UI when the event contract next changes | Threading `persona_name` from the `POST /v1/query` response into the renderer for this surface alone, or adding the field to the `think` and `plan` payloads now | <details><summary>why</summary>Section 13.3 asks the CLI's `think` and `plan` lines to be "prefixed with the persona name". No event payload carries one: `persona_name` is returned once at run creation and never repeated, and Section 12.7 already flags the identical gap for the web UI and leaves it for Section 2 or 13 to resolve. Two surfaces now want the same missing field, which makes it a contract question rather than a per-surface fix. Product-owner decision, 2026-08-16: leave it, and close both surfaces together whenever the event contract is next opened. Fixing it here alone would mean designing the plumbing twice and would leave the web UI's flag open regardless. The lines read `[think]` and `[plan]` meanwhile, which is honest rather than wrong.</details> |
| 2026-08-16 | Build phase 4.2: run `finish()` on the Ctrl-C path so an interrupted run still prints its references block | Leaving the interrupt path as an early return, on the grounds that an interrupted run is not a completed answer | <details><summary>why</summary>An interrupted run had been leaving a redirected `answer.txt` holding a pathogenicity claim, a live `[1]` marker, and no source beneath it. That is the same cite-or-refuse failure the phase had already fixed twice on other paths, and the argument that an interrupt is not a completed answer cuts the other way: the user still has the partial answer in a file, so it still needs its sources and a statement that it was cut short. Exit code stays 130, which is the conventional interrupt signal and was already proven correct. Decided by the lead rather than escalated, since it follows directly from the cite-or-refuse rule the product owner has already endorsed as non-negotiable.</details> |
| 2026-08-16 | Fix rounds partition by FILE and run serially within a file, rather than partitioning by finding | Continuing to fan out one agent per finding, which is how build rounds are partitioned and how fix rounds 1 through 4 were run | <details><summary>why</summary>Five consecutive review rounds in build phase 4.2 each found their worst defect inside the previous round's fix, and the cause was the same every time: two agents editing the same file in the same round, each individually correct, neither able to see the other. The clearest instance is round 4, where one agent routed a message through the sanitizer and a second agent added an unsanitized write eleven lines below it, in the same commit. Round 5 was run deliberately as a single agent holding every fix at once and immediately found a sixth unsanitized site that five parallel rounds had walked past. The rule that follows: group findings by the file they land in, give one agent every finding in that file, never split a cross-file finding, and end every fix brief with a sweep instruction rather than a list of reported lines. Parallelism stays the right lever for independent work on disjoint files; it is an active hazard for two findings sharing a seam.</details> |
| 2026-08-17 | Build phase 4.3: the GraphQL surface wraps the one agent core, and does NOT expose the seven tools as resolver fields | A resolver per tool (`gene`, `variant`, `trial`), which is what a GraphQL developer builds by reflex and what most literally delivers the PRD's "nested biomedical data" | <details><summary>why</summary>The locked spec deliberately leaves this surface unspecified, and the PRD contributes one sentence naming "shared tools". Section 13.2 had already ruled on that exact phrase for MCP: a raw per-tool passthrough lets a caller bypass cite-or-refuse and the cost caps entirely, which is the control the one-core, adapters-filter model exists to hold. A per-tool resolver layer would have been a third entry point into the tool layer with no guardrail, no trust signal and no cost cap, reachable by any credential that can reach the schema. Rejected on that basis, not on effort. "Nested biomedical data" is therefore the typed answer graph: a caller selects exactly the fields it wants from a cited answer, its citations, and each citation's provenance.</details> |
| 2026-08-17 | Build phase 4.3: the GraphQL surface requires a registered account, with no guest path | Wiring in `get_caller`, the dependency every REST `/v1/*` route uses, which resolves a guest token as happily as a user token and would have taken one import | <details><summary>why</summary>Both existing programmatic surfaces already work this way: MCP authenticates registered users only and the CLI never holds a guest token. Three reasons, in order of weight. The guest allowance is not one function but an ordering (concurrency precheck, then a four-bound spend, then run creation, plus a refund callback), and the continuation prompt already records that the anonymous path's accepted residual is a caller with many source addresses being bounded only by the day; reproducing that ordering on a second surface doubles a surface with a known residual. A guest token exists so a first-time visitor need not sign up before one question, and a GraphQL client is a developer integration by construction. And the direction of error is safe: refusing a guest is recoverable, letting guest allowance be spent through an unhardened second path is not.</details> |
| 2026-08-17 | Build phase 4.3: the GraphQL surface is request/response only, with no subscriptions | Building a GraphQL subscription over the run registry's `subscribe()`, which is multi-consumer and would have served one | <details><summary>why</summary>Section 13 places GraphQL among the surfaces that do not subscribe to the event stream, and assigns live streaming to the surfaces that already have it. A subscription would be a second live-stream transport with its own abandonment, resumption and cost-filtering semantics to keep in step with the SSE one. The registry's `subscribe()` is still what the resolver consumes internally to build the folded response; nothing else may read a run's events. The surface additionally advertises NO WebSocket protocol, so it does not offer an upgrade path nobody designed or bounded.</details> |
| 2026-08-17 | Build phase 4.3: keep the 403-versus-404 distinction on every surface, rather than collapsing them to hide whether a run exists | Collapsing both answers into one indistinguishable response, which the phase's own premise gate originally demanded of the GraphQL surface | <details><summary>why</summary>Product-owner decision. Answering 404 for an unknown run and 403 for a foreign one does tell a caller which of the two it holds, so it is literally an existence oracle, and the phase's first draft of its gate asserted the opposite property. Two things settle it. Run ids are uuid4, so there is nothing to enumerate and the oracle is worthless in practice. And the two distinct answers are the more actionable ones: "not yours" and "gone" are different problems with different fixes, which `tool-call-budgets.md` requires an error to distinguish. Hardening only the newest surface would also have been drift dressed as improvement, since REST's behavior is asserted by shipped tests and T-1.2-02's acceptance criteria. Revisit only if run ids ever become guessable.</details> |
| 2026-08-17 | Build phase 4.3: enforce a per-request timeout in ASGI middleware around the router, not in a Strawberry schema extension | Keeping the timeout in a `SchemaExtension`, which is where Strawberry documentation and the ticket brief both pointed | <details><summary>why</summary>Measured, not theorized. `asyncio.timeout` inside an extension bounds a request by cancelling the running task. The cancel fired on time, converted to `TimeoutError`, and the handler set a correct short-circuit result, and the request still died with `CancelledError`, because the task that was cancelled is the same task that must then serialize the response. Catching `CancelledError` too, distinguishing by `Timeout.expired()`, distinguishing by wall clock, and calling `Task.uncancel()` were all tried and none worked. The middleware runs the request as a CHILD task under `asyncio.wait_for`, so the cancellation is confined to the child and the parent, never cancelled, writes a clean error. The general form: a task cannot cancel itself and then go on to serve the response, so bound a hung operation from outside it. Mounting the wrapped router instead was tried and reverted, since `app.mount` 307-redirects a bare POST, the same trailing-slash trap already documented for the MCP mount.</details> |
| 2026-08-18 | Cap the autonomous review loop at two rounds, fan out on files but go serial on findings inside one file, and stop mid-round on any regression inside an earlier fix | Continuing to add scrutiny per round; capping at three rounds; keeping one fix agent per finding regardless of file overlap | <details><summary>why</summary>Measured, not theorized. Build phases 2.1 and 4.2 ran five and six review rounds, and in both, every round found its worst defect inside the previous round's fix, so rising scrutiny was demonstrably not the lever: 4.2 saw 40-plus findings across five rounds with no fall in the recurrence rate. The cause is structural. A parallel fix agent is individually correct and blind to the sibling editing the same function, so two correct fixes compose into a defect no reviewer of either one can see. 4.2's round 5 was run deliberately as ONE agent holding every finding at once, closed both assigned findings, and found a sixth unsanitized site on a fallback path that four parallel rounds had walked past. Three rounds was rejected because 4.2's rounds 3 through 5 each introduced the defect the next round found, so that budget would have burned three rounds before reaching the product owner anyway. The escalation is not "still failing": it carries the open findings, a diff summary per fix attempt, any regression links, and options including revert-and-re-decompose. This is `goal-contracts`' blocked-stop applied to review, where a blocked stop is an honest end state.</details> |
| 2026-08-18 | Do not adopt the `/design` canvas skill; the Claude Design plus `DesignSync` mirror stays the only design-to-code path | Adopting `/design` alongside the existing mirror; replacing the mirror with the canvas | <details><summary>why</summary>The existing chain has a code-facing contract and the canvas does not. Values live in `docs/build/design/design-system/foundations/colors.html`, are mirrored one-to-one into 21 component cards via `DesignSync`, transcribed into `frontend/src/theme.ts`, and pinned by premise gates that fail the build on a nudged colour, plus an axe sweep over all 21 cards. `/design` produces `.dc.html` artboards published as a hosted artifact with no token export, no component contract, and no path back into `frontend/`; it has zero footprint in this repository today. Adding it would create a second design source that no gate checks, which is the exact failure already recorded in `LEARNINGS.md` for 2026-08-14, where a token push reached the 21 mirrored cards and left two unmirrored prototype copies stale. It stays available for drafting a screen that does not exist yet, upstream of any card, which is the one job the mirror does not do.</details> |
| 2026-08-18 | The transport preflight passes on ANY HTTP status, probes one endpoint per transport, and reports an unconfigured transport as `skipped` rather than `ok` | Comparing the status against 200; a single probe against the product's model provider, which is what build phase 3.0 shipped; treating an unconfigured transport as a pass; failing the run on a `skipped` result | <details><summary>why</summary>Each choice closes a way this guard could have been vacuous or a false-alarm generator, and all three look like defects to a later reader, so they are recorded rather than left to be "fixed". Any-status: the harness provider answers 404 to a bare HEAD /, which is a live server, and liveness is the only question a dead-connection guard asks. Comparing against 200 would fail permanently on the most expensive transport, and a guard that cries wolf gets ignored and then protects nothing. One probe per transport: the 2026-08-04 correction in `Build_velocity_post_mortem.md` measured the single-probe version failing exactly this way, returning 200 from the product's provider while two researcher agents died against the harness's provider, one closing mid-response and one stalling 600 seconds. `skipped` over `ok`: nothing was verified, and a verified pass is a different claim; the summary keeps them apart so a phase cannot dispatch on an unprobed transport believing it was checked. Mutation-tested rather than trusted for being green, since this repository has eleven recorded assertions that could not fail: four arms driven deliberately, DNS failure and refused connection to exit 1, a blackholed address bounded at 3.09s against a 3s timeout (the 600-second stall class), and a known-open port to prove the passing arm fires at all.</details> |
| 2026-08-19 | Build phase 4.4's KGX export is a query-scoped subgraph export: seed CURIEs, bounded hops, `nodes.tsv` plus `edges.tsv` plus a manifest, read out through the existing read-only Layer 1 path. The `file-protection` rule is amended to permit reading KGX out of the graph while still forbidding writing KGX into it | Serving the upstream full Layer 1 snapshot (roughly 144 GB) as a hosted download and writing no exporter here; building both, with the snapshot deferred to the production track; deferring 4.4 entirely and opening 4.6 or 4.7 instead | <details><summary>why</summary>Section 25 and the PRD scope 4.4 only negatively, "scoped to the existing Hetzner graph, not a full graph export" and "a batch job, not a live adapter", and Section 6 declines to restate the design, so the shape was genuinely undecided at phase open. The full-snapshot option was rejected because the merge pipeline in the data-engineering repository already produces those files, the hosting is infrastructure this repository does not own, and it would leave 4.4 with almost no code while still not satisfying "not a full graph export". Deferring was rejected because 4.4 has no in-repo dependency and Section 25's order resumes here. The rule amendment was necessary rather than optional: `file-protection` forbade "KGX exporters" outright, which forbade a deliverable two locked documents require, so one of the two had to give. The distinction chosen is direction of data flow, writing into the graph stays with Systems 1 and 2, reading a scoped subgraph out is a delivery surface, which keeps the original ban intact for every case it was written for.</details> |
| 2026-08-19 | Authorize a third review round for build phase 4.4, scoped to one defect category only, rather than merging with a reachable major open or reverting the phase | Merging as-is and tracking the output-directory category for build phase 6.1; adding only a cheap guard that refuses a non-empty directory without closing the underlying race; reverting and re-decomposing the export package | <details><summary>why</summary>The review budget is two rounds and it was spent, so continuing needed the product owner rather than the lead, which is exactly the escalation the cap exists to force. The open item was one category wearing three finding numbers: two exports into one directory produced a torn, self-certifying bundle, with no lock, no temp-then-rename and no directory check anywhere in the write path. It was reproduced, and two scheduled batch runs sharing a directory is an ordinary operational mistake, so it was reachable and therefore blocking under the bar set for the phase. Merging and deferring was rejected because the hazard sits in a batch tool a person points at a directory, which is precisely where the mistake gets made. The cheap-guard option was rejected because refusing a non-empty destination does not stop two runs that both start against an empty one, so it would have closed the symptom and left the race. Reverting was rejected as disproportionate: the critical was already fixed and independently verified, and the premise gate was green against the live graph. The third round was therefore authorized narrowly, fenced off from the two modules whose round 2 work was already verified, because re-opening finished code is how this project's phases have historically spiralled.</details> |
| 2026-08-19 | The KGX export refuses a non-empty output directory by default and replaces it wholesale under an explicit override, rather than merging into it | Merging into the directory as before; always replacing without asking; writing into a timestamped subdirectory per run | <details><summary>why</summary>Silently merging is what produced the defect: a reader could see nodes from one run beside edges from another, with a manifest certifying the pair, and stale siblings from a previous export surviving into a new bundle. Timestamped subdirectories were rejected because they change the published contract for every downstream consumer and leave the operator to work out which directory is current. Always replacing without asking was rejected because an export is easy to point at the wrong path and the files it would destroy are not recoverable. Refusing by default makes the common mistake loud at zero cost, and the override keeps the re-run case a single flag away. The refusal is raised before the traversal is paid for, so a wrong path costs one directory check rather than a full graph walk.</details> |
| 2026-08-20 | The build phase 4.5 persona list ships as roughly 30 curated names in a versioned data file, with the drawing mechanism, the storage column and all four caller surfaces built to handle 100 | Curating the full top-100 inside build phase 4.5; building the mechanism against the existing 10-name placeholder and waiting for the product owner to supply the file later | <details><summary>why</summary>Section 14.2 names a curated top-100 list and calls its curation a Phase 6 build task, logged on 2026-07-21, and never produced. Two surfaces are already blocked on it: F-4.2-03, the CLI status prefix that Section 13.3 asks to carry the persona name, and Section 12.7's web UI. Curating 100 names is a research project whose expensive part is the selection judgment rather than the typing, and letting it sit inside a build phase risks the phase becoming that project. Sizing the schema, the draw and the four surfaces for 100 while shipping about 30 makes any later extension a data change to one versioned file rather than a code change, so the full list can land without reopening the phase. Building against the placeholder instead was rejected because it leaves the two blocked surfaces blocked and defers the only genuinely undecided part of the phase past the phase that owns it.</details> |
| 2026-08-20 | The curated persona list contains deceased scientists only | Any scientist living or deceased, the literal reading of "top 100 by contribution"; deceased only with explicit per-subfield quotas | <details><summary>why</summary>The persona name is rendered above generated answers, in the web UI chip, in the CLI status prefix and on the POST /v1/query response. A living scientist's name in that position reads as an association or an endorsement that person never gave, on a product that makes biomedical claims and that will sometimes be wrong. Deceased-only removes that exposure entirely rather than managing it, and it costs only a few names from the current placeholder list. It also fits the register Section 14.2 already fixed for the persona, a small static label that builds connection without undercutting the provenance-forward positioning. Subfield quotas were left out of the basis deliberately: at roughly 30 names the spread can be achieved by curation without a quota table that a later extension to 100 would then have to honor.</details> |
| 2026-08-20 | An answer that omits a retrieved finding attempts one bounded regeneration and, if the omission survives, names what it left out and floors the trust outcome at ask, rather than guaranteeing completeness by constructing the missing sentences in code | Deterministic append of the omitted findings, the literal reading of the structural-completeness decision taken earlier the same day; dropping clinical_brief from v1; leaving the omission silent, which is what the phase started with | <details><summary>why</summary>Build phase 4.5 measured audience depth silently dropping one of four pinned disease associations, fully cited and confidently worded, with nothing in the output announcing the loss. Structural completeness was chosen over prompt tuning after two directive strengthenings failed to hold. The bounded regeneration recovers most omissions but not all, so the remaining question was how far to push. Deterministic append would guarantee the fact set, and it would also generate answer prose in code, in the one path where prose has so far only ever come from the model, while interacting with Section 9.4's marker renumbering. That is the change in this phase most in need of independent adversarial review, and the review loop for it was not available. Regenerate-then-disclose removes the dangerous property, silence, without adding a new risk to the most safety-critical path in the product, and it leaves the guarantee available as a later, separately reviewed change. The premise gate encodes the decided contract rather than the original ambition: an answer must be complete or name what it omitted, and a silent omission still fails at every depth.</details> |
| 2026-08-20 | Session memory is stored as a JSONB column on the existing `sessions` row rather than in a new table or in process | A dedicated `session_memory` table; an in-process dictionary keyed by session id; Redis | <details><summary>why</summary>The summary is exactly one object per session with no independent identity, no history worth querying and nothing to join against, so a table would add a join and a lifecycle for nothing. The in-process option was cheaper and wrong on two counts that are both invisible in a single-process development run and obvious in production: it does not survive a restart, so a conversation loses its context on every redeploy, and it is not shared across workers, so the same session gets different memory depending on which worker answers. Redis is not yet a dependency of this system and adding one to hold a per-session blob that Postgres already has a home for is not justified. The decisive reason is the `sessions` row already carries `user_id`: storing the memory next to its owner is what lets the database answer "is this session yours", which is how F-4.1-A-15 closes. Alembic revision 0007.</details> |
| 2026-08-20 | When resolved entities alone exceed the session-memory token budget, the rendered block carries as many as fit and discloses the count omitted, while the stored summary keeps every one | Returning a block over budget; dropping the excess entities from memory itself; lowering the budget | <details><summary>why</summary>Section 14.3 says resolved entities are never dropped for budget reasons and also makes the token budget hard. Those two collide when the entities alone exceed the budget, and the section does not resolve it. This resolves it by distinguishing the STORED summary from the RENDERED block, which is the distinction the section is really drawing: nothing is dropped from memory, so an entity is still there next turn when the budget may allow it, while the block that goes into a prompt stays under the cap and says how many it left out. Returning an over-budget block was rejected because a cap that is sometimes exceeded is not a cap, and every caller would then have to re-check it. This row exists because a code comment in `build_session_context` already claimed the decision was logged here before it was, which is the same "a comment claiming verification is not verification" defect build phase 4.3 recorded.</details> |
| 2026-08-20 | A caller naming a session that is not theirs gets a stateless turn on READ and a refusal on WRITE, rather than the same answer to both | Refusing both, the first implementation; allowing both; refusing the whole query with a typed error event | <details><summary>why</summary>The two operations can do different amounts of harm, so they get different answers. A read discloses nothing: the ownership check runs before any content is loaded, so the caller simply gets no memory and loses nothing they were entitled to. A write would overwrite another caller's conversation, so it still refuses. Refusing both was the first implementation and was wrong twice over: the refusal escaped `core.run.run` as an unhandled exception, breaking that function's documented never-raises contract, and it failed 27 existing tests by turning a benign session-id collision into a failed query. Refusing the whole query with an error event was rejected as disproportionate to a caller who has simply reused an id.</details> |
| 2026-08-20 | A caller-supplied `session_id` maps to its `sessions.id` row key by uuid5 when it is not already a UUID, rather than by adding a text key column | A new unique text column on `sessions`; rejecting non-UUID session ids at the contract boundary; a separate lookup table | <details><summary>why</summary>`Query.session_id` is a free-form string of up to 64 characters and the surfaces genuinely send different shapes: the web UI sends `crypto.randomUUID()`, the CLI sends bare 32-character hex, and MCP accepts whatever the caller passes. `sessions.id` is a UUID column. The first store simply dropped every session whose id did not parse as a UUID, which would have made session memory work in a browser and be silently inert for the CLI and for most MCP callers. uuid5 needs no schema change and is deterministic, so the same caller id maps to the same row on every process and every deploy, which is the property that makes deriving the key safe rather than storing it. Rejecting non-UUID ids at the contract boundary was rejected because it breaks two shipped surfaces to serve a storage detail. Build phase 4.6 must reuse this mapping for `interactions.session_id` or the two will disagree about which row a conversation is.</details> |
| 2026-08-21 | Build phase 4.5's post-merge review fixes the criticals and the reachable majors, and tracks the minors and the latent findings | Fix only the three criticals and fold the majors into build phase 4.6; fix criticals plus only what 4.6 directly inherits; reopen the whole ledger as a numbered build phase 4.5.1 | <details><summary>why</summary>The two review rounds returned roughly 35 unique findings across 3 criticals, about 13 majors, 15 minors and 4 latent. Build phase 4.3 converged only once it was given an explicit merge bar, a critical or a reachable major blocks while minors and latent findings are tracked, and every phase since has been held to it, so applying a different standard here would have been a silent exception rather than a decision. The narrower options were rejected for a specific reason rather than on principle: several of the majors sit in the completeness repair, which is on the most safety-critical path in the product, and build phase 4.6 inherits both the session-ownership model and the uuid5 row mapping, so deferring those majors would have had 4.6 build on known-broken foundations.</details> |
| 2026-08-21 | Measure the completeness repair's real firing rate with live runs before tuning its trigger, rather than choosing a threshold from the reviewers' reasoning | Gate the trigger on materiality now; change the Synth prompt so a complete answer is achievable; leave the repair as shipped and accept the cost | <details><summary>why</summary>Both review rounds derived the firing rate arithmetically from the shipped prompt, reasoning that a two-to-five-sentence instruction cannot ground up to twenty findings. Every tuning option depended on that rate being roughly right. The measurement found 0 or 1 findings ever reach synthesis, never twenty, so the premise both rounds shared was false and any threshold chosen from it would have been fitted to a system that does not exist. This is `attack-the-constraint`'s own rule, that when a generated output looks wrong the input is the constraint and is cheaper to inspect, applied one level up to two careful reviewers rather than to a model.</details> |
| 2026-08-21 | `persona_name` is added to the MCP response and therefore to `_ALLOWED_RESPONSE_KEYS`, the hand-maintained anti-leak allowlist | Leave MCP as the one surface of four without a persona; agree it belongs but sequence it as its own later change | <details><summary>why</summary>Build phase 4.5's own ticket said the persona reaches all four surfaces and MCP silently got none. The allowlist pins the resolved key set of the whole response tree so that any unlisted key fails, including a renamed cost field a denylist would miss, which makes adding a key a deliberate act rather than a mechanical one. Three things had to hold, the same three the build phase 4.10 entry above it records: the value is a server-side name from a checked-in file of deceased scientists carrying no user data, no cost data and no identifier; the field is optional so Section 13.2's four required fields are untouched and the change is additive under pattern 10; and it is not cost-adjacent, so the leak the allowlist exists to catch is still caught. The agent that wrote the source change stopped at the allowlist and escalated rather than editing a gate to make its own change pass, which is the control working, so the edit was made by a different agent after product-owner approval and the control was re-proven afterwards.</details> |
| 2026-08-21 | Deployment becomes two numbered build phases pulled forward, 4.11 (the read-only HTTPS graph query service) and 4.12 (the demo deployment on Railway), rather than staying inside build phase 6.1 | Leave both in 6.1 where the locked order puts them; add the tickets without moving the timing; build the graph service alone and defer the deployment; record the gap and change nothing | <details><summary>why</summary>Two gaps, found when the product owner asked where deployment sat on the board. First, deployment was implied inside 6.1's "CI and CD gates" line and named as no ticket, and 6.1 depends on "everything above", so nothing was demoable to anyone until the whole build finished. Second, and worse, the read-only HTTPS query service that Decision D names as the v1 Layer 1 transport had no phase at all: Section 24 specifies it in full and says its bearer credential is populated "when the service is built (Section 25 build order)", and Section 25 never assigns it, so one locked section points at another for something that is not there. That gap is load-bearing rather than cosmetic, because Railway does not guarantee a static egress IP, which is the stated reason the service exists, so without it there is no specified path from a deployed API to the graph and a deploy attempt walks into a wall. Split into two phases rather than one because the graph service must exist before a deployment can reach the graph, so bundling them makes a single branch that cannot merge until both are done, and because the service is independently valuable: it removes the hand-opened SSH tunnel that gated every live premise-gate arm for two days this week. Section 25 and the PRD stay unedited until the next reconciliation, per `v1-scope-boundary`; this is the same deliberate board-level insertion 4.8 and 4.10 already set as precedent. The security scan keeps exposure as its trigger rather than a phase number, and now has a phase attached to that trigger for the first time: it runs inside 4.12, before any public URL exists.</details> |
| 2026-08-21 | Build phase 4.6, feedback capture, is taken next, following Section 25's locked order. Build phases 4.11 and 4.12 stay on the board and are not pulled ahead of it | Take 4.11 (the graph query service) first, since it removes the hand-opened SSH tunnel and is the hard prerequisite for any deployment; take 4.12 first to get something demoable soonest | <details><summary>why</summary>Product-owner decision on 2026-08-21, after the two deployment phases were added to the board the same day. The locked build order in Section 25 is followed rather than reordered a fourth time: 4.8 was inserted, 4.10 was pulled ahead, and 4.11 and 4.12 were just added, so the order has already absorbed three exceptions and each one costs the plan some of its authority. The case for taking 4.11 first was argued and rejected rather than overlooked: it would delete the hand-opened SSH tunnel that went down on 2026-08-21 and silently blocked the whole live premise gate for two days, and every future tool phase inherits that fragility. That argument keeps its force, and 4.11 stays on the board with its dependency (2.1) already met, so it can be opened the moment the product owner wants it. What this decision does NOT do is close the deployment question: 4.11 and 4.12 remain scheduled work with 4.12 carrying `product_refine` and the product owner as owner, and the whole-repository security scan still runs inside 4.12 before any public URL exists.</details> |
| 2026-08-21 | `interactions` gains a nullable `owner_id` column (alembic 0008), so ownership of a captured row is a recorded fact and the feedback check is a direct compare | Derive the owner from the `sessions.memory` envelope capture would write; recompute the owner-scoped session key at the feedback endpoint from the run registry | <details><summary>why</summary>Build phase 4.6's feedback writer answered "is this row yours" for a guest by reading the `sessions.memory` ownership envelope, the only durable guest-owner record that existed. `core/session_memory.py` writes that envelope lazily, only on a turn that resolved an entity or grounded a finding, so a guest whose turn resolved nothing was refused. Fail-closed in the right direction and the wrong outcome, the two-armed shape build phase 4.10's gate named: a control that refuses every guest passes every attack test and destroys the product. The envelope option was examined and rejected by the agent asked to build it, on grounds worth keeping: capture has no real summary to wrap, so the envelope needs a placeholder, and the next legitimate memory write then either fails validation on the fabricated payload or finds the row already claimed and locks the true owner out of their own memory. A security fix that becomes a memory-loss bug is not a fix. Recomputing the key at the endpoint works and was rejected for a narrower reason: it ties who owns a row to how long the in-memory run registry keeps a finished run, so ownership stops being answerable after eviction. The column makes ownership answerable forever and independent of both. It also applies build phase 4.3's most expensive lesson deliberately, since that phase shipped the same critical twice, three rounds apart, because a check kept asking a proxy for safety instead of checking the value. The cost is honest and recorded: it overturns this phase's own board premise that the schema already existed and needed no migration, which was written at phase open before any of this was known. Additive and expand-contract safe, nullable with no server default, so every existing row stays valid untouched.</details> |
| 2026-08-21 | Build phase 4.6's F-4.6-04 (predicate coverage tags) is REVERTED rather than repaired, and the phase ships coverage tags with the `concept:` half only | Repair the fix forward and keep the feature; revert the whole phase and re-decompose | <details><summary>why</summary>The round-1 judge found that adding `node_or_edge_type` to `CitationPayload` broke build phase 4.1's blocking MCP premise gate in two arms, one of which asserts over a real MCP client call. That is a finding located inside an earlier fix in the same phase, which `.claude/skills/bossman-mode/SKILL.md`'s Rule 4 makes a stop condition, so the phase halted mid-round and escalated rather than opening another fix round. Three things decided the revert over the repair. The fix had ZERO verification: the judge showed that reverting it entirely left the offline suite byte-identical, and its only real check, premise gate arm P12, cannot run without the live graph, which is down. It also bypassed a documented control, `_ALLOWED_RESPONSE_KEYS`, the hand-maintained anti-leak allowlist that both prior widenings of that payload (build phases 4.10 and 4.5) updated only with explicit product-owner approval, with the escalation recorded in the gate itself as the control working. And the feature it bought is half of one field on a row nothing reads yet. Reverting restores the MCP gate to 49 passed, leaves the allowlist exactly where the previous two phases left it, and shrinks the phase to what is proven. The cost, accepted deliberately: `predicate:` coverage tags do not exist in v1, the prose-matching heuristic that preceded them was NOT restored because it was known-bad, and premise gate arm P12 keeps its exact assertion under an xfail marker so the day the field returns with proper approval the arm signals by passing unexpectedly. Full account: `tracker/phase_4.6.md`, F-4.6-04 and F-4.6-J-02.</details> |

| 2026-08-22 | The transport swap happens INSIDE `graph_connection.execute_cypher`, dispatching on `GRAPH_QUERY_URL`, with the function's signature, return type and callers unchanged, and naming both transports in one call raises rather than silently picking one | A new transport-selecting wrapper above `execute_cypher`; a parameter on `cypher_query` naming the transport; letting an explicit `connection_factory` quietly win over the environment | <details><summary>why</summary>Measured before decomposing: `execute_cypher` is the only I/O boundary and `cypher_query.py` is its only caller in `src/`, at three call sites that all pass it as a callable. Confirmed after the fact: those three call sites needed zero edits. A wrapper or a parameter would push transport choice up into the tool layer, contradicting `system-design-patterns` pattern 2's line that transport is a harness decision rather than an agent decision. The raise-on-conflict half was the expensive part and it was still right: it broke seventeen unit tests the moment the local `.env` was cut over (finding F-4.11-07), because importing `litellm` makes the whole `.env` ambient. The fix was to make tests state their transport rather than to soften the rule, since silently honouring one transport and discarding the other is how a suite goes on passing against a transport nobody is shipping.</details> |
| 2026-08-22 | TLS terminates on the Hetzner reverse-DNS hostname `static.133.128.225.46.clients.your-server.de` with a Let's Encrypt certificate obtained by Caddy, rather than on a purchased domain or a self-signed internal CA | Buying and pointing a domain at the box; Caddy's `tls internal` self-signed CA with the certificate pinned in the client | <details><summary>why</summary>The project owns no domain and none appears anywhere in the requirements or the reference documentation, so a purchased domain would be a new recurring dependency introduced by a transport phase. The Hetzner reverse-DNS name already forward-resolves to `46.225.128.133`, verified by lookup before the choice was made, which is all Let's Encrypt's HTTP-01 challenge needs. Issuance succeeded on the first attempt and the premise gate verifies the certificate against the ordinary system trust store. That keeps Section 24's automatic-HTTPS reverse proxy literally true and leaves no certificate material to distribute to Railway later, which is why the self-signed option was rejected: it works, and it turns every future consumer of this service into a place a CA certificate has to be installed and rotated. One risk is accepted and recorded rather than hidden: `your-server.de` is a shared Hetzner namespace, so a future Let's Encrypt rate limit on the registered domain is possible and outside this project's control. If renewal ever fails for that reason the fallback is the self-signed CA, and that fallback gets its own decision row rather than being taken silently.</details> |
| 2026-08-22 | Caddy is installed from Ubuntu's own `universe` repository (2.6.2, served from `mirror.hetzner.com`) rather than from the vendor's apt repository or install script | The upstream Caddy apt repository, which carries newer releases; the vendor's `curl` install script | <details><summary>why</summary>`.claude/rules/supply-chain-security.md` treats every new execution surface as needing provenance, and the distribution package is the strongest provenance available here: signed by Ubuntu, mirrored by the hosting provider, and carrying security updates through the same channel already trusted for the rest of the box. Adding a vendor apt repository means trusting a second signing key for one package, and an install script piped to a shell is the exact pattern that rule exists to refuse. The cost is a Caddy a few minor versions behind, which does not matter for the single feature being used: automatic HTTPS on one hostname with a reverse proxy to localhost.</details> |

Build phase 4.12 is where the product itself gets a URL a person can open, which is the exposure the deferral has always meant. Recorded as a judgment made deliberately rather than an omission, and the phase's adversary was briefed to attack it specifically: if a reachable path from this port to anything user-facing exists, the judgment is wrong and the scan runs.</details> |
| 2026-08-22 | A Hetzner Cloud API token was requested from the product owner to open firewall ports, and the request was WRONG: no firewall existed. Recorded as a decision because the reasoning that produced it will recur, and because it cost a real credential grant | Running the negative control probe first, which would have cost seconds and prevented the whole exchange | <details><summary>why</summary>The lead probed the box from inside this harness and read ports 80, 443 and 5432 as filtered, then checked the operating system, found `ufw` inactive and `iptables`, `ip6tables` and `nftables` all empty, and concluded the filter must therefore be upstream at the provider. A control probe HAD been run and is why the conclusion felt safe: `github.com:443` and `github.com:22` both connected, which was read as proving egress worked generally. It proved only that those two destinations had listeners. The Hetzner API then returned zero firewalls in the project and none attached to the server, and installing Caddy made port 80 answer from outside in 0.14 seconds, so the ports had been open the entire time. The actual cause is that this harness renders a destination with nothing listening identically to a blocked one, as an eight second timeout: `github.com:12345` times out exactly like the graph box did. GENERAL FORM, and the reason this is a decision rather than only a learning: a control probe must exercise the NEGATIVE case, a destination that should fail, not only one that should succeed, because a control that can only come back green is not a control. The same ambiguity had already been written into the phase's own premise gate, where the arm asserting the database port stays closed would have passed with that port wide open (F-4.11-01). The token was never used; the product owner is asked to revoke it.</details> |

That rule fired three separate times here, and each time the lead stopped and escalated rather than deciding it. What made the extra rounds worth authorising, rather than a slide into build phase 4.3's six-round failure, is that the findings changed in KIND each round: round 1 found two reachable majors in the original code, round 2's review found an unbounded resource inside round 1's fix, round 3's found a diagnostic regression and a CPU cost inside round 2's fix. Severity fell monotonically, from a control that did not bound the unauthenticated path, to a bound whose bookkeeping was unbounded, to a counter that under-reported and a sweep that cost too much. The stopping rule that actually applied was not the round number but that: when the newest round's worst finding degrades a diagnostic rather than breaching a control, the marginal round is no longer buying safety.</details> |
| 2026-08-22 | Round 4 was run GATE FIRST: every arm written and watched failing before the fix it grades | The order used in rounds 2 and 3, fix first and add an arm afterwards; merging with the findings tracked as flags for build phase 4.12 | <details><summary>why</summary>Rounds 2 and 3 each fixed first and wrote the arm afterwards, and each planted a defect inside its own fix. An arm written after its fix is written to confirm the fix works; an arm written before it is written to find where it does not. The measured return in one round: the arms caught the real defect, caught TWO WRONG PREMISES IN THE LEAD'S OWN ARMS (one asserted an active caller survives twenty newer arrivals under a strict recency cap, which correct code refuses; the other expected refusal on the 60th request against a limit of 60), and caught a tension introduced minutes earlier where amortising a sweep also deferred the cap it contained. Two of the three findings the round set out to fix turned out to be GATE GAPS OVER ALREADY-CORRECT CODE, so fixing them on the strength of the report alone would have meant changing working code. That is the argument for the ordering: writing the arm first is what distinguishes a defect from a gap in the instrument.</details> |
| 2026-08-22 | The bookkeeping bound uses TWO mechanisms, an amortised idle sweep and a hard ceiling with batched eviction to a low-water mark, rather than either alone | Eviction on idleness alone; a hard cap alone; an LRU container such as OrderedDict; leaving the tables unbounded and accepting the memory growth | <details><summary>why</summary>Either mechanism alone fails on a case the other covers, and both cases are real. Eviction alone does not handle sources arriving faster than they expire, which is exactly what an attacker with an IPv6 /64 produces for free, and the box has one. A hard cap alone does not stop ordinary traffic creeping to the ceiling over days and then paying eviction costs forever. The batching is a third, separate decision forced by measurement: trimming to exactly the ceiling meant every later arrival pushed the table one over and paid a full sort of the whole table to remove one key, measured at 0.857ms against 0.00087ms cold, a 990x amplification running synchronously on the event loop on the PRE-AUTH path, which is the same failure class as this phase's own round 1 headline. Trimming ten percent at once amortises that sort over the next thousand arrivals: measured after, 3.7x. An LRU container was rejected because the eviction order needs a secondary key, keys carrying an unreported suppressed count must sort last, and that is clearer as an explicit sort than as a container's implicit ordering.</details> |
| 2026-08-22 | TLS terminates on the Hetzner reverse-DNS hostname with a Let's Encrypt certificate, and the ACCEPTED RISK is recorded rather than hidden: `your-server.de` is a shared namespace and a future rate limit on it is outside this project's control | Buying a domain; Caddy's `tls internal` self-signed CA with the certificate pinned in the client | <details><summary>why</summary>Logged as its own row on 2026-08-22 and restated here because the risk half is the part a later reader needs. The project owns no domain and none appears in the requirements, so buying one introduces a recurring dependency from a transport phase. The reverse-DNS name already forward-resolves to the box, which is all the HTTP-01 challenge needs, and issuance succeeded first time against the ordinary system trust store, so there is no certificate material to distribute to Railway later. The self-signed option was rejected for exactly that reason: it works, and it turns every future consumer into a place a CA certificate must be installed and rotated. If renewal ever fails because of a shared-namespace rate limit, the fallback is the self-signed CA and it gets its own decision row rather than being taken silently.</details> |

Three supporting reasons, none of them decisive alone: `plan_node` compensates for the stub by scanning for a capitalised token and guessing it is a gene symbol, which breaks four of the seven must-pass moat questions, so the gap is central rather than cosmetic; 4.7's verification is now cheap, which was the single stated reason it was deferred, and that condition is met the moment 4.11 merged; and a demo shows a person the version they will remember. 4.12 loses little by waiting, since its dependency is met and stays met and the deployment work does not get harder. This decision DISSOLVES both rather than answering either: with typed extraction, `ADHD` comes back typed as a disease and `brca1` comes back typed as a gene, and there is no list to tune. The code's own comment already calls the list "a filter at the wrong layer: it can only ever be as complete as the last defect someone noticed". The fallback option was rejected because a fallback keeps the defective extractor alive and reachable on a degraded path nobody watches, so the exact refusal F-2.0-15 describes (GTR, AMR and SRA read as gene symbols, refusing 4 of the 7 must-pass questions) could still fire when the model provider is down. The honest degrade is to resolve nothing and let Plan proceed without target entities. Product-owner decision, taken at phase open before any ticket was written</details> |

T-4.7-05's own acceptance criteria and the premise gate's P5 arm both name only the four shapes; building a fifth, undirected mechanism (what table, how populated, how kept current) would have been scope invented mid-build, which `.claude/rules/v1-scope-boundary.md` and `goal-contracts.md` both treat as the wrong default when a ticket's own text is silent on something the spec mentions elsewhere. | 2026-08-23 | Build phase 4.7 fixes F-4.7-A-01 (injection steers entity resolution) and F-4.7-A-03 (taxon hardcoded to human) inside the phase, files F-4.7-A-02 (a discontinued gene record answered as its successor, undisclosed) as PRE-EXISTING with a named owner, and runs a THIRD review round, over the two-round cap, with product-owner authorization | Fixing all three here, including the pre-existing one; reverting build phase 4.7 entirely and re-decomposing | <details><summary>why</summary>The split is by CAUSATION, established by evidence rather than by argument. The retired heuristic was `\b[A-Z][A-Z0-9]{1,9}\b`. It MATCHES `BRCA3`, so A-02 reached the identical undisclosed BRCA2 answer before this phase existed and is not this phase's defect. It does NOT match `Tp53`, because of the lowercase `p`, so the old code resolved nothing and refused, while the new model extractor identifies it and answers about human TP53 with 13 real citations. This phase therefore converted an honest refusal into a confident, fully cited wrong answer, which is this system's worst output class, so A-03 blocks and belongs here. A-01 is new by construction: a regex cannot be talked into anything, and moving question understanding to a model is what made the payload worth writing. Fixing A-02 here was rejected on blast radius, not on merit, since its fix is genuinely cheap (the deciding `status` and `currentid` fields are already inside the ESummary response the resolver retrieves and it reads only `name`): this phase has ALREADY had a critical land inside its own fix, a Rule 4 stop, and widening a repair round in a phase with that history is this repository's most-measured hazard. Config that will not propagate is not a mechanism to keep debugging, so this moved into code, where it is version-controlled and verifiable from the application's own logs. WHAT IT DOES NOT SOLVE, recorded in the function's own docstring rather than left to be discovered: two instances starting at once both run it. Alembic locks its version table so the loser waits rather than corrupting anything, but running migrations from application processes is the wrong shape at any real concurrency. Correct for a single-instance demo, and it should be replaced by a platform pre-deploy hook the moment one is reachable. Default OFF, so nothing about local development or the test suite changes.</details> |
