| 2026-08-25 | `ToolStartPayload.status` widens to `Literal["running", "ok", "empty", "error"]`, and `ToolResultPayload` RE-NARROWS it to the original three rather than inheriting the wider union | Emitting `"ok"` on a start event as a placeholder; making `status` optional on the start payload; leaving the union alone and emitting no `tool_start` at all | <details><summary>why</summary>Build phase 4.16 is the first phase to actually emit a `tool_start`, and the event is written the instant before a tool is dispatched, so its outcome does not merely happen to be unknown, it does not exist yet. Every value in the original three-member union would therefore have been a claim about a call that had not run. Emitting `"ok"` there asserts success before the fact, which is the assert-what-you-have-not-checked shape build phase 4.3 shipped as a critical twice and build phase 4.7 hit again inside its own gate; the type was wrong, not the caller. Making the field optional was rejected as a redefinition rather than an addition, which `system-design-patterns` pattern 10 forbids inside v1, whereas that same pattern permits a new enum value by name. `ToolResultPayload` re-narrows because a result knows its outcome: letting it inherit the wider union would make "the tool finished, and it is still running" a representable state, and its accepted set is byte-for-byte what it was before, so no existing producer or consumer of a `tool_result` changes behaviour. It is also the value the UI already assumed: `useRunView.ts` has hardcoded the literal string "running" for a chip's detail text since build phase 4.8, against an event nothing had ever emitted.</details> |
| 2026-08-25 | Act-step events are delivered through LangGraph's custom stream via `get_stream_writer`, AND still returned through `sink.result()`, with `run_streaming` de-duplicating by `seq` | Emitting inside `act_node` and accepting node-return delivery; splitting Act into one graph node per tool call so each flush is a node boundary; delivering only on the custom stream and dropping the state return | <details><summary>why</summary>`run_streaming` drives `astream(stream_mode="updates")`, which yields one dict per completed node, and `_EventSink` accumulates a node's events until its return. `act_node` is one node that runs every tool, so the obvious fix, a plain `sink.emit` inside its loop, would have flushed every tool frame in a single burst immediately before the answer and left the measured 10.9-second silence exactly as long: correct in a unit test, invisible to a person. Splitting Act into per-tool nodes was rejected as a change to the graph's shape, which Section 25's five-node loop is built on, for a delivery problem. Custom-stream-only was rejected because the state return is what puts an event into `GraphState.events`, which the resumable replay buffer, the run's own record and the interaction capture row all read, so dropping it would have made the entire Act step missing from every one of those. Keeping both means each such event arrives twice, so `run_streaming` de-duplicates on `seq`, which is assigned once in `_EventSink.emit` and unique within a run by construction. `get_stream_writer` was verified to be a no-op under `ainvoke` by probing the installed LangGraph directly rather than reading the `>=0.2` pin, so the buffered `run()` path is unchanged.</details> |
| 2026-08-25 | Client-side routing is built on the History API with NO router dependency | Adding react-router; leaving every page at `/`; a hash-based router | <details><summary>why</summary>Four static paths, no route parameters, no nested layouts, no loaders, and one already-existing piece of state (`App.tsx`'s `screen`) that a router would only mirror. `production-standards` requires a security review for every new dependency and `system-design-patterns` puts it in the ASK bucket, which is the right price for a library that earns it and the wrong one here. Deep links needed no server change and never did: the frontend is served by `serve -s dist`, whose `-s` is single-page-app mode, so `/integrations` always reached the app and nothing read it. Revisit if this app grows real route parameters or nested layouts, and pay for the review then rather than pre-paying now.</details> |
| 2026-08-25 | The `ask` trust outcome reads "Single source, not independently confirmed" rather than "Needs a narrower question" | Keeping the existing copy; "Treat with caution, one source"; changing the trust-pills design card first and shipping nothing until it lands | <details><summary>why</summary>Product-owner decision. The old copy was seen live above a grounded answer carrying five resolving citations, and it described something that had not happened: locked specification section 8.3.3 says "Ask is reserved for a high-stakes claim resting on a single independent-origin source", so the outcome is about the EVIDENCE and the copy blamed the reader's question. On a product whose whole argument is that its status line can be believed, a status line misattributing caution to something the reader did is a trust defect rather than a copy nit. The new wording states the evidence the way every neighbouring pill does rather than instructing. Taken directly rather than through the design system because the trust-pills card has NO `ask` state at all, so there was nothing to build against; the card gap is recorded in `tracker/phase_4.16.md` for the card to absorb.</details> |
| 2026-08-25 | A citation chip's redundant source prefix is removed at DISPLAY time, never on the wire | Changing `source_id` to a bare local id in `core/graph.py`; leaving "MedGen MedGen:C0346153" as shipped; dropping `source` from the label instead | <details><summary>why</summary>Neither producer was wrong. `core/graph.py` documents its choice deliberately, "the CURIE prefix names the source database, the full CURIE is the source id", and a resolvable CURIE is exactly what makes a Layer 1 citation followable; `ncbi_efetch` sends a database name and a bare record id, which joins correctly. Only the join is wrong, and only for one of the two shapes, which is why it looked right wherever anyone checked it. Changing `source_id` would trade a provenance field for a display convenience, the wrong direction in a system whose trust moat is that a citation can be followed. The case of `source` itself is deliberately NOT normalised: "gene" should read "Gene" per the card, but the same field carries "dbSNP" and "MedGen", whose casing is meaningful, so a display rule for values this function has not seen is left to the design card rather than guessed.</details> |
| 2026-08-25 | A follow-up keeps the previous turn on screen, collapsed, rather than replacing it | Leaving each follow-up to replace the answer; raising the follow-up field's prominence instead; demoting the "New search" button | <details><summary>why</summary>Product-owner decision, in their own words: "follow up is part of the current search. Check out style in the design system that we have." The prototype already specifies it in code: `askFollowUp` calls `archiveCurrent()`, which lifts the finished turn into a collapsed `<details class="prev">` appended to `<div class="thread">` and only then renders the new answer above it, ordered sources, verdict, thread, follow-up form. Nothing had built it, so every follow-up replaced the answer and the screen stopped reading as a conversation while the dispatch itself worked perfectly, which is why two rounds of investigation looking for a failed second turn found nothing. The two alternatives were this phase's own earlier readings and both were wrong: "renders late" was an artifact of a vacuous landed-signal, and the salience theory was measured and ruled out, since the follow-up input sits above the fold at every viewport tested. The follow-up and the thread exist ONLY in the prototype and in no component card, which is the design-system gap that let this ship; the styling is taken from the prototype's own `.prev` and `.thread` rules rather than invented.</details> |
| 2026-08-25 | The `doc-readability` skill enforces its no-information-lost guarantee with a deterministic script plus a fresh-context auditor, and both must pass | A fresh-context auditor alone; a human reviewing every diff by eye; trusting the restructuring model to self-check | <details><summary>why</summary>Product-owner decision. The constraint they set was that a readability pass may never drop a fact, and a guarantee enforced only by a model grading its own restructure is the shape this repository has already seen fail: a checker that cannot diff misses what it cannot see. The script makes loss a build failure rather than a review finding, which is the same move as the permanent mutation harness build phase 4.7 added after three vacuous gate arms. The auditor stays because one class of defect is genuinely unscriptable: a model that ADDS a paragraph which is really the original prose wall reworded passes every mechanical arm, since it reuses the original's own words. Neither half covers the other's gap, so neither is sufficient alone.</details> |
| 2026-08-25 | The preservation gate compares before against after in ONE direction only, never as a diff | A symmetric diff; a line-level or sentence-level comparison; requiring the after version to be a superset of the before version verbatim | <details><summary>why</summary>The product owner approved the skill ADDING first-principles explanation while forbidding it from dropping a fact, and those two permissions are asymmetric, so the check must be too. Findings are computed only as before minus after; after minus before is computed as the additions manifest and can never change the exit code. A symmetric diff would fire on every legitimate restructure, because converting a prose wall into bullets destroys word order, deletes connectives, and collapses repetition by design. Measured: the three arms are set-based rather than order-based, connectives are stripped before counting, and the soft arm compares distinct types rather than tokens, which is what lets a real restructure pass while a deleted number, date, path, table row, or negation still fails.</details> |
| 2026-08-25 | Both bundled scripts reject a loosened threshold and an unrecognized flag with exit 2 | Accepting any threshold value and trusting the operator; warning on a loosened threshold but proceeding; ignoring unknown flags silently as most CLI tools do | <details><summary>why</summary>`.claude/rules/goal-contracts.md` states that changing the check so the check passes is a failed run, and a rule stated only in prose is one an autonomous run can talk itself past. Making the loosened value unreachable through the CLI is `system-design-patterns.md` pattern 8 applied to a gate: remove the ability rather than ask nicely. Strict flag validation was added for a defect found during the build rather than anticipated: a typo such as `--lex-threshhold 0.5` was silently ignored, so the gate ran at its default while the operator believed they had tightened it. The tighten-only guarantee depends on the flag actually being read.</details> |
| 2026-08-25 | The two locked requirements documents get a report-only readability analysis, never an edit, while the lock stands | Unlocking them for this run and restructuring both; a minimal reformat-only pass on them; skipping them entirely with no analysis | <details><summary>why</summary>Product-owner decision, made when a readability pass was requested across five documents and two of them turned out to be frozen. Three separate controls already said no: `.claude/rules/v1-scope-boundary.md` forbids editing them, the `doc-readability` skill's Refusals section names them, and both bundled scripts refuse them with exit 3. Crossing all three quietly would have been the worst version of this. The chosen path keeps the freeze intact and still produces the value: a prioritized, executable findings list for each, so the restructure can run the moment the Step 6.2 reconciliation unlocks them. The rejected middle option, a reformat-only pass, was rejected on `writing-style.md`'s own three-state permissions, which say to ask before restructuring a locked document even when the reformat preserves meaning exactly.</details> |
| 2026-08-25 | Hosting cost is budgeted against the invoice, not the rate card, in every document that states it | Keeping the pre-tax figure as the headline; publishing only the invoice figure and dropping the rate-card breakdown; adding a rounding buffer to the pre-tax figure instead | <details><summary>why</summary>Both README.md and the server reference carried ~$28/month for Hetzner, which is the pre-tax rate card rather than what leaves the account. The server reference published it under the label 'Total all-in', and that label is what let it propagate: it reads as final, so README inherited it and every total built on it was roughly 23 percent low. The real charge is ~$35 with tax, confirmed against the invoice. Both figures are now published, relabelled 'Total before tax' and 'Actual charge including tax', because dropping either one loses something: the rate card is what Hetzner lists and is checkable against their site, the invoice is what you budget against. The 'round to $30/month for FX volatility headroom' guidance was removed rather than adjusted, since it pointed the wrong way: the real charge is above the rate card, not within rounding distance of it. Same principle applied to Railway on the same day, where a published-guidance estimate of $10-30/month was replaced by $5/month measured from the live services.</details> |
| 2026-08-25 | A determinism check must be pointed at a real document pair that PRODUCES FINDINGS; a fixture, or a clean pair, is not a valid subject for one | Testing determinism against the bundled golden fixture; building a synthetic fixture engineered to force anchoring ties; running the comparison repeatedly in one process | <details><summary>why</summary>Measured, not reasoned. The preservation gate was found NON-DETERMINISTIC: string hashing is randomized per process and its candidate ranking followed set iteration order, so byte-identical input produced different answers between runs. The obvious test for that was built three times before it worked. With the fix deliberately reverted, the bundled golden fixture passed under every hash seed, a fixture built specifically to force ties passed, and a real 355-line document pair carrying zero findings also passed. Only a real pair carrying actual findings produced two different results and caught it. The third case is the dangerous one and is why this is recorded as a decision rather than a note: a real document with a clean comparison LOOKS like a legitimate test and certifies a broken build. In-process repetition was rejected outright because Python fixes the hash seed once per process, so the loop cannot fail by construction. The general form, which applies beyond this script: a test for a property that only manifests under load, contention, or ordering must be run against a subject that actually exercises the property, and a green result on a subject that cannot exercise it is not evidence.</details> |
| 2026-08-25 | A document is not reworded to satisfy a checker when the document is right and the checker uses a different definition | Changing the document's PR number to match the computed value; adding a hedge word so the checker's classifier skips the line; changing the checker's own definition | <details><summary>why</summary>`tracker/check_doc_drift.py` reported `requirements/Plan.md` stale for saying build phase 3.1 merged as PR #22, computing PR #23. Git shows PR #22 merged `phase/3.1-ncbi-efetch` and PR #23 merged a separate `fix/3.1-rereview-round1-critical-regressions` branch, and the checker's own docstring says it deliberately takes the max across both branch shapes, so a phase's PR is the LAST one that closed it. Both were right about different events. The document was rewritten to state both parts explicitly rather than adopt a single number that would have made a true sentence false. The same situation exists for build phase 4.12, where PR #61 merged the phase branch and PR #62 merged a `fix/4.12-` follow-up: `tracker/BOARD.md` and `tracker/phase_4.16.md` still say #61 and were deliberately LEFT ALONE, because they are historically accurate and the drift checker does not flag them. The principle worth keeping: changing a document so a check passes, when the document was correct, converts a green gate into a false record, which is the failure `goal-contracts.md` names.</details> |
| 2026-08-26 | Import order gets exactly ONE tool, and it is isort; ruff's `I001` is turned off | Letting both tools run and reconciling their configurations until they agree; dropping isort and letting ruff's `I` rules satisfy Section 24's gate 2; excluding `alembic/` from one of the two | <details><summary>why</summary>Measured, not reasoned, and it was found only by running both. Section 24 names `isort --check` as gate 2 and `ruff check` as gate 3, and this repository's ruff rule set already enables the `I` rules, so both tools policed import order. They do not agree. With ruff green, `isort --check` failed on 26 files; after running isort, `ruff check` failed on 23. Each tool's fix re-breaks the other, so one of the two gates is red no matter what anyone commits, permanently. The largest single cause also settles which tool to believe: ruff sees a directory named `alembic/` at the repository root and classifies `from alembic import op` as FIRST-PARTY, when it is the installed third-party migration library and `alembic/` is the migrations directory, not an importable package. isort classifies it correctly. Reconciling the two configurations was rejected because it is an unbounded ongoing tax that pays nothing: the property is already fully covered by either tool alone. Dropping isort was rejected because Section 24 is locked and names it. This is deliberately NOT weakening a gate: the property stays checked, by the tool the specification assigns to it and by the tool that is right about this repository's layout, and `test_p13_import_order_has_exactly_one_owner` fails loudly if anyone re-enables the second one.</details> |
| 2026-08-26 | `pip-audit` runs as `-r requirements.txt`, never bare, and ruff is pinned to an exact version | Upgrading or pinning `cryptography` to clear the finding; adding an ignore list for the two advisories; leaving ruff on a `>=` floor as the other dev tools are | <details><summary>why</summary>A bare `pip-audit` audits the ambient virtualenv rather than the project. On the machine where build phase 4.14 was written it reported two findings, and neither belonged to this project: `cryptography 49.0.0`, which `pip show` reports as required by nothing at all, and `pip` itself, the installer. `pip-audit -r requirements.txt` returns "No known vulnerabilities found". Pinning `cryptography` was rejected precisely because it was the tempting fix: it would have edited a dependency this project does not declare in order to satisfy an instrument pointed at the wrong subject, which is the inverse of reward hacking that `goal-contracts` names, and it would have produced a green gate certifying nothing. Ruff's exact pin is the same discipline aimed at reproducibility rather than scope: this repository sets no explicit `select`, so the enabled rule set is whatever the installed version defaults to. Measured during this phase, ruff 0.16.0 enables far more than its documented defaults, so a `>=` floor would let a minor release silently change what gate 3 enforces with nobody editing a line of configuration.</details> |
| 2026-08-26 | CI runs a PostgreSQL service AND asserts no test skipped for a database reason; the service alone is not accepted as evidence | Trusting the service block; pinning the total skip count; letting the database-backed tests skip on the runner as they already do locally when Postgres is down | <details><summary>why</summary>Every database-backed test in this repository probes connectivity and calls `pytest.skip` when it fails, rather than failing. On the machine where this phase was written PostgreSQL was running, so those tests RAN and were counted inside "3947 passed"; a GitHub Actions runner has none, and the identical command there would have printed an identical confident green while silently skipping all of them. Measured directly rather than argued: pointed at a dead database, `pytest tests/system_03_search_agent/auth/test_router.py` prints "1 skipped in 0.29s" and EXITS 0, with 62 real tests gone. So the workflow runs the service, and a separate step reads the skip reasons and fails if any names a database. Trusting the service was rejected because a wrong port, a wrong database name or an unhealthy container all leave a workflow that looks correct and certifies nothing. Pinning the total skip count was rejected because the suite has 136 legitimate `RUN_PREMISE_GATE` skips and would fail every time someone correctly added one, which is how a gate earns being switched off; matching on the REASON stays quiet across all 137 and still goes red on the real failure, proven in both directions.</details> |
| 2026-08-26 | CORRECTION to the same day's import-order decision: ruff's `I001` is RESTORED, and the disagreement is fixed with `known-third-party = ["alembic"]` rather than by disabling the rule | Leaving `I001` ignored repository-wide as first decided; excluding `alembic/` from ruff entirely; reconciling every remaining difference by further configuration | <details><summary>why</summary>The earlier row that day concluded ruff and isort were irreconcilable and gave import order to isort alone by ignoring `I001`. The judge and the adversary rejected that independently and measurement settles it: one line, `known-third-party = ["alembic"]`, takes the disagreement from 23 files to 2. Disabling the rule left 274 files unpoliced by ruff to solve a problem that 21 of 23 did not have. What was RIGHT in the original reasoning is the diagnosis: ruff does see a directory named `alembic/` at the repository root and does classify `from alembic import op` as first-party, and isort is correct where ruff is not. What was wrong is the conclusion drawn from it, reached by measuring the whole disagreement and never asking how much of it one setting would remove. The residual 2 files are a genuine conflict and are resolved the other way: isort skips them, ruff's `I001` still checks them, and isort is overruled there because its preferred form collapses a multi-line explanatory comment into a single semicolon-joined line. Every file is now checked by at least one of the two tools, which is the property the premise arm now asserts, instead of asserting that the second checker stays switched off. The general form worth keeping: when two tools disagree, measure how much of the disagreement is one configuration line before concluding they cannot both run.</details> |
| 2026-08-26 | The CI workflow contains NO shell: every gate's command moves into a checked-in script under `.github/gates/`, and a gate step's `run:` must EQUAL that script's path | Adding `;#` to the comment stripper and continuing with substring matching; using a real shell parser such as `bashlex` to analyse the inline shell; abandoning the premise gate and trusting CI's own runs | <details><summary>why</summary>Product-owner decision, taken after build phase 4.14 hit its Rule 4 stop, and the reasoning is the transferable part rather than the outcome. The premise gate had been defeated twice by text that read correctly and executed nothing. The second defeat: `run: ":;#ruff check"` runs NOTHING, because bash begins a comment at `#` whenever `#` starts a word and `;` ends a word, while the stripper only treated `#` as a comment after whitespace. Eight of the ten gates were neutralised with all 97 tests green, including the mutation harness whose whole job was proving those arms could fail. Handling `;#` was rejected precisely because it is locally correct: the fourth version loses to `&&#`, the fifth to `(#`, the sixth to a YAML block scalar, and the sequence never terminates, because matching substrings inside arbitrary shell is what cannot be made safe. A shell parser was rejected as a heavier dependency that still answers the same unanswerable question, what does this arbitrary shell do, and would have to stay correct against every bash edge case forever. Trusting CI alone was rejected because a workflow defect shows up as a green check rather than a failing run, which is the whole reason the premise gate exists. Moving the shell out converts the check from a substring search into two whole-string EQUALITIES, and an equality has no room for a comment or a second command. The evidence it is a class removal rather than a fifth patch, measured rather than argued: ONE structural arm now catches `:;#`, `&&#`, `&#124;&#124;#`, `(#`, a newline-separated second command, `true # command`, an appended `&#124;&#124; true`, and an appended redirect, each of which the previous version would have needed its own case for. The general form worth keeping: when a check keeps losing to inputs of the same shape, stop hardening the check and restructure the subject so the property becomes an equality.</details> |
| 2026-08-26 | A test that cannot run in CI is fixed, never added to the skip allowlist, even when the test is one of ours | Adding the skip reason to `_SANCTIONED_SKIPS`; marking the arm `xfail` on CI; deleting the arm as environment-dependent | <details><summary>why</summary>CI run 6 failed on ONE unsanctioned skip, and it was the lead's own, written hours earlier in the same phase: a gate 10 filter test looked for a real commit touching `frontend/` with `git rev-list --all`, and the `python-gates` job checks out at depth 1, so on a runner no such commit exists and the arm skipped. Adding it to the allowlist would have taken four seconds and been the exact reward-hacking move `goal-contracts` forbids: weakening the verify surface so the verify surface passes, with the aggravating factor that the allowlist exists precisely so that decision cannot be made quietly. The durable fix removes the ambient dependency instead: both filter arms now build a throwaway git repository with two commits and diff those, so they run identically on a developer machine, a shallow clone and a fork. Recorded as a decision rather than a note because the guard caught its own author, which is the strongest evidence available that it is not merely decorative, and because the cheap wrong move was genuinely tempting.</details> |
| 2026-08-27 | Build phase 4.13 makes the LIST of past questions durable and not the answers: a restored history item re-asks its question, exactly as a live item already does | Adding an answer column to `interactions` and replaying the stored narrative; storing the assembled answer in a side table keyed on `trace_id`; deferring the whole phase until answers could be persisted too | <details><summary>why</summary>`interactions` stores `query_text`, `citations`, `trust_signal`, `rubric_outcome`, timings and cost, and no synthesised narrative at all (`feedback/contracts.py`, `InteractionRow`). Persisting one is a new column, a migration and a retention decision about storing generated biomedical prose, none of which the phase's board row asked for, and a migration would also have destroyed this phase's ability to re-measure its own suite baseline (the build phase 4.6 lesson in `LEARNINGS.md`). The board row asked for "a read path over the `interactions` rows build phase 4.6 now writes, plus the UI that renders them", which is exactly what the substrate supports. Recorded as a scope boundary in `tracker/phase_4.13.md` rather than narrowed silently, since a reader could reasonably expect clicking a past search to show the past answer.</details> |
| 2026-08-27 | `GET /v1/history` refuses a `limit` above 50 with a 422 naming the maximum, rather than silently clamping to it | Clamping to the maximum and returning 200; returning 400 with a bare message; leaving the limit unbounded and capping in the read function alone | <details><summary>why</summary>A clamp answers a question the caller did not ask and tells them nothing about the substitution, so a client asking for 200 rows and receiving 50 cannot tell a complete answer from a truncated one. `tool-call-budgets` requires that a bound's error say what to do next rather than what failed, and 422 carrying the string "50" does exactly that. The bound is enforced twice on purpose: `FastAPIQuery(ge=1, le=50)` produces the caller-facing refusal, and `list_history` raises `ValueError` independently, so a future non-HTTP caller of that module gets the same contract rather than inheriting the adapter's validation by accident.</details> |
| 2026-08-27 | Build phase 4.13 merges with F-4.13-02 OPEN: after a reload a visitor must sign in again before any history appears, and "keep me signed in" becomes its own scoped piece of work | Persisting the refresh token in localStorage inside this phase; moving it to an httpOnly cookie inside this phase; dropping the `signedIn` gate so guests see their own rail | <details><summary>why</summary>The phase's own done-when is that searches survive closing the browser. On the server they do; on screen they do not, because `App.tsx:208` holds the access token in React state alone, so every reload signs an account out, and the rail is gated on being signed in. The backend has issued a rotating refresh token since build phase 1.1 (`auth_sessions`, rotation, absolute TTL, revocation) and the frontend discards it, so the fix is wiring rather than invention. It was still not taken here: where a bearer credential may be persisted is a security trade-off (localStorage is XSS-reachable; an httpOnly cookie needs a backend change, CSRF handling and a CORS review across two deployed origins), and settling it in the last hour of an unrelated phase is how that decision gets made badly. Product-owner decision, 2026-08-27, with all three alternatives put explicitly.</details> |
| 2026-08-27 | A rail row's identity is `local-` plus a module-scoped counter for a locally created row and the server `trace_id` for a restored one, rather than `crypto.randomUUID()` | Using `crypto.randomUUID()`; keeping the positional `${current.length}` and reverting the re-ask fix that made it unsafe; deriving an id by hashing the question text | <details><summary>why</summary>`crypto.randomUUID` was CHECKED rather than assumed and it is present in this environment (jsdom 29, Node 24, defined on both `globalThis` and `window`). It was still declined: in a real browser it exists only in a secure context, so a build served over plain HTTP on a LAN address returns undefined and throws, as does Safari before 15.4. A counter needs no environment support and is deterministic under test. Reverting the reducer was rejected because filter-then-unshift is correct and matches the prototype: the prototype can do it safely because its row identity is not positional, so the defect was the identity and not the reducer. Hashing the question text was rejected because a re-ask must be able to produce a genuinely new row. The two id spaces cannot collide, a UUID never matching `local-N`.</details> |
| 2026-08-27 | A value read back OUT of the database is untrusted input exactly like one arriving over HTTP: no computation runs on a stored value before its type is checked, and a count that cannot be computed honestly is disclosed as unknown rather than fabricated | Guarding only the adapter, where the symptom appeared; coercing a non-list JSONB value to an empty list and reporting zero; letting the whole request fail so the problem is loud | <details><summary>why</summary>F-4.13-A-02's first fix guarded response-model construction in the adapter, and `feedback/history.py`'s `len(row.citations or [])` still raised one layer upstream, reproducing the exact whole-list 500 the finding was filed for, reachable by calling the read function directly rather than through the endpoint. Coercing to zero was rejected under the rule this repository already settled at F-3.3-J-04 on 2026-08-15: if the system drops or shortens anything it discloses that it did. A JSONB string had been yielding a fabricated `citation_count: 3` alongside `omitted_count: 0`, a wrong number presented as complete, which is worse than an error because nothing signals it. JSON `null` is now unreadable rather than zero, since "no citations recorded" and "this row does not say" are different claims. The category was proven by an exhaustive sweep of the four other JSONB columns and `sessions.memory` rather than asserted.</details> |
| 2026-08-27 | The write-first rule lands at the THREE points an agent meets it (the shared-ledger convention, the sub-agent prompt template, and Rule 4's ordering), rather than as prose in one place | Stating it once in the bossman-mode skill's narrative; building a mechanism that snapshots agent context; accepting the loss as an infrastructure fact and relying on the lead to notice | <details><summary>why</summary>Four agents died in one session on 2026-08-27 and one died mid-sentence holding build phase 4.13's blocking regression, which existed in that agent's context and nowhere on disk. Prose in one place is what already existed and is what failed: this repository's own `attack-the-constraint` standard calls an unenforced mandate an ownerless requirement, and the prompt template is the only surface every dispatched agent provably reads. A context-snapshotting mechanism was rejected because the failure is in the process lifecycle rather than in anything under `src/`, and the portability audit's own conclusion is that anything expressed as a file survives while anything expressed as a mechanism does not. Relying on the lead noticing was rejected because that is what happened, it worked once, and it is a rescue rather than a control: two other agents died the same way in the same session without a finding at stake.</details> |
| 2026-08-27 | Build phase 4.15's develop environment is a SECOND Railway environment inside the existing `system3-search-agent` project, the current environment stays production and keeps the already-shared URL, and develop gets its OWN Postgres and Redis rather than sharing production's | A separate Railway project per environment; repurposing the existing environment as develop and standing up a new production; sharing one Postgres and Redis across both environments; sharing Postgres but splitting Redis | <details><summary>why</summary>Three parts, settled together on 2026-08-27. The TOPOLOGY is not a free choice: Section 24 already names Railway's environment feature, "production plus optionally a staging or preview environment", hosting "a separate variable set per environment, so a dev credential never reaches production", so a second environment in the same project is the specified shape and a second project would be an invention. WHICH ENVIRONMENT KEEPS THE URL was decided on what is already outside this repository: the existing environment is literally named `production`, it holds the Hetzner graph credential, and `search-agent-web-production.up.railway.app` has been handed to people since build phase 4.12, so moving production to a new URL would break a link already in circulation for no gain. THE DATA SPLIT is the only part that spends money, and it was the product owner's call on 2026-08-27: develop gets its own Postgres and Redis. Sharing was rejected on two concrete grounds this build has already measured rather than on principle. First, build phase 4.6 writes an `interactions` row per run and build phases 4.6 and 4.10 count those rows to enforce the per-user, system-wide and anonymous daily caps, so shared data makes every develop run consume a production quota and pollute the very table build phase 4.13's history read renders. Second, `railway.json`'s start command is `alembic upgrade head`, so a shared database means a wrong migration reaches production the moment it is merged to develop, which is the same unguarded promotion build phase 4.14 exists to stop, one layer down. The cost is two managed database services, accepted deliberately.</details>
| 2026-08-27 | REVERSAL of the same day's topology decision: build phase 4.15's two environments become two separate Railway PROJECTS, one per environment, rather than two environments inside `system3-search-agent`. The measurement: after pointing `search-agent-api` at a new branch for production only, BOTH environments read that branch, while `search-agent-web`, untouched, read its own branch in both. The axis of variation is the service. Three independent write paths agree, including Railway's own agent, which stated the constraint plainly. Railway's DOCUMENTATION says the opposite, and that contradiction is recorded rather than resolved: the guide "Isolate staging from production" says each service in each environment has its own deployment trigger, and the likeliest reconciliation is a dashboard-only setting, the same shape as build phase 4.12's dashboard-only Root Directory field. The product owner was given the dashboard route as the first option and chose two projects instead, on 2026-08-27, preferring a topology that works today over one that depends on an unverified console field. Separate services in one project was rejected because it makes Railway's environment feature decorative and pushes the variable-set and database isolation Section 24 wants the platform to enforce back onto a naming convention. Manual promotion was rejected because it removes the merge-triggers-a-deploy property that is the point of the phase. The cost accepted: two dashboards, and Section 24's environment-feature sentence is now describing something this deployment does not use, which is a Step 6.2 reconciliation item rather than an edit to a locked document.</details> |
| 2026-08-27 | The production line's branch is named `production`, and `develop` REMAINS the repository's default branch | `main` as the production branch, which is GitHub's convention and what this phase first created; renaming the default branch to `main` and treating `develop` as a feature-integration branch | <details><summary>why</summary>Product-owner direction on 2026-08-27, in their own words: "production branch should be called production. The phase had already created `main` at `develop`'s head; it was deleted the same day and `production` created in its place, at the identical commit, so nothing was lost. The naming is not cosmetic here. This repository's whole working model is that `develop` is where work lands continuously and production is a deliberate promotion, so a branch called `main` sitting downstream of `develop` inverts what both words normally signal to a reader, and `git-workflow.md` already names `develop` as the branch every phase branch is cut from. Keeping `develop` as the GitHub default matters for the same reason: it is what a clone checks out, what a pull request targets by default, and what the product owner sees first.</details> |
| 2026-08-28 | Build phase 4.15's release automation is a GitHub Actions workflow built from `git log` and `gh` with NO new dependency, rather than release-please, git-cliff or standard-version | `release-please`, Google's action, which is the most common tool for exactly this; `git-cliff` or `standard-version` as a pinned changelog generator inside a hand-written workflow; documenting the release ritual as commands to run by hand with no automation at all | <details><summary>why</summary>Product-owner choice on 2026-08-27 from four presented options. The deciding factor was FLOW SHAPE rather than effort: `release-please` works by keeping a long-lived release pull request open and merging it when you want to ship, which is a different model from the release branch the product owner asked for in their own words ("I cut a release branch, and that release branch merges into production"). Adopting it would have meant adopting its flow. The supply-chain cost was the second factor and it is this repository's standing rule rather than a preference: `supply-chain-security` requires a review gate for any new dependency, and a third-party action running in CI with `contents: write` and `pull-requests: write` on the production line is the highest-trust surface in the repository. The hand-written path costs more code to own, which is real, and it is offset by every step being a checked-in script this repository already lints, tests and mutation-tests. Hand-documenting with no automation was rejected under `attack-the-constraint`: an unenforced ritual is an ownerless requirement, and the product owner had asked for CI/CD to be configured rather than described.</details> |
| 2026-08-28 | The back-merge pull request's missing CI is DISCLOSED rather than closed, and no personal access token enters the release path | Switching `open_backmerge_pr.sh` to a personal access token or a GitHub App token, either of which would make the pull request raise workflow events and run the gates; pushing the back-merge directly to `develop` instead of opening a pull request; dropping the automated back-merge and doing it by hand | <details><summary>why</summary>Product-owner decision on 2026-08-28, taken after a judge and an adversary filed the same gap independently (F-4.15-J-10, F-4.15-A-12). `gh pr create` authenticated with the workflow's built-in `GITHUB_TOKEN` does not raise events that start workflow runs, which is GitHub's recursion guard working as designed and is also what stops the release workflow triggering itself. The gap is narrower than it first reads: the content of that pull request is code that already passed CI on the release pull request, so what is missing is a re-check of the MERGE, not unreviewed code arriving on the default branch. Against that, a personal access token with write access, stored as a secret and used by an automated job on the production line, is a permanent increase in blast radius to close a temporary and narrow gap. The disclosure is made in three places a reader will actually be, rather than in a tracker row nobody opens: the script's own header, the body of every pull request it opens, and `docs/build/Release_flow.md`. Direct push was rejected because a back-merge can conflict, and an automatic conflicting push to the default branch is worse than a pull request someone reviews.</details> |

The split does not reopen that, because every commit still reaches the reader either as a bullet or inside a stated number, and the check now proves bullets plus counted equals total rather than bullets equals total. Silence was the defect; brevity with a number is not. Proven in a throwaway repository in both directions, exit 1 with a group dropped from the emit list and exit 0 with it intact. The collapsed-block option was rejected because the bytes stay in the release body, which is what GitHub truncates at 125,000 characters, so it would have looked fixed while leaving the real limit in play.</details> |

LENGTH WAS THE SMALLER PROBLEM. 173 of those 280 bullets sat under Fixes and every one of them predates the first release, so the changelog was telling a reader about 173 bugs fixed in a product nobody had used. That is not merely noisy, it is false in what it implies, and no amount of filtering fixes it because the commits genuinely are of type `fix`. The convention for a first release is a summary of what the thing does, which is both shorter and true. The file's preamble said not to hand-edit a released section, so the preamble now states the exception and the entry explains itself in its own text rather than leaving the next reader to wonder why one section looks unlike every other. FIELD AVAILABILITY: Section 20.3 requires HTTP status and latency on every line, and both exist ONLY at the transport, since the numeric status is classified to a three-value ok, empty or error status inside the action modules and discarded before a tool returns, and nothing times a call. | 2026-08-29 | Tracing requires BOTH the tracing flag and an API key before it is enabled, not the flag alone | Honouring `LANGCHAIN_TRACING_V2` on its own, which is what the installed langsmith does by default | <details><summary>why</summary>That flag has read `true` in this repository's `.env` and `env.example` since long before tracing was built, so on its own it carries no operator intent. | 2026-08-29 | The audit sink's error field is BOUNDED rather than scanned, and the same move was then applied to the LangSmith error path | A fourth round of hardening the string scanner, which had already been beaten three times | <details><summary>why</summary>Product-owner decision, 2026-08-29, after F-5.0-13, F-5.0-14 and F-5.0-19 each defeated a scanner with an input of the same family: something upstream consumed the credential before the check saw it. The same question was then asked of the LangSmith path and answered the same way: the message is discarded and only an anchored class name at position 0 is kept, so there is no pattern left to defeat. This is `attack-the-constraint` applied to a check: when a check keeps losing to inputs of one shape, change what it is checking.</details> |
| 2026-08-29 | PostHog analytics require a `phc_` project token; `phx_` and `phs_` are refused by name and anything unrecognised fails closed | Accepting any non-empty key, which is what the code did until the judge measured it | <details><summary>why</summary>A `phx_` personal key is account-wide read and write, where event capture needs only append. `ai-security-standards` forbids wiring a full-access credential to an integration that needs a scoped one, and the capture endpoint authenticates on the project token anyway.
