Waiting on an external reviewer who was never named had already blocked the phase, and an unnamed dependency is an ownerless requirement under `attack-the-constraint`. The consequence is recorded rather than glossed: the sign-off's authority is now bounded by the product owner's own domain confidence, and build phase 5.1 states that where it states the dataset's provenance, so a later reader knows what the sign-off does and does not certify.</details> |
| 2026-08-30 | The three JUnit reports the Section 24 gates write are gitignored BY NAME, one line each | A `*-results.xml` glob, or leaving them untracked as they were | <details><summary>why</summary>Two of them, `unit-results.xml` and `required-paths.xml`, turned up untracked while closing build phase 5.0, and grepping the gate scripts found a third, `integration-results.xml`, that had simply never been produced on that machine. Three instances is a class rather than an oversight, so all three were covered at once. A glob was rejected on two grounds: `*-results.xml` does not match `required-paths.xml` anyway, so it would have fixed two of three while looking complete, which is the confident-incompleteness shape this phase kept hitting; and a pattern wide enough to catch all three would quietly swallow a file somebody meant to commit. The cost of the explicit list is that a new gate writing a report needs a line adding, and that is the intended trade: adding a line is a visible edit in a diff, while a silently ignored file is not. Leaving them untracked was rejected because `git-workflow` already forbids `git add -A`, but a build artifact sitting in `git status` is noise in exactly the list a person scans before committing.</details> |
| 2026-08-30 | The gate 2 import-order fix keeps the `# noqa: F401` on ONE name, using a plain `import package.module` statement, rather than taking isort's own converged output | Committing isort's output as produced, or adding the file to an isort ignore list | <details><summary>why</summary>isort is NOT idempotent on this input, measured rather than assumed: its first pass splits the statement in two and its second merges it back with the comment hoisted onto the `import (` line, where it suppresses the unused-import check for all three imported names instead of the one that needs it. That converged form passes gate 2 AND gate 3, so it looks like the answer, and taking it would have traded a red gate for a quietly weakened one, which `goal-contracts` forbids outright: a check that silently covers less than it did is the same failed run as changing the check so the check passes. Three forms were measured. A narrower `from ... import <name>  # noqa` on its own line fails BOTH gates. isort's converged form passes both and broadens the suppression. A plain `import system_03_search_agent.tools.graph_http_transport  # noqa: F401` as its own statement passes both and keeps the comment on exactly one name, because isort keeps a different statement kind separate. An ignore-list entry was rejected on the same ground as the converged form: it makes the gate green by making it check less. The general rule, recorded because it will recur: a formatter's output is a proposal, not an answer, and when a tool is non-idempotent its second pass is a different proposal from its first.</details> |
| 2026-08-30 | The golden dataset pins CONSTRAINT ASSERTIONS authored independently of the agent (required CURIEs, required source records, forbidden behaviours, expected outcome class), never full reference prose and never the agent's own curated output | Writing 50 full reference answers by hand; or running the agent over the 50, correcting what looks wrong, and freezing that as expected | <details><summary>why</summary>Product-owner decision, 2026-08-30, opening build phase 5.1. Curating from the agent's own runs was rejected as CIRCULAR: anything currently wrong that the reviewer does not personally catch becomes the certified correct answer permanently, behind a green gate, which is the precise failure `requirements/phase_6/Continuation_prompt.md` flags when it says a wrong expected answer certifies a wrong agent forever. Full reference prose was rejected on maintainability rather than on rigour: pinning prose for LLM-generated output breaks the fixture every time the agent rewords a CORRECT answer, and a fixture that fails on correct behaviour creates standing pressure to weaken the check, which `.claude/rules/goal-contracts.md` names as a failed run rather than a completed one. Constraint assertions separate the two jobs cleanly: the dataset pins what must be TRUE and is established from a live NCBI or graph lookup, while the playbook's 8-point rubric grades how well it was said. Every row carries a provenance field naming the live source the constraint was read from, the date, and the bound on the sign-off, namely that the product owner signed rather than an external clinical or human-variation reviewer.</details> |
| 2026-08-30 | Build phase 5.1 SPLIT after review: the 50-query golden dataset ships as 5.1, the grading harness becomes build phase 5.2 | Fixing the harness inside 5.1 and merging both together; or merging both as-is and fixing the harness later | <details><summary>why</summary>Product-owner decision, 2026-08-30, after both independent review rounds returned FAIL. The judge filed 15 findings (4 critical) and the adversary 26 (9 critical), and every verdict was against the GRADER. Both rounds passed the dataset and both independently re-verified all 50 rows against live NCBI, finding every identifier current and every symbol matched. The measured headline against the real shipped dataset: an answer whose entire prose is the word "Fabricated." passes 34 of the 50 rows, and an agent that refuses all 50 questions scores pass@3 and pass^3 of 100 percent. Merging both as-is was rejected outright: a harness that certifies fabricated answers is worse than no harness, because CI would be green while the instrument blessed bad output, and the whole project is meant to trust it. Fixing inside 5.1 was rejected on sequencing rather than on scope: the two-round review budget was already spent, Rule 4 had fired (adversary finding A-5.1-05 sits inside the F-5.1-01 fix made earlier the same session), and holding a twice-verified dataset hostage to a harness rewrite delays the one artifact that is already correct and expensive to reproduce. The split is a deliberate exception to Section 25's build order, in the same class as the five exceptions tracker/BOARD.md already records, and Section 25 stays unedited per v1-scope-boundary. Nothing was deleted and nothing was weakened: all seven harness modules, 25 premise arms and 28 mutation cases moved to 5.2 verbatim, the 41 findings keep their original 5.1 identifiers so the trail stays readable, and both review reports move with them.</details> |
| 2026-08-30 | OpenRouter's Auto Router is DEFERRED to build phases 7.0 and 7.1 rather than adopted now, and is recorded as a candidate there | Adopting `openrouter/auto` for one or more tiers now; or rejecting it outright | <details><summary>why</summary>Product-owner decision, 2026-08-30, raised after OpenRouter introduced it. WHAT IT DOES, read from the vendor documentation rather than assumed: a caller sets `model` to `openrouter/auto`; a lightweight classifier assigns the prompt one of about 30 task types; the router then picks from the models the OpenRouter community actually spends on over a trailing 7-day window; the model that served the request is reported back in the response's `model` field; there is no additional fee beyond the selected model's own rate. It is explicitly NON-DETERMINISTIC and "can pick a different model on every turn", with session stickiness inside a conversation. WHY NOT NOW, and the reason is timing rather than merit: build phase 5.1 and 5.2 are building the instrument that measures answer quality, and a moving model makes that instrument unable to attribute anything. `pass^k` is a reliability figure across k samples, and if each sample runs on a different model it measures the router rather than the agent. The retrieval-consistency metric added at T-5.2-14 has the same problem one layer down. Separately, `.claude/rules/prompt-cache-discipline.md` requires a tier's model to be resolved once at query start and held for the query's duration, and one query makes roughly six model calls. `system-design-patterns` pattern 11 puts model identity in the harness, resolved from environment-configured tier values, and says to freeze the model and iterate the harness on a recurring failure. Selection by community spend-share is also a POPULARITY signal across all OpenRouter traffic, not a fitness signal for biomedical evidence assembly under cite-or-refuse. WHERE IT DOES BELONG, and this is the product owner's own framing: build phase 7.0 is model-bench, which benchmarks candidate models per tier against the golden dataset and picks the tier winners, so `openrouter/auto` is a legitimate CANDIDATE there and can be measured against a pinned model rather than argued about. Build phase 7.1 is the A and B randomized-routing mechanism, where it is a natural arm. A THIRD USE IS DISTINCT FROM BOTH AND WORTH KEEPING SEPARATE: as a FALLBACK when a pinned tier model is unavailable or rate-limited, which is degradation resilience rather than model selection, and which does not disturb determinism on the ordinary path. The response's `model` field makes any of the three auditable, since build phase 5.0's audit log can record which model actually served each call.</details> |
| 2026-08-30 | Build phase 5.2, the eval harness grading, is PARKED unmerged after four failed review rounds | A fifth fix round rebuilding grounding on the dataset's live-verified values; or dropping deterministic grounding and letting a judge own correctness | <details><summary>why</summary>Product-owner decision, 2026-08-30. THE REASON IS NOT THE FAILURE RATE, and that distinction matters for whoever picks this up. Four rounds returned FAIL with roughly ninety findings, and that alone would argue for a fifth round rather than a park. The actual reason is that THE 50 QUESTIONS ARE A FIRST ATTEMPT, not a settled target: this is a prototype, the product owner is not yet confident the golden questions are the right ones, and a grader precise enough to catch an invented fact about the right gene is precision spent against a moving specification. The instrument cannot be more settled than the thing it measures. By `.claude/rules/attack-the-constraint.md`, the bottleneck is the question set, not the grader, and the four rounds were optimising a non-bottleneck. WHAT SURVIVES: build phase 5.1, the 50-query golden dataset, is merged to develop as PR #85 and passed all four review rounds with every identifier independently re-verified against live NCBI. That is the expensive half and it stands. WHAT DOES NOT: the grading harness on `phase/5.2-eval-harness-grading` is KNOWN BROKEN and must not be merged or trusted. Its own suite is green with every defect live, which is precisely why it is dangerous. THE ROOT DEFECT, recorded because it is the useful part: grounding compared the agent's prose against the agent's own citation payload, so it measured self-consistency rather than grounding, and an invented gene citing an invented record scored 16 of 16. The same circularity was deliberately designed OUT of the dataset builder, which verifies on an independent path against live NCBI, and then designed back INTO the grader one file over.</details> |
| 2026-08-30 | Build phase 5.2 is MERGED to develop WHILE PARKED, superseding the same day's "must not be merged" line | Leaving it unmerged on its branch indefinitely; or deleting the branch and losing the work and the four review reports | <details><summary>why</summary>Product-owner decision, 2026-08-30, taken after reviewing pull request #86 and stated in their own terms: "this is a prototype. I am not 100 percent sure what the exact golden dataset questions should be. This is our attempt to get going. So parking it is ok in my opinion." THIS SUPERSEDES the earlier row the same day which said the branch must not be merged. That row is left unedited, per `.claude/rules/decision-logging.md`'s rule that existing entries are a historical record and are never modified; the supersession is recorded here instead so both the original judgement and its reversal stay readable. WHAT MERGING MEANS AND DOES NOT MEAN: the code, the four review reports, the two learnings and the full record of why the harness failed land on develop rather than being stranded on a branch that would rot. THE PHASE'S DELIVERABLE STILL DOES NOT EXIST: there is no harness that can grade an answer, and the evaluation gate over the 50 questions still cannot run. WHY IT IS SAFE, verified rather than assumed: the `eval` package is DORMANT, since nothing outside it imports it, no adapter reaches it and no CI gate runs it. On top of that `replay()` raises `HarnessParkedError` unless the caller passes `acknowledge_parked=True`, and the message names the specific defect rather than saying "known issues", which is `system-design-patterns` pattern 8 applied to a parked component: removing the ability rather than asking the next reader not to use it. `tracker/phase_5.2.md` opens with a do-not-trust banner and the board row is `blocked`.</details> |
| 2026-08-30 | A check that cannot reach a verdict returns NOT MEASURABLE with a reason, rather than a score | Scoring it as passing when the data is absent; or not building the check at all until the data exists | <details><summary>why</summary>Product-owner decision, 2026-08-30, taken while designing build phase 5.2's freshness check. Section 7.4 sets staleness thresholds per FIELD CLASS, and finding F-3.4-T06-01 records that this graph's vertices carry only generic BioLink properties, so no citation names a field class and the threshold cannot be chosen. Scoring it as fresh in that situation is the dead-check defect this project has now produced three separate times: a premise arm that could not fail, a coverage metric reporting 0 percent for a quantity nothing could observe, and a truncation constraint reported as checked while reading a field its own contract forbids. THE DISTINCTION THAT MATTERS: "we could not tell" and "we looked and it was fine" are different facts, and only one of them is a measurement. A check that conflates them reads as protection that is not there. Building it now rather than deferring means it starts working the day the ingest carries a richer per-domain property, with no code change, and says so out loud until then. The same treatment was applied the same day to the coverage metric, which now reports NOT MEASURABLE rather than 0 percent.</details> |
| 2026-08-30 | The branch steady state is `develop` locally, and `develop` plus `production` on the remote | Leaving merged branches in place as historical pointers, which is what several earlier phases did | <details><summary>why</summary>Product-owner decision, 2026-08-30. `.claude/rules/git-workflow.md` step 5 already said "delete the phase branch after merge", and it was read as optional: three pull requests were merged the same day with `--delete-branch=false` and the local checkout was left sitting on a merged phase branch, which the product owner had to notice and ask about. THE DEPARTURE WAS THE ERROR RATHER THAN THE LEFTOVER BRANCHES: a convention that exists in one line and is departed from silently is not enforcement. Stating the steady state makes a stray branch a question rather than background noise, since anything outside `develop` locally, or `develop` and `production` on the remote, is then either uncleaned or genuinely in flight, with no third category. The safe order is recorded with it: merge with deletion on, return to `develop` and fast-forward, confirm the branch is an ancestor of `origin/develop`, delete with `git branch -d` rather than `-D` so git refuses anything unmerged, then delete the remote copy. Keeping a branch after merge stays allowed, provided it is said out loud at merge time, because silence reads as having followed the rule. Written into the rule itself as pull request #88.</details> |
| 2026-08-31 | The 50-query golden dataset is v1, derived from data alone, and the settled set is data plus SME input | Treating the merged 50 as the golden dataset and calibrating the grader against it; or blocking all eval work until an SME is engaged | <details><summary>why</summary>Product-owner decision, 2026-08-31, stated directly: "this golden dataset is just a start based on the data that we found. Nothing else. Data + actually SME input will determine what should be the right answers and that is where we form the golden dataset. This is v1." THIS DEMOTES WHAT BUILD PHASE 5.1 DELIVERED, and the demotion is the point rather than a caveat: 5.1 shipped the data-derived v1 of a golden dataset, not the golden dataset. Every constraint in it was read from a live NCBI lookup and independently re-verified, so the rows are FACTUALLY sound; what no lookup can supply is whether the question is worth asking and whether the expected answer is the one a domain expert would give. The dataset's own provenance block already records `signed_off_by` as the product owner rather than a clinical or human-variation reviewer, and the open item "Golden fixture domain sign-off, nobody is named to verify the clinical and human-variation expected answers" has been open since 5.1 was refined. That item is now THE GATING ITEM for a v2, not a footnote. Two consequences follow directly. Build phase 5.2's grader stays parked, and this is a second independent reason for it beyond the four failed review rounds: precision aimed at a set that SME review will move is precision spent twice. And no downstream gate may describe a pass against these 50 as evidence the agent is correct, only as evidence it is consistent with the data we found.</details> |
| 2026-08-31 | A golden row pins BOTH the retrieval path (`must_reach`) and a live-only fact in the answer | Pinning only the path, which grades how rather than whether; or pinning only a live-only fact, which needs no schema change but cannot be counted per tool | <details><summary>why</summary>Product-owner decision, 2026-08-31, after the measurement below was put to them. THE MEASUREMENT: across all 50 rows, LitVar2 and PubTator3 are required by ZERO rows, ClinicalTrials.gov by two, Pathogen Detection by two, and 38 of 50 rows are gene-variant-literature. That alone reads as thin coverage. The real defect is one layer deeper and is why more questions would not have fixed it: THE SCHEMA CANNOT EXPRESS THE REQUIREMENT AT ALL. The only constraint fields are `must_resolve` and `must_cite`, and `must_cite` pins a URL. A citation to `ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/gene/672` is satisfiable straight from the Layer 1 graph node's own `source_url` with no live call ever made, so even the eight rows citing PubMed and the four citing dbSNP can be passed entirely from the graph snapshot. The dataset could not distinguish an agent that federated three layers from one that only read the graph. BOTH mechanisms were chosen over either alone because they fail differently and cross-check each other: `must_reach` makes per-tool coverage countable and is easy to author but grades the path rather than the answer and breaks whenever routing legitimately changes; a live-only fact (a value post-dating the graph snapshot) cannot be faked by a graph-only agent and needs no schema change, but ages as the snapshot moves and cannot be counted. A row that satisfies the path assertion while failing the fact means the tool WAS called and its result was discarded, which is a distinct defect worth being able to see. Note the standing architectural caveat: the target architecture serves everything from Layer 1, so this requirement is explicitly for the current stage, not permanent.</details> |
| 2026-08-31 | `search_category` and `query_class` stay two separate axes; KISS is not a synonym for `lookup` | Collapsing them into one field, on the reading that a known item search and a lookup are the same thing | <details><summary>why</summary>Raised by the product owner on 2026-08-31 as "KISS = known item search and lookup are the exact same thing, correct me if I am wrong", and the correction is recorded rather than the conclusion alone. THE ACRONYM IS RIGHT and the three must-have types are right: KISS is Known Item Search, KISSES is its plural, discovery is the conversation, and those three are already the dataset's primary taxonomy. What is not right is the equivalence. `search_category` is WHAT THE USER WANTS BACK and decides the grading metric (KISS scores precision, KISSES scores recall plus honest truncation disclosure, discovery is graded across the thread). `query_class` is HOW THE AGENT MUST RETRIEVE IT, inherited from technical specification Section 17. THE DATA SEPARATES THEM: 10 of the 24 KISS rows are not `lookup` class, including G-010 (one exact answer, four hops), G-034 (one exact answer, aggregate retrieval) and six single-hop rows; and 2 `lookup` rows are not KISS, G-012 being one tool call that wants every result and G-039 being one tool call that opens a conversation. Collapsing the axes would lose exactly the case most worth measuring, a known item search that is EXPENSIVE to reach, which is where a confidently wrong answer is most likely. `search_category` stays primary, as the product owner has it.</details> |
| 2026-08-31 | The debugging guide is ONE document, a symptom index followed by a file inventory, not two | Splitting the reference (what every file does) from the playbook (symptom to file), which is the cleaner separation of concerns | <details><summary>why</summary>Two documents means two index rows in each of five places, two surfaces that can drift apart, and a reader with a bug who has to know which one to open first. The audience is a developer or an agent landing cold, and both want one entry point. The repository's house style already favours tables over prose, so a 120-row inventory sitting under a symptom index reads fine in one file. Ordering carries the weight: the symptom index comes FIRST, because a reader arrives with a failure rather than with a desire to browse the tree.</details> |
| 2026-08-31 | Currency of the file inventory is enforced by a TEST, not by a rule in `.claude/rules/` | A 23rd always-loaded rule stating the obligation; a one-off manual check at write time; an arm added to `check_doc_drift.py` | <details><summary>why</summary>`attack-the-constraint` names an unenforced mandate as an ownerless requirement, and `communication-style` records that adding content to an always-loaded surface is not an optimization. A rule would have inflated every turn's context to restate what two assertions enforce mechanically. `tests/system_03_search_agent/test_debugging_guide_coverage.py` rides CI gate 4 with no wiring at all, which `check_doc_drift.py` would have needed. THE ARMS EARNED IT BEFORE THE DOCUMENT EXISTED: arm 1 caught three uncovered files in the assembled draft, one of them `tools/__init__.py` where the read-plus-one-source tool contract is written down, and arm 2 then caught a phantom path written by hand. Arm 3 watches a hash of each file's docstring summary line, since a path check cannot see that a file's JOB changed. Each arm ships with a mutation case in the same edit, per the rule build phase 4.15 produced.</details> |
| 2026-08-31 | The guide names SYMBOLS and never line numbers | Citing `core/graph.py:3012`, which is more precise and saves the reader a grep | <details><summary>why</summary>A line number is a claim that goes stale silently on the next edit, and this repository has already shipped four defects that were a confident sentence describing a check that was not there. A symbol name is greppable, self-correcting, and teaches the habit the guide is built around. The cost is one `grep -rn` per lookup. The one number that survived, `core/graph.py`'s size, was challenged by an auditor for exactly this reason and was rewritten from "about a fifth of the package" to the measured 5947 of 63102 lines, since the original proportion did not reproduce.</details> |
| 2026-08-31 | Build phase 4.3's historical test count is restored to 3308, NOT develop's hedged 4652 | Keeping 4652 with the words "as measured then", which is what `phase/5.3` did to the same line independently | <details><summary>why</summary>Both branches hit the same corrupted line in `requirements/phase_6/Phase_6_history.md` and fixed it in opposite directions. The true value is 3308, proven from commit `f52761f` where build phase 4.3's close was recorded, and again from `a02936e`. Commit `5249f36` overwrote it with the then-current 4652 while relocating the section out of the continuation prompt. Adding "as measured then" makes a number that was NEVER measured at that phase read as though it was, which is worse than the bare wrong number because it adds false authority. This is `goal-contracts`'s never-corrupt-the-subject rule broken in the direction that looks like diligence, and it is the second recorded instance in this repository.</details> |
| 2026-08-31 | The environment table is hand-written and auditor-checked, NOT mechanically generated | Deriving the whole table from a script over `env.example` plus a grep of `os.environ` and `os.getenv` | <details><summary>why</summary>The generator was written and then DISCARDED after measurement, which is why this is a decision rather than a preference. It reported `GUARD_MODEL`, `LANGSMITH_API_KEY` and `POSTHOG_API_KEY` as read by nothing, because it could not follow the indirection those reads go through: a name built from a tier, or a list of candidate variants in `observability/config.py`. A mechanically wrong table is worse than a hand-checked one, since it carries the authority of derivation. The residual risk is stated plainly: this is the one section of the guide no arm watches, and nine of one audit round's fourteen defects sat in it. That correlation was found by the auditor, not the author.</details> |
| 2026-08-31 | A THIRD `doc-auditor` round was run past the two-round cap, on falling severity | Shipping after round 2, which the cap permits once the owner is told | <details><summary>why</summary>Authorised by the product owner. Severity fell monotonically, 14 defects then 8, which is the condition build phase 4.11 established for continuing past the cap. Round 3 found 8 more, only one of which was a wrong pointer, so the returns were genuinely diminishing and the document shipped after it. TWO PROCESS FAILURES ARE RECORDED RATHER THAN TIDIED AWAY, because they weaken round 2's verdict: its brief told the auditor it was a second round, which leaks prior-round knowledge into a context that `self-eval-loop` requires to be blind, and the document was EDITED WHILE THAT ROUND GRADED IT. The auditor flagged both itself. Round 3 ran with a clean brief against a frozen file, which is the shape every round should have had.</details> |
| 2026-08-31 | Build phases 6.0 and 6.1 go BEFORE any UI fixes, and UI feedback is collected into `UI_feedback.md` until it is complete, then fixed in ONE pass | Fixing the UI first, which is what the assistant recommended on the grounds that a correct but unreadable answer makes every other item worth less | <details><summary>why</summary>Product-owner decision, overriding the assistant's recommendation, and the product owner's reasoning is stronger on the thing the recommendation was not weighing. A piecemeal UI pass leaves some issues fixed and some live, and every partial pass forces a re-test of the whole surface, which is worse than one coherent pass against a complete list. The feedback is also not finished being collected: the app has barely been used, and the list will grow. THE EVIDENCE THAT PROMPTED THIS: a live browser run against production on 2026-08-31 captured the answer screen, and the page is competent while the answer it renders is `MedGen:C0346153, MedGen:C2676676, MedGen:C3280442, and MedGen:C4554406`, four opaque identifiers where a researcher needs disease names. Screenshot at `docs/build/design/evidence/2026-08-31_live_answer_brca1.png`. THE ONE STANDING CAVEAT, recorded rather than assumed away: if 6.0 and 6.1 run long enough that the product is shown to anyone before the UI pass, the answer-readability item should be revisited on its own, because it is the single item that makes every demonstration fail regardless of how good the rest is.</details> |
| 2026-08-31 | Build phase 6.0's RATE ceiling is allowed to bind before its CALL ceiling, so a `lookup` query against E-utilities fails fast at roughly the sixth sequential call rather than reaching Section 21.3's twentieth | Widening the `lookup` queue wait ceiling from 1.5 to about 7 seconds so the 20-call ceiling becomes reachable; or adding a third, smaller bound on how many sequential calls one tool may chain | <details><summary>why</summary>The two ceilings were built in the same phase and were found to interact by the phase's own premise gate, not by review (F-6.0-02): a `lookup` query derives a 1.5 second queue wait ceiling from Section 21.4 while the `eutils` pool paces at 3 requests per second, so scheduled wait accumulates and the pool refuses at about the sixth call. Both figures follow the specification and neither is wrong alone. Keeping it is what Section 21.4 literally describes, that a short-budget query "accepts only a short queue wait" and fails fast rather than queueing, and the direction of failure is safe: the call is refused, Act degrades, and Write synthesizes from what arrived. Widening to 7 seconds was rejected because it contradicts Section 21.4's own stated "one to two seconds" for this class and would let a user's shortest query sit 7 seconds in a queue against a shared NCBI pool before its first byte. The third bound was rejected as scope Section 21 does not ask for. The cost is stated rather than hidden: Section 21.3's 20-call ceiling is effectively unreachable for the commonest query class on E-utilities, so it guards the longer query classes and mid-tool fan-out rather than the typical query. Product-owner decision.</details> |
| 2026-08-31 | Section 21.4's named open question is CONFIRMED as written: no jump-the-queue behaviour in v1. Every call is served FIFO within its family, and query class affects only how long a call is willing to wait, never its position | Cancelling a run's queued calls when the user presses Stop, so they stop holding shared NCBI pool slots other users are waiting on | <details><summary>why</summary>Section 21.4 recorded this as "an assumption to confirm, not a silently settled decision", and build phase 6.0 is the phase that owns Section 21, so it is the phase that should close it rather than let it ride a third time. The specification's own reasoning was re-checked rather than taken on trust and it still holds: the guardrail runs before Think, and Think is where the first Layer 2 call is issued (build phase 5.0 proved that by execution, the first audit line a live query ever wrote came from `think_node`'s symbol resolution), so a guardrail rejection cannot have in-flight calls to cancel. There is nothing to build. The Stop button case is different and is genuinely reachable today, unlike the guardrail case the specification reasoned about, but it is new scope Section 21 does not ask for and it would extend a phase whose two real gaps are already closed; it is recorded here so a later phase can pick it up knowing it was considered rather than missed. Product-owner decision.</details> |
| 2026-08-31 | Build phases 6.0 and 6.1 move BEHIND the prototype, reversing that morning's ordering, and the disease-name defect becomes the next work | Keeping 6.0 and 6.1 ahead of any UI work, which is what was decided earlier the same day | <details><summary>why</summary>A live browser run that afternoon showed what an answer looks like to a person: `MedGen:C0346153, MedGen:C2676676, MedGen:C3280442`. Three opaque identifiers where disease names should be. Build phase 6.0 delivers contention protection, which is invisible with one user and which the phase's own record shows is mostly unreachable for the commonest query class anyway. Hardening a product whose core answer nobody can read is optimizing a non-bottleneck, which is exactly what `.claude/rules/attack-the-constraint.md` exists to prevent, and the evidence for the real bottleneck was already sitting in `UI_feedback.md` while 6.0 was being built. The sequence is now: make the prototype usable, put it in front of people, hear back, then harden. What makes the disease-name fix the right next move rather than merely the loudest complaint is that it is cheap: `ncbi_efetch` already runs in that same query as the second tool and already reaches MedGen, so the machinery to turn a CURIE into a name is already in the loop and simply is not used to label the answer. Product-owner decision.</details> |
| 2026-08-31 | Build phase 6.0 MERGES with eight of its nine judge findings open, three of them critical, and with NO adversary round run | Fixing all nine before merge, which is the harness's normal requirement; or holding the branch unmerged until an adversary round ran | <details><summary>why</summary>The three criticals say one thing three ways: the gate does not pin the production wiring. The whole Section 21.4 wiring can be deleted and every test stays green (F-6.0-J-05); the arm claiming to measure Section 21.2 builds its own limiter and never touches the shared registry (F-6.0-J-07); removing both production scope bindings changes no test (F-6.0-J-09). That is TEST DEBT rather than a broken product, and the distinction is load-bearing for this decision: both features are verified working by execution rather than by the gate, the wait ceiling having been proven wired by a premise arm failing against it live during the build. One finding was fixed before merge, F-6.0-J-03, and only that one, because it alone made answers strictly worse: the ceiling guard refused Layer 1 graph calls, so a query that spent its budget in Think answered with zero graph rows and refused where a partial cited answer was available. The adversary round was stopped mid-flight rather than skipped unknowingly. Once 6.0 was established as not the constraint, spending an adversary round on it would have compounded the original misallocation. All eight open findings carry named owners in `requirements/Plan.md` Phase 7 and rows in `tracker/BOARD.md`, so closed does not read as finished. Product-owner decision.</details> |
| 2026-08-31 | THE UNIT OF WORK CHANGES from the numbered build phase to open flags plus UI feedback. From here on, work is picked from `tracker/BOARD.md`'s flags table and `UI_feedback.md`, not from Section 25's build order | Continuing to open numbered build phases, which is what the harness, the board and `bossman-mode` are all built around | <details><summary>why</summary>Section 25's build order has run its course as a driver: every numbered phase has merged or moved to Phase 7, and the board now carries no open phase at all. What remains is of two kinds, and neither is phase-shaped. Findings attached to code, which are conditional and become work only when someone touches that code. And defects a real person hit on the live site, which is `UI_feedback.md`. Build phase 6.0 is the argument for the change. It was opened because the board said it was next, it delivered contention protection invisible with one user, and measuring its own specification section first showed five of eight requirements were already built. Meanwhile the defect that makes answers unreadable sat in `UI_feedback.md` throughout. A phase number is a poor proxy for value once the spec is mostly built, and following one cost a session. WHAT THIS DOES NOT CHANGE, stated so it is not quietly lost: the premise gate before code, the judge round, the write-first rule for findings, and the goal contract all still apply to a piece of work, whatever it is called. The cadence in `docs/build/Build_workflow_cadence.md` is scoped to a build phase and now needs a smaller sibling for flag-sized work; that is not yet written and is recorded here rather than assumed. WHAT WOULD REVERSE IT: a genuinely phase-sized deliverable, the most likely being whatever user feedback asks for that does not exist yet. Section 25 stays the source of truth for the numbered phases that did run, and `tracker/BOARD.md` never invents a phase. Product-owner decision.</details> |
| 2026-09-01 | The contents of `UI_feedback.md` become BUILD PHASE 6.2, a numbered phase on the board with fourteen tickets, reversing the previous day's decision that the unit of work was no longer a build phase | Working the items as loose flags picked one at a time, which is what the 2026-08-31 decision set up; or opening only the disease-name fix and leaving the five interface complaints unscoped | <details><summary>why</summary>The 2026-08-31 decision named its own reversal condition in the same paragraph, "a genuinely phase-sized deliverable", and this is that deliverable. The product owner's reasoning from `UI_feedback.md` is the argument: fixing these piecemeal leaves some fixed and some live and forces a re-test of the whole surface each time, and this is a show piece that needs to be right as a whole rather than right in patches. A phase also buys the three controls a loose flag does not: a premise gate written before the code and watched failing, a judge round, and one goal contract covering the whole surface rather than fourteen separate ones. NUMBERING: 6.2 rather than 4.17, because 4.x is the delivery-surfaces group and this is not a delivery surface, and 6.1 is a real Section 25 phase that still exists in the specification so its number is not free. THE EXCEPTION IS STATED RATHER THAN CROSSED SILENTLY: Section 25 has no row for defects a live user hits, the same gap build phase 4.16 was inserted to fill, so this is the sixth stated exception on `tracker/BOARD.md`. Both locked documents stay unedited until the next reconciliation, per `v1-scope-boundary`. Product-owner decision.</details> |
| 2026-09-01 | The disease-name fix RESOLVES MedGen concept ids to names through Layer 2 at query time, and the corrupted graph `name` field is left exactly as it is | Exposing the graph's `name` property to the Cypher generator, which was the first hypothesis in `UI_feedback.md`; or weakening `_is_vocabulary_token_artifact` so the existing value survives into the answer; or waiting for a System 1/2 re-ingest | <details><summary>why</summary>Measured against the live graph before choosing: Disease `name` holds the SOURCE VOCABULARY, taking three distinct values across 25 sampled nodes (`MedGen`, `MeSH`, `SNOMEDCT_US`), while Gene `name` works correctly with 23 distinct values across 23 nodes. So exposing `name` to the generator would have changed four identifiers into the word `SNOMEDCT_US` four times, which is worse. Weakening the detector was rejected for the same reason plus a stronger one: the detector is the trust moat working correctly, and this repository's own record shows the moat and the defect are one mechanism, so removing the guard ships a confident wrong answer instead of an unreadable right one. Waiting for a re-ingest was rejected because the ETL parser is System 1 and 2's, lives in another repository, is forbidden here by `file-protection.md`, and is not on a schedule. THE BRIEF WAS CORRECTED IN THE PROCESS, and this is the part most likely to be wrongly simplified back: `UI_feedback.md` and the continuation prompt both say `ncbi_efetch` already reaches MedGen in that query so the fix is close to wiring, and both told the reader to verify it. Verified, and it is half true. A MedGen ESummary call keyed on a concept id is REJECTED by NCBI, `{"error":"Invalid uid C0346153 at position= 0"}`. Resolution takes TWO calls: ESearch on `C0346153[ConceptId]` returns UID `87542`, then ESummary on `87542` returns `title="Familial cancer of breast"`. What survives of the claim is the load-bearing half, that both actions already exist in the shipped tool with `medgen` on both db enums and `title` already on the `medgen` field allowlist, so it stays wiring rather than building. What does not survive is that it is free: two extra live calls on a path already taking 12 to 14 seconds makes caching part of the ticket rather than a later optimization.</details> |
| 2026-09-01 | A follow-up carries THE WHOLE THREAD forward, bounded by the existing session-memory cap, and strictly as retrieval guidance rather than as citable evidence | Carrying only the prior question and answer; carrying only the resolved entities from the previous turn | <details><summary>why</summary>Product-owner decision, choosing the option that most moves this from a search box to an assistant. Both narrower options were rejected for the same reason: they answer "what did the last turn mention" when a real conversation refers back further than one turn, and a user who says "what about the second one" is pointing at something two turns old. TWO REFINEMENTS ACCEPTED WITH IT, both offered by the assistant and neither changing the decision. First, "whole thread" means the whole thread UP TO THE EXISTING BOUND: build phase 4.5 already ships bounded session memory with a tokenizer-counted cap and per-session persistence, so the thread is carried until that cap is reached and then the OLDEST turns are dropped, which keeps `prompt-cache-discipline.md`'s rule that carried context enters the dynamic suffix and never the stable prefix. An unbounded thread would grow the prompt without limit and eventually crowd out the system instructions. Second, and this is the load-bearing constraint: the thread guides WHAT GETS LOOKED UP and never WHAT GETS ASSERTED. A claim from an earlier answer must never become a citable fact in a later one, because it was grounded against that turn's retrieval and not this one's, and laundering it forward would let a citation drift from the record that supports it. The product already tells users exactly this, in the follow-up help text: "Earlier context guides what gets looked up, never what gets asserted, so every claim is cited from scratch." That sentence stays true.</details> |
| 2026-09-01 | When the grounding pass strips a clause from the middle of a sentence, the WHOLE SENTENCE is dropped rather than the remainder being shown | Keeping the surviving fragment, which is the current behaviour; repairing the remainder into grammatical prose | <details><summary>why</summary>Product-owner decision, agreeing with the assistant's recommendation. The current behaviour was observed live: "BRCA1 (gene symbol: BRCA1 [1]." with an unbalanced parenthetical and no verb, because the stripped clause carried the sentence's verb and its closing bracket. The cost of dropping the sentence is real and was weighed: one unsupported disease out of four takes the other three with it, turning a partial answer into a smaller one. It is accepted because a fragment is not merely ugly, it is a sentence whose meaning nobody verified, and this system's whole position is that what survives grounding is what a reader can trust. Repairing the remainder was never on the table: it means generating prose AFTER the grounding pass has run, which is the one thing that would let unverified text reach a reader. The decision matters more since journey 2 measured the wait at over 25 seconds, because a fragmented answer at the end of a long wait is worse than a shorter complete one.</details> |
| 2026-09-01 | An answer MAY offer a next step, and the offer is derived from what retrieval actually returned and had to leave out, never generated as free text | Generating the suggestion with the Synth tier; shipping no offer at all and leaving the answer to end on a full stop | <details><summary>why</summary>Product-owner decision. Generating it freely was rejected on the reasoning already recorded in `UI_feedback.md`: an offer to go deeper is a claim that there IS something deeper, so a model-invented follow-up about data the graph does not hold is a confident wrong answer wearing a question mark, and it would defeat cite-or-refuse through a surface nothing checks. Deriving it from the omitted findings and truncation disclosures the system ALREADY COMPUTES is harder and is the honest version, and those values exist today: build phase 6.2's T-6.2-03 rewrote the omission disclosure and the count behind it is `len(omitted)`. AN ANSWER MUST BE ABLE TO DECLINE, and this is not a detail: a refusal, an empty retrieval or a single-fact lookup has no honest next step, and a system that always asks something will pad. ORDERING: this sits BEHIND the whole-thread decision above and depends on it, because an offer the system makes and then forgets making is worse than no offer, so accepting an offer must continue the thread rather than start a fresh run.</details> |
| 2026-09-01 | The assistant does NOT drive the browser unless explicitly asked. It fixes the frontend and backend, then writes the manual test workflow into `UI_feedback.md` for the product owner to run | The assistant running the eight journeys itself and reporting what it saw, which is what it had been doing | <details><summary>why</summary>Product-owner decision, and a working-rule change rather than a one-off. The goal is stated plainly: the assistant's job is to fix the UI and the flow across frontend and backend, and then hand over a workflow the product owner runs themselves. THE REASONING IS ABOUT WHOSE JUDGEMENT IS BEING EXERCISED. A journey the assistant runs produces a filmstrip the assistant then interprets, and every interpretation this session produced needed correcting at least once: two of one capture's five fields named testids that do not exist and reported plausible constants, which nearly became a confident wrong conclusion about the agent. A person looking at the running product forms a judgement no capture can substitute for, and it is the product owner's judgement that decides whether this is usable. It also spends real guest allowance and real model budget on every run, which is the product owner's to spend. WHAT DOES NOT CHANGE: the eight journeys stay built and gated behind `RUN_LIVE_JOURNEYS=1`, so they are available the moment they are asked for, and the three already run keep their committed evidence.</details> |

The product owner testing on develop is the verification step, so a build phase merges with its tickets at `in-review` and they reach `done` on the product owner's verdict | Running the judge round before merging build phase 6.2, which is what `docs/build/Build_workflow_cadence.md` stage 8 and `bossman-mode` both require, and what every phase from 1.0 to 6.0 did | <details><summary>why</summary>Product-owner decision, taken by asking a question the assistant could not answer well: "what are you going to judge, I think judging is my domain now". WHAT DECIDED IT ANYWAY: the product owner's loop is faster and its evidence is better. A defect a person hits on develop is worth more than one a reviewer hypothesizes, and `develop` is now a test environment rather than the product, so a defect there costs a test cycle rather than a user. The judge round's traditional job here was to gate a merge when `develop` WAS the product, and that premise no longer holds. ONE CONTROL IS KEPT and it is not a gate on the product owner: the maker still does not sign off their own work, so tickets merge `in-review` and only the product owner's verdict moves them to `done`. WHAT WOULD REVERSE IT: a phase whose defects are not visible from the running product, since the whole argument rests on the product owner's testing being able to see what a review would have found.</details> |

| 2026-09-20 | Fix CI redness by repairing test isolation, never by relaxing a gate or the CI environment | Adding `POSTGRES_HOST_AUTH_METHOD=trust` to the CI postgres service; pointing the nine hardcoded test URLs at a credentialed one; skipping or xfailing the four failing tests | <details><summary>why</summary>The three causes were a stale debugging-guide manifest, a process-wide SQLAlchemy engine poisoned by an earlier test's `monkeypatch`, and an MCP session manager entered twice in one process. Every one is a real defect that a CI-environment change would have hidden. The engine leak is the sharpest case: `data/base.py` caches `_engine` from whatever `USER_DB_URL` said at first use, so a test that points it at a credential-less URL leaves every later test in that worker talking to whatever database that URL resolves to. On a developer's machine it resolves happily under local trust authentication, which is exactly why it was invisible locally for five days of red CI. Making CI tolerate the credential-less URL would have made the gate green while leaving a live path by which application code can be pointed at the wrong database. The fix is an autouse guard in `tests/conftest.py` holding one invariant at setup and teardown: the engine either does not exist or matches the ambient `USER_DB_URL`. Order robustness was demonstrated in both directions rather than assumed. Full evidence and rejected alternatives: `testing/Developer/reports/2026-09-19_ci_green/python_gates.md`.</details> |

That guarantee was the whole argument for the landed fix, which widened the test's wait instead of adding a flush trigger. Two explanations fit and this session could not separate them: a real stall on a stream that closes with no `done`, which a person with a dropped connection would hit, or event-loop starvation, since the same suite failed 87 tests under parallel execution at load average 48 to 60 and passed only serially in 1123 seconds. Patching again would have been a fix inside a fix, which is this repository's own stop condition, and would have shipped a change whose verification cannot be told apart from a flake to the branch testers use. 11.27, the bold change, is almost certainly innocent and was reverted only because it shares the merge; splitting it out is a fair next step and was deliberately not done unattended. Nothing is lost: branch `worktree-agent-a8393711bb57d579b` holds every commit.</details> |

First, `requirements/Technical_specification.md` is locked and `.claude/rules/v1-scope-boundary.md` forbids editing it without explicit product-owner sign-off; that sign-off is this row. Second, the host-pinned pattern is a real security control, not a formality: it exists so a citation URL cannot be spoofed to point anywhere, and the production-standards rule requires a host-pinned regex rather than a bare `https://` check. So `omim.org` is added as an EXACT additional host, never as a loosened pattern that would admit arbitrary domains.</details> |

Changing the product to satisfy a documentation linter, and loosening the checker, were both rejected as the inversion this repository already forbids: never corrupt a correct subject, or weaken a verify surface, to turn a gate green. Plain language diverges by register, sentence granularity and length only | A code-owned glossary of plain definitions rendered as its own labelled block; model-written explanatory sentences rendered as visually labelled unsourced background | <details><summary>why</summary>The product owner's words, given directly while the build was being scoped: "Everything has to have a source. A labelled-background slot is that same exemption with a nicer label on it, and a reader who has been told a sentence is unsourced has still been told the sentence. The glossary option was genuinely safe, since a checked-in definition is code-built like the record tables and never touches the grounding gate, and it was declined anyway: it answers a question the product did not ask, adds a surface nobody maintains, and the decision above makes it unnecessary. What makes the remaining path viable rather than merely strict is that the material changed on 2026-09-20: since `9cf8572` a whole retrieved abstract is citeable as one finding, so an explanatory sentence CAN be grounded by restating an abstract in simpler words, drawing its vocabulary from the abstract itself. Cite-or-refuse is not relaxed, widened, or special-cased by this item.</details> |
| 2026-09-21 | The plain-language depth directive's upper bound is a SHAPE, paragraphs and sentences per paragraph, rather than a word count. This closes the second of the two questions the 11.31 row reserved for them. A shape bound was chosen over a bigger number because all three recorded failures of this directive failed the same way, by trying to buy a property with an instruction about FORM, and a word count is the form instruction most likely to be gamed: `tests/system_03_search_agent/synthesis/test_answer_quality.py` records a 700-word ask returning 300 to 740 words of which only 160 to 230 survived the gate, so the number never described the output. A paragraph count constrains neither which tokens may appear nor which findings are covered, which is the Section 14.1 firewall a depth directive may not cross. Removing every instruction was rejected because unguided length varies run to run, which makes the divergence between the two modes inconsistent and therefore untestable. ONE PREMISE WAS FOUND STALE WHILE DECIDING THIS AND IS RECORDED RATHER THAN QUIETLY DROPPED: the 11.31 row justifies keeping a cap by citing F9-04, where 3 of 25 researcher runs died at the 45-second budget on a 700-word ask, but `harness/harness.py` attributes those same deaths to synth reasoning effort `low` burning the whole 4000-token ceiling on reasoning, and records that at effort `none`, which is what ships today, the same prompt finished 6 of 6 in 5.2 to 7.2 seconds. Two fixes landed the same day for one symptom and each claims the cause; the effort claim carries a control experiment and the ask claim does not, so the word cap is no longer known to be load-bearing.</details> |
| 2026-09-21 | SUPERSEDES the shape-bound row above: the plain-language directive carries NO length instruction at all, not a word target and not a paragraph shape | Keeping the paragraph shape decided earlier the same day; returning to a word count at a higher number | <details><summary>why</summary>The shape bound was decided in the morning and its first live output disproved it by lunchtime, so the earlier row is left standing as the historical record rather than edited. Version 4 of the directive asked for "a sentence per finding, grouped into paragraphs of three to five", and got exactly that: 206 words of "One disease is called X. Another disease is called Y.", which is longer than the 89 it replaced and explains nothing. The product owner's verdict closed it: "It is not the words that matter but the content and how easy is it to explain and understand", and "Number of words do not define an answer". The deeper reason a shape bound failed is the same one that killed the three directive versions before it, recorded in `_DEPTH_DIRECTIVES`' own comments: it bought a property with an instruction about FORM. A paragraph count is a form instruction wearing a different hat. The right length is audience fit, which the product owner named as the fuzzy part deliberately: plain language assumes no biology and no NCBI, carries everything the findings show and synthesises it; researcher assumes both and gets the specifics and the tables. What bounds the output is what bounds every other model call here, the 4000-token ceiling and the 45-second step budget, both in code where a model cannot negotiate with them.</details> |
| 2026-09-21 | Give plain language something plainer to quote, by retrieving NCBI's own gene summary, rather than relaxing the grounding gate | Quoting the abstracts already retrieved and changing nothing else; relaxing `ground_claim`'s contiguity requirement with a new control; accepting that plain language cannot explain and diverging only by sentence granularity | <details><summary>why</summary>Product-owner decision, taken after being shown the measurement. `ground_claim` accepts a claim against a long free-text finding only as a verbatim contiguous excerpt, so against abstract-length source text the gate permits QUOTING and forbids EXPLAINING: a faithful paraphrase using only the source's own words, merely reordered, is stripped. An offline probe of 19 sentences across 8 shapes returned 3 survivors, all literal excerpts. Relaxing contiguity was measured and rejected on evidence rather than on principle: with it dropped, three of four reorderings of an abstract's own words pass the content-token allowlist with the meaning wrong, because that allowlist defends against INVENTED words and never defended against REARRANGED ones. So the input changed rather than the gate. NCBI publishes a plain-English `summary` for every gene, the field allowlist omitted it, and it passes the gate verbatim. The known limit, reported rather than hidden: the summary is now retrieved, cited and rendered, and the model still does not use it to explain, so the ambition is only half delivered.</details> |
| 2026-09-21 | Remove both notes in the answer's Notes section outright, in the web UI rather than in the backend | Folding the further-records count into the records heading so the fact survives; keeping one note reworded in plain words; suppressing both at the source in `core/graph.py` | <details><summary>why</summary>The product owner's words: "the Notes section is super confusing. remove it", naming the unverified-summary note and the further-records note, and choosing outright deletion when offered the two options that kept the count. They were told before deciding that the further-records note is load-bearing, since without it a reader sees five records and cannot tell ten were found, which is the partial-answer-looking-complete shape their own D-2 decision of 2026-09-20 had guarded against. Hiding in the web UI rather than suppressing at the source was an engineering judgement, not a softening of their decision: both notes are built in the same branch that floors `trust_outcome` at `ask`, and removing them at the source was tried and turned five tests red that guard F-4.5-06 breach 2, an answer reporting a subset of its findings while looking complete. That attempt was reverted. The trust floor is untouched, so an incomplete answer still reaches the screen reading "not yet confirmed", and the API, CLI and MCP surfaces still carry the sentences for a programmatic caller.</details> |
| 2026-09-21 | Accept that Layer 1 graph sources render under the Live NCBI heading rather than their own, because the source list deduplicates by URL | Keying the dedup on layer as well as URL so a Knowledge graph group appears; showing the graph rows separately and accepting duplicate rows | <details><summary>why</summary>The product owner asked why the Layer 1 knowledge-graph sources were not visible, was shown the measurement, and answered "All good if deduped. The consequence was stated to them in those terms before they accepted it, including that graph facts are therefore LABELLED as live-API sources, which is a provenance nuance rather than a missing source.</details> |

For 2a: `tail_is_listing` is unconditionally true, so the findings tail already grounds every admitted finding and merges those claims, and the residual gap was entirely item 11.34. Measured before building, five admitted findings gave three citations and both misses carried multi-sentence values; after 11.34 the same measurement gives five of five with zero stripped. Building the obvious per-row version would have REINTRODUCED a recorded defect, since build phase 2.1 cited every row and shipped twenty-five chips over an answer to a different question, which is why the claim-scoped design replaced it. For OMIM: the host rule was widened and the dispatch was enabled and then REVERTED the same session, because `breadth_plan.filter_omim_titles` exists and nothing calls it, dead code for as long as OMIM has been dropped. Without it the first OMIM hit for `GCK` is `MAP4K2`, so an unfiltered result cites a different gene than the question asked about, fully and correctly cited, which is the confident wrong answer this product exists to avoid. What finishing it needs is written into `_BREADTH_FOLLOW_UPS`'s own comment.</details> |
| 2026-09-22 | The 10.3 consistency run is measured as three PASSES over all 50 golden questions, signed in on two fresh test accounts, two worker threads partitioned by question, the depth omitted so it resolves to the account default, a fresh session per run, and every record appended the moment its run ends. | Three back-to-back tries per question, as the 2026-09-12 baseline did. One account for all 150 runs. Anonymous guest sessions. Three or more concurrent workers. Recording only the summary at the end. | <details><summary>why</summary>Each choice is a measured constraint rather than a preference. Two accounts because `PER_USER_DAILY_QUERY_CAP` is 100 on develop, which is what turned 65 of the baseline's 150 runs into refusals from one account, and the anonymous path is tighter still at a tenth of `ANON_DAILY_RUN_CAP` per source per day. Concurrency two because the E-utilities pool is 10 requests per second with a fail-fast queue of 15 and one run makes about six `ncbi_efetch` calls, so two runs worst case is 12 queued and three is 18, and `.claude/rules/tool-call-budgets.md` forbids an integration run that trips the limit it is measuring; the client counts any rate-pool signal so contamination is visible rather than assumed absent. Passes rather than back-to-back tries because three tries in one minute measure a minute while three passes measure the hour, and L-01 is a variance question. Write-first because the 2026-08-27 lesson is that a process ends without warning and a record that exists only in memory was never taken.</details> |

Keeping 500 would disclose the cut honestly but still discard most of a 1253-character gene summary, and the product owner's 2026-09-20 verdict is that answers read as surface level, with the grounding gate permitting only quotation, so the quotable source text is the answer's ceiling. 4000 would exceed what `SynthFinding` accepts and be cut again silently one hop later. Repairing the two tiers keeps a distinction that never had a threat model behind it, only F-2.0-14 parity. Exempting named fields would make the control depend on a list that a new tool forgets to update. A new category rather than `off_topic` follows the repository's own precedent, F-3.0-01, where the product owner added `write_seeking` so a caller switching on `category` can tell refusals apart and the web banner, whose copy is chosen by category alone and never reads `reason`, can say the right thing; the cost is five enumeration sites and it is paid once. Deterministic rather than classifier-based because the Guard-tier classifier judges injection only, and because the trigger set is small and literal. Two factors, not one, because the module's own history (ADV-04) shows one-factor rules refusing ordinary questions, and `align`, `alignment` and bare `sequence` are deliberately not triggers so a question about sequence alignment methods or a reference sequence accession still admits.</details> |
| 2026-09-22 | An errored `cypher_query` outcome's `tool_result` summary carries the tool's own error text after the row-count prefix, `"0 row(s) of 0: <reason>"`, built in `core/graph.py`'s `_execute_planned_call`; the act budgets, Think's entity extraction and the answer text are deliberately unchanged. | Replace the summary with the reason and drop the prefix. Appending rather than replacing keeps the shape a reader has learned, and nothing parses the prefix. A separate `error` event would change what the web UI does with a tool failure and is the disclosure decision the product owner is being asked to take with the cause now in hand, not a side effect of reading it. A log line alone would help a developer with the container's log and nobody else. The reason is bounded by the tool's own 500-character cap, so the event's 1000-character summary bound holds without a second truncation. Reading the causes locally with this change in place found two: no entity resolved so no lookup attempted, and the second graph call running past the act step's per-query-class budget.</details> |

The choice stands as made: develop is the surface the fix loop hammers with hundreds of runs a day, and tracing it would spend the observability budget on throwaway measurements while production, the deployment a real reader uses, is the one whose runs need a join key. The method consequence is recorded so it is not rediscovered: L-01's causes were read the same day by running the API locally on the changed code, which produced the tool's own error text on the stream, and that path is available for any develop measurement.</details> |

The parked build phase 5.2 grader treats `ask` as a clarifying-question abstain, in `RunRecord.is_non_answer` and `rubric_grader._dataset_says_a_source_exists`, while the trust gate's `ask` means "grounded, not yet confirmed" and is the modal outcome of a well-sourced answer (51 of 86 answered runs). | 2026-09-22 | In `select_template`, an `exploratory` question that names an entity but matches no shape takes the record template, the same behaviour a `lookup` question already had; a `single_hop`, `multi_hop` or `aggregate` question with no shape keeps the model path. | Drop the class gate entirely, as the slow-search diagnosis proposed. The two golden questions of the exploratory no-shape kind, G-033 and G-039, lost their own graph search on every measured pass because the generated query's plan mis-estimates an id match by four orders of magnitude and the graph's 30-second statement timeout kills it after about 85 seconds; the record template returns the row those runs never got in under a second, so the plain-terms BRCA1 explanation goes from about 100 seconds and a thinner answer to seconds and a fuller one. The gate was NOT dropped wholesale because the 2026-09-22 consistency run shows the model path earning its keep on the other classes: a multi_hop question's generated search finds forty disease rows a record would lose (G-011), an aggregate question computes a count a record cannot (G-034), and a single_hop question about a name that resolved loosely would turn a correct refusal into an answer (G-014). A retry after the timeout would still make the reader wait thirty seconds for the same single row, and raising either budget makes the answer slower on the way to the same row. Evidence: `testing/Developer/reports/2026-09-22_slow_second_search/findings.md`.</details> |
